728. JOHN FOGGERTY, JAMES CRAIN , and WILLIAM CARRALL , were indicted for stealing, on the 27th of Jan., 288Ibs. weight of lead, value 2 l., the goods of Benjamin Bond Cabbell, Esq., and fixed to a building, &c.—2nd COUNT, not stating it to be fixed; and that Foggerty and Crain had been before convicted of felony.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM ROSS ( police-constable D 157.) A few minutes before nine o'clock, on Friday evening, the 27th of Jan., I was in plain clothes, in the New Road, near Lower Lisson-street—the prisoners were all three standing together, about a dozen yards up Lower Lisson-street—they were talking together—I went up Lower Lisson-street to meet them—they separated about a yard apart, and came to meet me—I went on, and passed Crain, who came first—I then passed Carrall, with this piece of lead on his left shoulder—it was not a dozen yards from Mr. Cabbell's house, which is No. 14—I turned sharp round, and followed Carrall—Foggerty was behind me—Crain got first to the New Road, looked right and left, and then beckoned Carrall to come on—Crain went towards Homer-street, to the left, and Carrall went straight across—Foggerty said to me, "Master, what is it o'clock?"—I crossed, took hold of Carrall, and said, "What are you going to do with that lead?"—he said, "My mate behind just picked it up, and I am going to take it to the station-house"—I took hold of him, he dropped the lead, I sprung my rattle, and the others ran away—Crain towards Homer-street—I did not see where Foggerty went—I took Carrall to the station—shortly after another officer brought in
Crain—Carralle then said that Crain had picked it up—" No," said Crain, "I did not; it was the other picked it up, who is not here"—in halt an hour afterwards Foggerty was bronght in—I am positive he was with the others, and said, "Master, what is it o'clock?"—I went back about half an hour after, and saw some mortar disturbed at No. 14, Lower Lisson-street, on the wall, by the side of the door—that attracted my attention to examine further—I waited till the constable came up—I knocked at the door, and a female answered me—I ultimately went on the portico, and discovered a lot of lead had been removed—this piece of lead, which I took from Carrall, was folded in the same form as it is now, but I unfolded it to match it with the lead—there were three pieces of lead remaining on the portico—they had been folded, but have been unfolded since—I unfolded the three pieces and the one I took from Carrall, and applied them to the portico, and the whole four pieces formed the whole of the cover of the portico—there was no doubt about it, and it had been recently cut—I live in a house nearly opposite No. 14, and saw this lead was perfectly safe at four o'clock that afternoon—I put my head out when my bell rang, and saw the lead.
MATTHEW RIERDON ( police-constable D 135.) About nine o'clock that night I heard the rattle sprung—I went in the direction, and saw Crain running along the New Road—he turned into Homer-street—I cried, "Stop thief," and sprung my rattle—I was in plain clothes—I saw him stopped in John-street by a policeman in uniform—I had not lost sight of him—as soon as I took him he said, "I have done nothing; I don't know what it is"—I said, "You must come back with me; I don't know what it it, but perhaps we shall find out"—he tried to get his hand into his jacket pocket—I said, "Keep your hand out"—I put my hand into hit pocket, and found this chisel, which appears to have been very sharp, but the edge is turned by using it—it would have taken a very considerable time for one man to have cut these four pieces of lead from the portico, and to roll them up—at the station Carrall pointed to Crain, and said, "He picked it up, and gave it to me"—Crain answered, "It was not me, it was the one not here."
HILL BECK ( police-constable D 127.) I took Foggerty about nine o'clock at night, on the 47th of Jan., at his mother's room in Holles-street—I said I wanted him about some lead—he said, "Very well, I will go with you"—when we got to John-street, I asked him if he knew Carrall and Crain—he said "Yes, I was with them this afternoon"—he then said, "This is a rum job, is it not?"—I took him to the station—I found in his mother's room such a jacket as he used to wear—I went next morning to the portico, and saw the lead had been removed—there is 288lbs. of it.
BENJAMIN BOND CABBELL, ESQ . I live in Brick-court, Temple. The house, No. 14, Lower Lisson-street, is my property.
Carrall's Defence. There was a gentleman and five or six people standing together; this lead laid against the lamp, against the old chapel; they told me to take it to the station, and I might get 1 s. to get a drop of beer; I turned the right way to the station, and met this officer; I told him how I got it.
WILLIAM ROSS re-examined. He never told me so.
HENRY SAUNDERS ( police-sergeant D 26.) I produce a certificate of the conviction of Crain, by the name of James Geer, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—Crain is the person I then saw tried and convicted.
THOMAS HARRISON . I was a sergeant of police. I produce a certificate of Foggerty's former conviction, which I got from the Clerk of the Peace at Clerkenwell—( read)—he is the person who was then tried by the name of John Winter, which is his right name.
FOGGERTY — GUILTY . Aged 18.
CRAIN — GUILTY . Aged 19.
Transported for seven Years.
CARRALL — GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18430130-729]
729. FREDERICK WILLIAM ROBERTSON was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of January, I handkerchief, value 1 s.; 1 knife, 6 d.; and 1 purse, 6 d.; the goods of William Bryant: and 5 watches, value 7 l. 15 s.; 1 eye-glass, 5 s.; 1 coat, 1 l. 10 s.; 1 waistcoat, 7 s.; and 1 handkerchief, 2 s. 6 d.; the goods of Sarah Avila, in her dwelling-house.
MR. WILKINS conducted the Prosecution.
JOSIAH BENJAMIN AVILA . I live with my mother, Sarah Avila, a widow, in Mile End-road, where she carries on the business of a pawnbroker—the prisoner lived as shopman in her warehouse for about two years, and has left for three years. On Sunday, the 8th of Jan., Sarah Gale, our servant, gave me information, when I returned from chapel, about one o'clock—I went to the station for a police-constable, and went with him over our premises—we have four warehousemen—in the second warehouse I found a ticket for a coat, and a ticket for a pair of hose on the window-seat—they related to articles which had been pawned at our shop—several of the bundles had been disturbed, and I missed a silk handkerchief from one of them—I went into the shop, and missed three watches from the plate-board; and ultimately I missed eight more watches from the drawer under it—this shop is connected with my mother's dwelling-house, and is in the parish of St. Dunstan, Stebonheath, or Stepney—altogether I missed eleven watches, which were worth abont 22 l.—the bolts of the door which opens from the shop into the street, had been drawn, and it was only on the latch—the garden at the back of our house is next door but one to the Vintners' Alms-houses—there is a wall between our garden and the Vintners'—a person could easily get over that wall and into our garden—the back door of our house is usually unfastened after day-light, and was unfastened that day—when we receive property in pledge, it is entered in a book, which is in my writing—I have referred to it, and the property which is here produced, exactly corresponds with it—this knife, purse, and handkerchief belonged to William Bryant, who is in our employ, and the other things were in pledge at my mother's—I know these four watches—I have compared them with the description we have of them—they were in our house on Sunday, the 8th of Jan.—on the Saturday following I went to Mr. Hall, a pawnbroker, and saw the prisoner there—he had on a coat, a waistcoat and a handkerchief, which I believe to be those lost from our ware-house—they were just like them—I have no doubt they were them, but I could not swear to them.
WILLIAM RALPH YOUNG ( police-sergeant K 9.) On Sunday, the 8th of Jan., I went to the prosecutor's premises, and on the Monday went over the premises with the shopman—I had some clothes which had been left behind by the person, and the shoes—I took the shoes into the garden, and one foot mark that was there corresponded with them, but others had been so trodden on that I could not trace them—these shoes have been tried on the prisoner's feet, and fit him exactly, and so do these clothes, except the trowsers, which are about an inch too long, which it may be seen was the case with the person who wore them, as they are all mud at the bottom.
ABRAHAM POUND . I live with Mr. Arnold, a pawnbroker, in Shoreditch. I have a watch which was pawned by the prisoner on the 9th of Jan., for 17 s., in the name of Thompson or Johnson—I am quite sure it was him—I was present when he pawned it.
GUILTY .† Aged 18.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18430130-730]
730. THOMAS SHAW was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of Jan., 2 printed books and case, value 7 s., the goods of John Diprose.
THOMAS HOWELL . I am shopman to John Diprose, who has a book-stand
in Covent-garden. On the evening of the 24th of Jan., about a quarter before eleven o'clock, I was getting the books to rights, I heard a footstep behind me—I turned, and saw the prisoner in the act of taking this case of books from the stand, and putting them under his coat—I told him to put them down—he did not—I followed him round to the back of the stand, and asked him for them—he said he had not got them—I found them about two yards from where he was standing, and five sr six yards from where they had been taken.

731. WILLIAM SPRINGATE and WILLIAM THOMAS were indicted for stealing, on the 21st of Jan., 2 handkerchief, value 7 s. 9 d., the goods of Thomas Carter.
MARIA CARTER . I am the wife of Thomas Carter, haberdasher, Cumberland-market. On the 21st of Jan., Springate came to the shop to inquire the way to Camden or Kentish-town—after he was gone I missed two silk handkerchiefs which had hung on a line in the shop—I had seen them safe about a quarter of an hour before—I went out, and saw the two prisoners five or six yards from the gate—I put my hand on Springate's shoulder, and said, "You have got my handkerchiefs"—he said to Thomas, "Give them to her"—Thomas took them from his waistcoat and threw them down—these are them.
Thomas. A boy came and asked us the way to Camden-town, and said he would give us a handkerchief a-piece, and he did.
(Springfield received a good character.)
SPRINGFIELD — GUILTY [Recommended to mercy. See original trial image.] . Aged 17.
THOMAS — GUILTY. Aged 17. Recommended to mercy.

732. ROSANNA ROGERS was indicted for, stealing, on the 22nd of Jan., 2 pairs of shoes, value 9 s.; 1 bonnet, 3 s.; 4 sovereigns, 8 half-crowns, 10 shillings, 8 sixpences, 12 pence, and 12 halfpence; the property of John Nelson, her master.
URSULA NELSON . I am the wife of John Nelson; we live in Bower-street, Commercial-road. The prisoner had been in my service for ten days—on the 22nd of Jan., I was at chapel from six o'clock till eight in the evening—I left her at home—when I came home I found the key of the street-door outside, and the prisoner gone—I missed the articles and money stated in this indictment, from a cupboard in my bed-room, which had been unlocked and locked again—the prisoner surrendered herself to an officer, on the Wednesday week—I had taken her from a refuge belonging to the Society of Friends.
WILLIAM DEVONSHIRE ( City police-constable, No. 237.) On the 1st of Feb., the prisoner called to me, and said she wanted to give herself up, as she had robbed her mistress of five sovereigns, in Bower-street, Commercial-road—I took her to the station, and then went to her master.
GUILTY . * Aged 21.— Confined One Year.

733. MARY MURRAY was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of Jan., 1 bottle, value 3 d.; 1 quart of wine, 3 .; and 3/4 of a pint of gin, 1 s.; the goods of Richard John Moscrop, her master.
ELIZABETH MOSCROP . I am the wife of Richard John Moscrop—he keeps the Green Man, Covent-garden—the prisoner was our servant—about two o'clock, on the 25th of Jan., I found her drunk in the coal-cellar—she came afterwards into the tap-room—I perceived something at the back of her gown, and found a bottle of port-wine there—I found the gin in the place where
she ought to wash dishes—that might not be mine, but I can swear to the bottle of wine—it had been in the cellar, and she could get access to it by abstracting the key from its place, but she had no right there.
Prisoner. I do not know how the bottle came there.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Three Months.

Prisoner. The boots were on the pavement. Witness. No; he untwisted them, and took them from the shutter pole, and put them under his frock.

735. THOMAS BURTON was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of Jan., 3 1/2 lbs. weight of brass, value 1 s. 6 d.; and 6lbs. weight of lead, 1 s.; the goods of Thomas Peachy, in a certain vessel in a port of entry and discharge.
THOMAS PEACHY . I am owner of the Industry, and all her stores. She was lying in the dock at Poplar—I went on board with Montague, the officer, on the morning of the 23rd of Jan.—he produced this brass-work, which I am able to swear was mine, and belonged to the Industry —it is the blower of the stove—I fitted this brass to the stove, and it corresponds exactly.
Prisoner. Q. You said you could not swear to it at the station? A. I said I had no private mark on it, but by the fitting I am able to swear to it, and the blower was gone.
JOSEPH MONTAGUE . I am a constable. I found the brass round the front of the prisoner, and a piece of this lead under each arm, just outside the dockgate at Poplar—he appeared to me to come direct from the gate—I saw the next day that the vessel had been broken into—the prisoner's companion ran away—he dropped one piece of it.
Prisoners Defence. I had no companion—it would be impossible for me to come out with these, things.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18430130-736]
736. JOHN M'BRIDE was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of Jan., 1 copper, value 1 l. 5 s.; and 43lbs. weight of lead, 5 s., the goods of Joseph Cundey, and fixed to a building; and THOMAS FRAME , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.; and that M'Bride had been before convicted of felony.
MR. WILKINS conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN SMITH . I am a bricklayer. I was at work at Mr. Joseph Cundey's house at No. 5, Ranelagh-grove—it is unoccupied—On Thursday afternoon, the 5th of Jan., I went into the wash-house and shut the door—I went up to the copper, and saw that it was safely fixed in the brick-work, and had lead on the top—I left at five o'clock, and went again next morning at seven—the copper was then gone, and the lead also, all but a small piece.
JAMES HURLEY . I had known M'Bride about a week before this happened—I saw him and Greeney, Myers and Lee, going down Ranelagh-Grove, Pimlico, at half-past five o'clock on Monday, the 30th of Dec.—they went
to No. 5, Ranelagh-grove. I did not know the other boys, only Greeney, who lived opposite to me—they were attempting to open the house, No. 5, Ranelagh-grove—M'Bride had something like a chisel and a stone—it sounded like metal as he was hammering it with the stone—I am certain he was the person I saw hammering in that way at the street-door—I went off directly—on the 4th of Jan. I was coming from chapel with my brother down Queen-street, about half-past eight at night—I saw M'Bride, Greeney, and Myers (three of the same boys I had seen before) Greeney and M'Bride bad a large copper—I know that when I first saw M'Bride and the others hammering at the door it was Monday the 30th of Dec., because the 29th was my birth-day—I am sure my birth-day was on a Sunday, and it was on the day after my birth-day I saw these boys, and I saw them with the copper on Thursday night—I gave notice of what I had seen the next day.
Q. Tell us what you saw on the Thursday? A. I and my brother were coming from chapel and saw M'Bride and Greeney go to Frame's shop with the copper—they staid in about five minutes, they then came out, and all three of the boys went in with the lead, which they fetched from a watering-place about twelve yards from the shop—I had noticed that Myers was standing at a place to watch the lead—I did not see what took place in the shop; but when they came out M'Bride had some silver in his hand, and they went away towards Greeney's house—I went home, and the next morning, about half-past nine, I told an officer, and I was called up at half-past two on Saturday morning by M'Culloch, and went to the station.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. When did you give information? A. On the Friday morning, which was the day after I had seen them the second time—I was at work for Mr. Rose, the carrier, when I saw the boys the first time, and I and another of Mr. Rote's boys were going to the Commercial-road with a basket full of dirty linen—it was about a quarter after five o'clock—the second time I had been to chapel in the New-road, Sloane-street—the premises I saw them go into are in Queen-street, Pimlico—my brother is eleven years old—Brennan has lately been a sweep—he is now in the workhouse—I was not with him at the time I was taking this walk to the Commercial-road—I had known him about four years—I was not with him on either of these occasions—I did not see him on either of these occasions—if he was there it was a mere accident—I lived three doors from him—I know the inspector got Brennan into the workhouse, because I was down at Queen-square at the same time—the inspector was going to get me some work—I have had my meals of the inspector ever since I have been with him—he is conducting this prosecution—I have not been living with him—he has given me my meals seven times; four times here, and three times at Queen-square—he has not examined me as to the evidence I was to give—I told the magistrate and the inspector at the station-house, and he put it in the police-force bill—he has not examined me since—I have never been in any scrape—that is as true as the rest of my story—my little sister, who is five years of age, found a horse-cloth since Christmas—she brought it up stairs—I had not stolen it—they did not charge me with stealing it—Mr. Rose said perhaps I might have gone down and taken it out of the cart—that was a mistake—I told my father of it—he brought it down, and it was returned to Mr. Rose, the carrier, who owned it—I will swear he did not threaten to have me and my father prosecuted unless the cloth was returned—he threatened to have my mother taken up, and then my father returned the cloth which he had been keeping—he did not know whose it was—Mr. Rose lives in Avery-row, which is about five minutes' walk from
my father's—I inquired about the cloth of the boys who work for Mr. Rose—my father had it for two months.
MR. WILKINS. Q. There was no foundation for this charge against you? A. No—I never was in any scrape in my life, nor was any threat of a prosecution held out to me—my father is a mason's labourer—the first day I was at Queen-square the inspector gave me some bread and cheese and beer—I was there from half-past eleven o'clock till three—the three days I have been here I have had my dinners of him.
JAMES BRENNAN . I was with Hurley in Queen-street, Ranelagh-grove, on a Thursday in last month—I know M'Bride—I saw him, Greeney, and Myers, with a copper, which they carried to Frame's shop—two of them went in first with it—they came out with some silver and copper in their hands—the third boy was minding the lead at the time, and then the three went into Frame's shop with the lead—I saw Frame in the shop—he put some silver and copper into M'Bride's hand.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Were you with Hurley when this was done? A. Yes, be and I were together—we were going up to the Commercial-road with some linen—I was with him all the while, and did not leave him at all.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What are you doing now? A. Nothing—I have been in St. Margaret's workhouse—the police-officer got me there—I told him on the Friday morning what evidence I had to give—I have seen him since then, and he has examined me, to see that I remembered all about it—he was quite satisfied with the mode in which I gave my answers—I knew Hurley when I was apprenticed—I have known him three or four years—I had not gone with him on this occasion—I was playing with him, and had been for two hours, when I saw this—I have never been in a Court of Justice before—I was taken up for begging, and had six weeks' imprisonment—that was the charge made against me—I did not take anything—I had begged the bread in Mount-street, Grosvenor-square, and a gentleman in plain clothes took me into custody—I am coming out of the workhouse—Mr. Barefoot put me in there till the trial was over—I do not know what is to become of me—Mr. Barefoot has promised to do something for me, if these persons are convicted.
MR. WILKINS. Q. Why did you beg? A. I had nothing else to do, nor anything to eat—I begged a piece of bread, and was sent to prison—I met Hurley in Clifford's-row, and was in his company that afternoon about an hour, when I saw M'Bride and the other boys coming with the copper—Hurley's brother was there.
Q. You told this gentleman just now that the inspector had promised to do something for you, supposing these men were convicted; did you mean that? A. No—he said he would do something for me when the trial was over—my father is a bricklayer—four boys were playing together that day.
ALEXANDER M'CULLOCH ( police-sergeant B 5.) I received information of this robbery in the first instance from Smith, and afterwards from Brennan, when he was brought into the watch-house on Friday, the 6th of Jan.—in consequence of that I went to Frame's shop—he was sitting in the parlour at the back of his shop—I asked him for the copper he bought last night of M'Bride and another—he said he had not got that copper, nor any other, nor any other metal, except three or four pounds of lead, in his possession—I told him I did not believe him—he then said he would tell me the truth of it—he then said the copper was brought there last night, and they left it, and
it remained there about half an hour; on their return to the shop he refused to boy it, and they then took it away towards Chelsea.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. I suppose you are an old officer? A. I have been an officer thirteen years—Barefoot is my inspector—the conversation took place with Brennan between seven and eight o'clock in the evening—when I went to Frame's shop, I had left the station to go home—I did not leave the inspector at the station then—I had seen him that day, but I do not think I bad that night—to the beat of my recollection, I had seen him at six o'clock that evening—I think Brennan came to the station between three and four o'clock on the Friday—the whole of this happened on the Friday—I made a memorandum as soon as I got home—this is it—( producing it)—this is not a book which I keep for the purpose of such memorandums, but I happened to put it here that time, as I had this book in my pocket when I was at Frame's—I live ten or twelve doors from Frame—his shop is three or four minutes' walk from the station—I did not take a note of this conversation by direction of Barefoot, but I put it down as soon as I got home—I never showed it to Barefoot, nor told him that I had made it.
HENRY BAREFOOT . I am an inspector of police. I received information of this robbery on the 5th of Jan.—I do not know the day of the Week, but I am positive it was on the 5th—I made a memorandum of it—I received the information from one of the sergeants at the station—I believe it was from Carter, but I am not certain—I bad not then received information from Hurley—on the 7th, I went with a warrant to search Frame's house—he was in his shop—I asked him for the copper be purchased of a boy-a night or two before—he said he had no such thing in his premises, and he had not bought one—I said I had a search warrant, and I proceeded to search—I went down stairs—he went with me—I came to a cupboard—I requested him to unlock it, which he did—I saw a quantity of wood and shavings in it—I had an officer with me—I requested the officer to move the wood and shavings—Frame then said, "You with find the copper there, "and it was there, beaten up—Frame then said, "Had I known you intended to have had a search warrant, I should have put it out of the way, for when the policeman called last night, I denied having it, and I was a fool for doing so"—he accompanied me into the yard—I looked into a shed, which appeared to have nothing but sacks, and a little rubbish in it—he said, "You will find nothing there"—I was satisfied, and walked back into the shop—I went again in about ten minutes, to the place where he said there was nothing, and found this lead under some sacks and rubbish—this is the copper, it has since been pulled open—this and the lead have been at the station ever since.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. How long have you known Hurley? A. Five or six months; perhaps I might say five or six years—I have been there that time—I do not know that I ever saw him with my eyes before this occasion—I dare say I have seen him running about—I have never spoken to him before this—I think I first spoke to him on the 6th, the day M'Bride was taken—I did not take down what he had to say—I heard what he had to say, and what Brennan had to say—they were both there—I have had no communication with Hurley as to what he had to say—I certainly have not taken down his evidence, or seen his evidence, for any purpose—one of these boys has been taken to the workhouse—all the promise that has been made to Brennan, is to be taken back to the overseers, to be passed from that parish to his own—Mr. Gundey is prosecuting this case at his own expense.
HENRY MORGAN . I am foreman to Mr. Joseph Cundey. I live in West-row, Pimlico—I saw this lead fitted to the place where the copper had been, and from what I saw I have no hesitation in swearing it is Mr. Cundey's lead
—I say that from seeing it fitted to the place—I had seen such a copper as this in that place.
JAMES BRADELY ( police-constable B 134.) I produce a certificate of M'Bride's former conviction, which I got from the office at Westminster—( read)—I was present at the trial—he is the person.
(George Harrison, carpenter, Chelsea; Henry Farrant, carpenter; Joseph Shelton, labourer; William Spaul, carpenter, Pimlico; and Benjamin Bedel, salesman, Royal Hospital-row, gave Frame a good character.)
M'BRIDE — GUILTY . Aged 17.
FRAME — GUILTY . Aged 57.
Transported for Seven Years.
(There was another indictment against the prisoners.)
[trialAccount t18430130-737]
737. ISAAC LEVY was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of Jan., 1 handkerchief, value 1 s.; the goods of Joseph Skinner, from his person.
JOSEPH SKINNER . I live in Whitechapel-road. Between four and five o'clock on the 27th of Jan., I was passing Harrow-alley, Houndsditch, and felt a twitch at my pocket—I turned, and saw the prisoner with my handkerchief, concealing it—he pushed into a crowd—I pursued, and collared him—I called, "Police," but no one came near me—a number of his own people came round, and ill-used me so much, that I was forced to let him go—I gave information, and the following day I heard the officer had got him—I am sure the prisoner is the person—I got my handkerchief as I returned from the crowd.
Prisoner. As I was going up stairs last Saturday night, the officer took me.
GUILTY . ** Aged 14.— Transported for Ten Years.—Convict Ship.
[trialAccount t18430130-738]
738. THOMAS MUNT was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of Jan., 3,500 prints, value 20 l., the goods of William Henry M'Queen and another, his masters.—2 other COUNTS, varying the description of the property.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM HENRY M'QUEEN . I am a copperplate printer. The prisoner had been employed by me for nearly fourteen years—he has always borne the character of an honest man—he had left my service eight or nine months—in working impressions, there are certain copies that are spoiled—the impressions that the prisoner is charged with stealing are of that number—I told the Magistrate I was disinclined to prosecute him—I have since heard that they are generally supposed to be prints which the men suppose they are entitled to—I find such an opinion generally entertained, though, as a master, I never tolerated such a custom.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430130-739]
739. THOMAS MUNT was again indicted for stealing, on the 1st of July, 100 prints, value 8 s., the goods of Joshua Butters Bacon and another, his masters.
JOSHUA BUTTERS BACON . The prisoner was in my employ for eight months—a short time since sergeant Archer called, showed me some print, and asked if I knew them—I said yes, they were my property and my partner's—I picked out a hundred and odd, which were mine—these are them—they are a great many of the damaged prints—I did not authorise the prisoner in any way to take any—I have been in the trade twenty years, and never heard till last Saturday night that there was an understanding in the trade, that the men may take these first impressions.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How do these become your property? A. Two of them are in our possession, and were never given out to
any one—these are them—I heard Mr. M'Queen examined before the Magistrate—there are three here which I can a wear to as mine, and were never given out of my possession to any one—they are worth 3 d., scarcely of any value—there may be seven-eighths of the others spoiled—I believe we employ more men than any in the trade—these are from annuals—when we are printing we throw aside those that are in any way defective—the plate belongs to the trustees of Charles Heath—in the present case I am required to print a certain number—all that are defective we throw aside—those which only require a slight touch, and would have been sent in as good impressions, and paid for, the orders have been completed—each of the partners have a proof, which is an agreement between us and the customers—the prisoner has been in my employ three times—the last time for eight months—the plate has not been in use for some years.
JOHN ARCHER ( police sergeant G 8.) I took the prisoner, and found some of these on him, and some at his house.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430130-740]
740. MARIA DOWNES was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of Jan., 4 sovereigns, 1 half-sovereign, and 2 Bank notes, for the payment of 5 l. each, the monies of Thomas Terry, from his person.
THOMAS TERRY . I live in South-street, Chelsea. On the 2nd of Jan., between ten and eleven o'clock, I met the prisoner—I went with her to her lodging—I had two 5 l. notes, and four half-sovereigns wrapped In the notes, in my left-hand waistcoat pocket—I was with her from twenty minutes to half an hour, and when I got home I missed my money from my left-hand pocket—one of the notes I traced to a pawnbroker's in the neighbourhood—this is one of the notes that I lost on that occasion—I did not pay the prisoner with a note.
Prisoner. Q. Were there not two females in company with you that night? A. No, certainly not—I was quite sensible of what I was about—I was drinking a glass of sherry and water at a public-house at the corner of King's-road—I met with one buss -man, who said he had been in the Navy, and having been in the Navy myself forty-four years, I spoke to him—I was a little sloosed.
ALFRED HINES CLARK . I believe I paid this 5 l. note to the prosecutor—I cannot positively swear that he is the party—there was a cheque paid on that day by Good and Lawrence, payable to Terry, at Spooner and Attwood's.
THOMAS TERRY re-examined. I took the cheque and got this money.
JOHN KILLINGWORTH . The prisoner paid me this note on the 3rd of Jan.—I am sure she is the person.
Prisoner. Q. Was there no other person in your shop? A. I do not think there was at the time—it is a pawnbroker's—the prisoner purchased an article for 9 s.—I paid her 4 l. 11 s. change—I asked her name, she said, "Connor, No. 3, George-street, Chelsea"—I do not remember having seen her before—I put some ear-rings in her ears.
Prisoner's Defence. I was coming down'Cower Sloane-street between twelve and one o'clock in the morning; I kicked a handkerchief on before me; I picked up a piece of paper; I can neither read nor write; I showed it to a friend in the morning; she said it was a 30 s. note; she took it out; I have never seen her or the note since; I never saw the prosecutor; he had two females taken up before me, and would have sworn to them; he said if I would open my shawl and show him the bosom of my gown he would swear to me; I never was inside the pawnbroker's, and dp not know how he came by the note.
THOMAS TERRY re-examined. I did ask her to open her shawl—I could have sworn to her directly, but I wanted to be satisfied in respect to her dress.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18430130-741]
741. JEREMIAH DANIEL HEALEY and CATHERINE HEALEY were indicted for stealing, on the 19th of Dec, 11 orders for the payment of 28 l. 11 s., the property of Peter Green.—4 other COUNTS, calling them warrants, securities, declarations, and pieces of stamped paper.
MR. CHARNOCK conducted the Prosecution.
PETER GREEN . I went to Oporto in 1832, and remained there some years—on the 16th of Dec. last I obtained these orders from the Portuguese Commissioners—on the 16th of Dec. I saw Jeremiah Healey on the subject of selling these warrants—he was recommended to me by a man—I saw him on the day I received them—he was attending the place where the Commissioners gave these warrants—he gave me his address, and said if I disposed of them to any one he would give me as much as any man—on the 19th of Dec. I went to his house with my daughter, between nine and ten o'clock, with these warrants—I was asked up stairs—I sat down, and saw the two prisoners—they asked if I had got the bills—I produced them—I said, "I called on you to ascertain the amount you will give for these bills"—Jeremiah Healey said, 14 s. 6 d. or 15 s. 6 d. in the pound—the gross amount of them is 28 l. 11 s., and there is five per cent, on them till they are paid—I did not agree to sell them—he handed them back to me, and to the best of my belief I put them into my pocket in my coat—I would not swear that I did—I was then coming out, and Jeremiah said, "I hope you will perform your contract to me, to let me have them sooner than any one else"—he put his hand on my shoulder, and we talked some time—I then bade him good night, and next morning I missed them—I went to him that day, and he said he knew nothing of them—I went again on the 21st—he said he knew nothing of them—I heard no more of them till Ben wood called—I went with him to Healey's—Jeremiah said he would not give me 5 s. for the chance of my payment, and I had better write to the Commissioners—in consequence of information I went to Mount-street—I found Jeremiah there—I called him on one side, and said I was given to understand he had these bills, and he had best give them up; I did not want to have any more to do with him—he said, "D—n your eyes, do you think you are going to bounce me out of them?"—Catherine was with him—she said she knew nothing at all about them—I called a policeman, and gave them both into custody—these are the orders.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Had you been trying to sell these titulos? A. No, I have not sold them since this—I did not sell them to Silver—I received altogether by this affair, 41 l. 1 s.—I do not know that their value is only 21 l.—I have heard many persons say they would give 14 s. 6 d. or 15 s. 6 d. in the pound for them—they were issued on the 15th of December, and carry interest at 5 per cent.—there are eleven of them—I understood them to be worth 28 l. 11 s.—I cannot tell who I received 41 l. from—I saw a man on the stairs, who gave me a paper with "20 l." in it, and asked me into a public-house—I believe he was the prisoner's solicitor—my lawyer was at my hand, and Mr. Strutt the lawyer was on the other hand—I believe I received 20 l. from the father of the prisoner—I believed that my titulos had been stolen from me by one of the prisoners—I do not know what I received 20 l. for, but I asked what it was for, he said, "Put it into your pocket"—they got me to write a receipt before I received 1 s. of it—I believe it was Mr. Silver put down 21 l. more—I never saw the titulos from the time I lost them till
last Thursday—I believe I received 41 l. to keep Silver from prosecuting this business, as I understood that my lawyer left it all to him—I had been before a Magistrate, and preferred a charge of felony against both the prisoners—I did not go before a Magistrate afterwards, till last Thursday—I was not taken on a warrant before the Magistrate, I went of my own accord—I had not been at home for three weeks—I understood the officer had been after me—the titulos are payable at six months—it would take five years and a half to have them all paid—according to my belief I put them into my pocket when I was at the prisoner's house, but I will not swear I did it—the value of the whole is 28 l. 11 s.
MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Whatever you did, was it by the advice of those to whom you confided your interest? A. I did—Mr. Barrett advised me, and he took me to the Magistrate.
CATHERINE HEALEY . I am the wife of Dennis Healey, we live in Brownlow-street, Drury-lane. On the 20th of Dec. I saw Catherine Healey in Queen-street, Seven-dials—she was alone—she said, "Come on, I want you"—I said, "What do you want me for?"—she said, "My husband is in a dying state, and I want somebody to buy some bills"—she went with me to Mr. Silver's place, and gave me these bills—she staid outside—I asked if there was a man there who bought bills—I sold them to Mr. Silver for 21 l. 1 s.—I came out, and gave the money to her—she gave me 1 s. and we parted—I gave Mr. Silver these eleven bonds.
JOSEPH SILVER . I am an agent for the purchase of these securities. On the day stated the last witness came to me, and brought these—I gave her 21 l. 1 s.—she asked 14 s. 10 d. in the pound—I sent word I was engaged, I should give no more than 14 s. 9 d.—she sent word I might have them—I said, I was engaged, she must call again—I returned in the evening—she came again, and said, "Here are the titulos, I will take your price"—I paid her 21 l. 1 s.—I saw the male prisoner that evening, I supposed they came from him—I called on him, and said I had bought some titulos, did they come from him?—he said, "No, they did not"—I said, "I thought you were short of cash, and wanted to put yourself in a little money, to prevent selling a quantity that I was buying of you."
Cross-examined. Q. You have got the receipt from Mr. Green for the 21 l.? A. Yes, it is dated 6th of January.
WILLIAM RUSSELL ( police-constable C 13.) I got these titulos at the Police-court from Mr. Silver.
(The prisoners received a good character.)
J. D. HEALEY.— NOT GUILTY .
C. HEALEY — GUILTY . Aged 32.— Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18430130-742]
742. JOHN HARRISON and GEORGE LEWIS were indicted for stealing, on the 15th of Jan., 2 tame rabbits, price 6 s.; the property of James Tillier.
JAMES TILLIER . On the 5th of Jan. I had two rabbits safe in the shed—I missed them the same evening—I went after the prisoners towards Hounslow, and met a policeman—I have seen the two rabbits again—I saw the prisoners going from my mother's cottage on the 5th.

Harrison's Defence. I met a man close by a row of trees; he asked if I knew any body likely to buy two rabbits; he wanted 4 s. I said I bad no money, I would give him a cod-fish and nine fresh herrings, and he took them.
Lewis's Defence. I was along with him for a walk, and when he bought these, I carried them for him.
CHARLES BAKER re-examined. They said they changed a cod-fish and seven fresh herrings for them—I gave 2 s. and a pint of beer for them—they had a rabbit each.

743. HENRY PERCY, ALFRED COLLINS , and FRANCIS WHITTLE were indicted for stealing, on the 21st of Jan., 1 till, value 1 s.; 1 shilling, 1 sixpence, 4 groats, 5 pence, 26 halfpence, and 9 farthings; the property of James Piggott.

MARY ANN PIGGOTT . There were 12 s. in the till, in shillings, sixpence, and pence—I followed my husband, and saw the three prisoners—they had the till in their hands—Whittle held it, and they all had their hands in it.
Collins. The lady said she had taken the money from the till three minutes before. Witness. No, I said I had taken some money from the till.
WILLIAM PALMER . I searched the prisoners, and found some silver and copper on Collins.
ROBERT DUNNETT ( police-constable F 198.) I was on duty, and was called to take the prisoners—I found no money on them.
Callins's Defence. I was full fifty yards off; the gentleman beckoned, and I came to him. The money I had belonged to my father.

747. WILLIAM BEER was indicted for feloniously receiving, of an evil-disposed person, on the 14th of Jan., 30lbs. weight of lead, value 5 s., the goods of Francis Bursell.
FRANCIS BURSELL . I am a plumber and painter, in Cleveland-street, Fitzroy-square. On Saturday, the 24th of Jan., I lost some lead from the roof of the workshop and back parlour—on the following Wednesday, in consequence of information, I went to the prisoner's house, in Warren-street, St. Paneras, in the neighbourhood where I reside—I saw the prisoner—I asked him if he had bought any lead—he said he had—I asked if he could give it to me—he said he could not, as it was melted down and sold—he said he had bought it of George Henry Wallington, whom I took with me.
Cross-examined by Mr. BALLANTINE. Q. You received some communication from Wallington? A. Yes—he told me he had stolen it—I told him, if he would tell the truth with respect to it, I would deal mildly with him—I had lost a tremendous lot of things—the prisoner told me he had bought some lead of that boy, and it was melted and gone—he concealed nothing from me—he behaved with the greatest possible openness—I did not give him into custody—he walked with me to the police-office, he was admitted to bail, and surrendered—I have had a good many communications with Wallington since, and I think he has told a good many untruths.
NOT GUILTY .
(There were two other indictments against the prisoner, on which no evidence was offered.)

On Thursday night, the 19th of Jan., I saw both the prisoners together at the door of No. 23, St. John-street—I think they saw me—they both rushed into the house, and up stairs—the street-door was pushed to—I ran to the door, and heard more than one person running up stairs—I got a light, went up, and found both the prisoners standing together on the top of the second stairs, and behind them was a black bag containing this harness—I said, "Halloo, who does this belong to?"—Swaine said, "It belongs to a person I know"—I said, "Who is he, and where does he live?"—he said, "I tell you it belongs to a person I know, what do you want more?"—I took them and the bag.

750. JOHN YOUNG was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of Jan., 1 a watch, value 10 l.; I watch-key, 3 d.; and I watch-ribbon, 3 d.; the goods of Frederick Henry Ridley, in his dwelling-house: and WILLIAM SCOTT , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.: and that Scott had been before convicted of felony.
LOUISA HOWES . I am servant to Mr. Frederick Henry Ridley, of Broomfield-row, Maida-hill, Paddington. I saw Young about a quarter past twelve o'clock on the morning of the 18th of Jan., in my master's scullery—I did not know him before—he was not dressed as a butcher, but he had a page's jacket on, and a piece of beef in a butcher's tray, which had not been ordered—he left the beef in the scullery—I said, "This is not for here"—he said, "It is"—I asked where he brought it from, and he mentioned a name which I cannot recollect—I left him in the scullery, and went to the dining-room to ask my mistress about it—I came back in about two minutes, and found him where I had left him—I again said, "I don't think it is right"—he said it was, and went out—I followed him, and locked the door—I returned to the kitchen in about three minutes, and missed my mistress's gold watch off the shelf—it had a key attached to it by a ribbon—I know it was safe about ten minutes before—it was gold, and was worth more than 10 l.—I am quite sure it was there—I made inquiries, and my mistress had not ordered the beef—I am quite sure Young is the lad—the watch has not been found.
Young. I did not go out till a quarter to twelve o'clock, and was back by a quarter past; it was not me; you said it was ten minutes after, I was gone you missed it. Witness. No, I said in three minutes after I looked to see what time it was, and the watch was gone—there was no one there but you and I.
GEORGE IBBS . I am going on for twelve years old, and live in Union-street,
Lission-grove—I know both the prisoners well—Scott lives down Clarementplace—I have seen him and Young together nearly every morning—I remember Wednesday, the 18th of Jan.—I was working with my father in Mr. Ridley's garden—I saw Young come there—he had a tray with a bit of beef in it—I am sure it was him—he went down into the kitchen with the beef—he was in the house about two minutes—when he got out of the garden-gate be ran to Scott, who was about ten yards from the gate, and I saw him take something out of his pocket and give it into Scott's hand—Scott took the article, and I saw there was a ribbon, and a key hanging to the bottom of the ribbon—Scott had it at that time in his hand, and he put it into his pocket—Young then ran up the hill, and Scott walked gently after him—I am quite sure the prisoners are the boys—I went to the station for a policeman.
Young. Q. How do you know me? A. You used to come to call Scott almost every morning—that was about a year ago.
Scott. Q. Do you say you saw the watch? A. No—I saw something—I followed Young out of the gate—when he gave it into your hand you put it into your pocket, and he ran, and you walked after him—you were then about thirty yards off—I did not say before that I was in the garden stooping down—I never was in trouble but once, and that was for throwing a stone—I do not know what Young gave you, but I saw a key and a ribbon—I did not say that you stood still for five minutes—you walked on—when I saw him give it you, I stood against the gate, and when the servant came out I told her—I had a good mind to run and tell her directly.
SAMUEL HAMPTON ( police-sergeant D 24.) I received information from lbbs, about one o'clock, on the 18th of Jan., that this watch was lost—I went and found Scott in Church-street, Portman-market—he said he knew nothing about it—I then went after Young—I found him at home, at No. 26, Little Carlisle-street—he said he knew nothing about it, and he did not know where Broomfield-row was—he was dressed in this page's jacket and this hat, and the same trowsers and boots that he now has on—he looked like a servant.
Young's Defence. I went out at a quarter to twelve o'clock, and heard of a place; I went home, and put on my other clothes, and went after it at Dr. Brook's, No. 1, Middlesex-place, New-road—he was engaged; I then went to Chelsea for an accordion, and came back; I had this dress when I was page at Dr. Bruce's, in Montague-street.
Scott's Defence. I was at home, I can assure you, at the time the boy says he saw me.
LAWARENCE ANDERSON . I produce a certificate of Scott's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—I was present—he is the person who was then convicted by the name of Henry Thompson.
(Young received a good character.)
YOUNG — GUILTY. Aged 17.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury, — Confined Six Months.
SCOTT — GUILTY . Aged 18.— Transported for Seven Years.
(There was another indictment against Scott.)

753. GEORGE LESTER was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of Jan., 4 planes, value 6 s.; 3 saws, 5 s.; 1 augur, 1 s.; 1 screw-driver, 1 s.; 1 chisel, 1 s.; and 1 square, 2 s.; the goods of John Pearce.
THOMAS PEARCE . I live with my father, John Pearce, at Shepherd's Bush; he is a carpenter, and is finishing the house where we are residing; the prisoner is a plasterer, and was employed there by my father—we had some tools, which I put into a box, on the 20th of Jan., which was put into the next room to the one we live in—there were four planes, and the other articles stated in the indictment in it—these are the tools—they are my father's.
Prisoner. I removed them from the kitchen up to that room. Witness. No, I took them up myself.
HENRY HAMPSTEAD . I conduct the business of Mr. Aldhouse, a pawnbroker,
at Hammersmith. On the 21st of Jan. this plane and this saw were pawned by the prisoner; on the 25th he pawned a screw-driver and square; on the 26th another plane; he also pawned some more tools, but I did not take them in—they were all pawned in the name of Smith—the duplicates produced by the officer are those I gave the prisoner.
JOHN PAISLEY ( police-constable T 86.) I took the prisoner last Tuesday—I found on him fifteen duplicates, eight of which refer to this property.
Prisoner's Defence. The prosecutor was taken ill on the Thursday, and the day after I packed up all his tools and took them up into an upper bed-room, and they laid there all the following week—I had no work to do that week—I sat up with him two nights, and stopped with him two days; I merely took the tools, as I wanted a shilling or two that week, not intending to rob him—I had the duplicates all carefully in my pocket, to get them out; I did not deny the fact when I was taken; I had commenced work again the day I was taken.
THOMAS PEARCE re-examined. I have known him since last June, but have not worked with him—I never knew anything wrong of him—it is true about my father being ill, and that he sat up with him two or three nights.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18430130-754]
754. HENRY THOMPSON was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of Jan., 1 printed book, value 1 s., the goods of Isaac Henry Hatfield.
ISAAC HENRY HATFIELD . I am a bookseller, and live in Chapel-st., Tottenham-court-road. This is my book—I missed it from a board outside my window, on Tuesday evening, the 24th of Jan.—I had seen it safe on the 22nd.
ANN RAWLINGS . I am the wife of John Edward Rawlings, a bookseller, in Tottenham-court-road. On the afternoon of the 25th of Jan. the prisoner offered me this book for sale—he said he had purchased it of a boy in the street the week before, and given him 6 d. for it—I gave him in charge on suspicion.

Prisoner's Defence. A boy asked me to sell it for him.

755. MARY ANN MANLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of Dec., 1 1/4 yard of satin, value 3 s., the goods of Thomas Gwyther.
SARAH TUCKER . I am the wife of William Tucker, of Warren-street, Tottenham-court-road. I have known the prisoner nearly two years—she had been my servant, and left me in Nov., 1841—she came to me before last Christmas, I do not know the day, and asked me if I would accept of this satin—it had been a bonnet, but was not a bonnet then—this bonnet ( producing it) was made of it.
WILLMOT GWYTHER . I am the wife of Thomas Gwyther, of Tunbridge-street, New-road. The prisoner came to my house several times before Christmas—I missed about a yard and a quarter of satin from my back-kitchen—it was for making a bonnet—I left the prisoner in my front-kitchen the day I missed it—I went to market, and when I came home I missed it, but I do not know that it had been taken that day—the prisoner said it must be the washerwoman who had taken it, and I accused the woman of it—I am quite sure this bonnet is made of that satin.
ELIZA GWYTHER . I remember this satin being lost—the prisoner was at our house that day—when my mother spoke about it, she said it must have been the washerwoman.
Prisoner. She gave me the satin; it was a bonnet, which had been given to her. Witness. I am sure I never gave it to her.
SARAH TUCKER re-examined. When she gave it me, she said a friend of hers, who was a dressmaker, gave it her—I heard her say that Mr. Gwyther's daughter gave it to her.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430130-756]
756. MARY ANN MANLEY was again indicted for stealing, on the 15th of July, 1 thimble, value 1 s. 6 d.; and 3 towels, 3 s.; the goods of William Tucker.
SARAH TUCKER . I am the wife of William Tucker. I have known the prisoner two years—she was in my service fourteen or fifteen months ago—after she left I missed three towels and a thimble—these are them—( looking at them)—when the prisoner called occasionally at my house, I told her I had lost them, and she helped me to look for the thimble, which I lost some time after she left her situation, and was in the habit of calling on me—I think it must have been between the latter end of April and the beginning of July—the prisoner came to me in June, 1841, and left in Nov.—she returned at the latter end of April, 1842, and was with me up to the 28th of June, and then left me—I suppose that was the time I lost the thimble.
JURY. Q. Have you your name on these towels? A. No, but I made them, and have been in the habit of washing and ironing them—I know this thimble perfectly—there is no mark on it, but I am familiar with it, and it fits me exactly—it is nearly new—the prisoner was occasionally at my house—she finally left about the 1st of July—I have found two more towels, which were also mine.
WILLIAM MURDOCK. I am shopman to Mr. Flemming, a pawnbroker, in Farringdon-street—I produce there towels, pawned on the 31st August—I cannot say by whom—I gave a duplicate of them.
JAMES HENRY STEWARD . I am shopman to Mr. Kirkman, a pawnbroker. I have a thimble, pawned by Allener, on the 15th of July.
JOHANNA ALLENER . The prisoner gave me this thimble to pawn for her, which I did.
Prisoner. I left it at her house, and she pawned it without my knowledge. Witness. The prisoner gave it me, and asked me to pawn it for her, and I pawned it, I think, the same day, or a day or two after.
SUSANNAH BURTON . I searched the prisoner at the station, on the 14th of Jan.—I found on her six duplicates, in a tin-box—I gave them to the officer.
EDWARD HURST ( police-constable E 23.) On the 14th of Jan. I took the prisoner on suspicion of stealing a quantity of wearing apparel—I received from Burton these duplicates, two of which correspond with these articles, and with the counterparts produced by the pawnbroker.
Prisoner's Defence. On the 28th of June, Mrs. Tucker had words with me, and I left her on the 29th; I staid at Mr. Cole's till the 1st of July; I never was in her house again till October; the towels are my own, and so is the thimble.
GUILTY. Aged 28.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18430130-757]
757. MARY ANN COWARD was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of Jan., 1 scarf, value 2 s.; 1 shawl, 10 s.; 1 gown, 10 s.; 1 bonnet, 3 s.; 1 purse, 3 d.; 1 cross, 1 s.; 1 neck-chain, 2 s.; 2 pairs of boots, 1 l.; 1 coat, 8 s.; 1 watch-chain, 2 l.; 2 seals, 10 s.; 1 watch-key, 5 s.; 1 ring, 5 s.; 1 brooch, 1 l.; 2 handkerchiefs, 5 s.; 1 half-crown, I shilling, 2 groats, and 2 pence; the property of Stephen William New; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
STEPHEN WILLIAM NEW . I am a musician, and live in Upper Mary-lebone-street—the prisoner it the daughter of my laundress—I misted a number of articles from my sitting-room, part of them on Sunday evening, the 1st of Jan., and part on the Monday—I missed a gold watch-chain, seals, key, and ring from the corner of the piano, in the sitting-room—I afterwards missed the pearl cross, the scarf, and other things stated—this is the chain, scarf, cross, purse, gown, shawl, and bonnet—they are my wife's, and were in my sitting-room.
HENRY FOWLER ( police-constable E 111.) I took the prisoner on the 3rd of Jan., in Tottenham-court-road—she had this scarf, shawl, gown, and bonnet on, and this chain and cross was round her neck—I took her to the station—she said, "Let me go, and take off the things, as I have got my own things under them"—they were taken off, and she had her other things under them.
Prisoner. They belong to Mrs. Hollis.
STEPHEN WILLIAM NEW re-examined. I kept this chain and cross in a drawer at the top of a cash-box—it was not locked—these are my wife's property, who is here—her name was Hollis before she was married.
ROBERT ADAMS ( police-constable 109 E.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Transported for Seven Years.

759. ELIZA MATENOT and MARY ANN JOHNSON were indicted for stealing, on the 28th of Jan., 2 hams, value 15 s., the goods of John Taylor; and that they had been before convicted of felony.
JOHN TAYLOR . I am an oilman, and live in Jermyn-street. At half-past eight on Saturday night the prisoners came into my shop—Matenot asked for an ounce of pepper—she gave me a fourpenny-piece—I gave change—I suspected them—I watched, and saw a shuffling between their shawls the moment I served them—I came round the counter and missed two hams—I pursued them—they had turned up a passage, where I found them, and said, "You have got my hams; you had better give them up"—then one dropped, and then another dropped—a light was got in the house they ran into, and the two hams were found in the dark passage—I cannot say from which they fell.
THOMAS MARCHANT ( police-constable C 49.) I was sent for, took the prisoners, and found the hams in the passage.
Matenot's Defence. I did not ask for the pepper; it was the other put down the fourpenny-piece.
EEWARD TANNER ( police-constable E 56.) I produce a certificate of
Matenot's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office ( read)—Matenot is the person that was tried.
GEORGE JOHN RESTIEAUX ( police-constable E 49.) I produce a certificate of Johnson's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office ( read)—she is the person who was tried.
MATENOT — GUILTY . Aged 18.
JOHNSON — GUILTY . Aged 19.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18430130-760]
760. WILLIAM WESTON was indicted for embezzling and stealing 2 s. 6 d., the monies of Joshua Chipchase, his master.
MESSRS. CLARKSON AND DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
JOSHUA CHIPCHASE . I am a shoemaker, and live in High-street, Shadwell. The prisoner was in my employ about sixteen months—in consequence of something in the week previous to the 9th of Jan. I agreed with Mr. Barrett to mark a half-crown—about ten o'clock on the 9th of Jan. I went down into my shop and asked the prisoner to account for what money he had taken—he said 5 s. 6 d. for a pair of boots, and 2 s. 6 d. for a pair of shoes, which had just gone out—I said, "Has there been nothing else sold?"—he said "No"—I said, "You sold a pair of slippers for half-a-crown at nine o'clock"—he said, "Yes, but you had the money for them." I had been called down at ten minutes past nine, and there was a customer in the shop, who bought a pair of slippers for half-a-crown—I had got that half-crown—I said, "Yes, but you sold a pair exactly at nine"—he denied it—if he had sold another pair at nine, and bad received half-a-crown for them, he was half-a-crown short in the money he had accounted to me for—I told him that when I came down at five minutes past nine I lifted up the lid of the desk, and in a back place, where money never was kept, I saw the edge of a half-crown—he said, "I suppose you put it there"—I said, "No, but I will tell you how it came there"—I told him for a long time I had suspected he was robbing me, and for the last few weeks I was quite sure of it—he denied it—I said, "Have you a half-crown about you?"—he said, "Yes"—he then threw down two half-crowns and two shillings on the counter—I told him that the half-crown with which the slippers were purchased was marked, and on taking up one it was marked—I sent for Mr. Barrett, and in the prisoner's presence asked him if he knew that half-crown—he said yes, it was the one marked.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Had the prisoner lived with you before?— A. Yes, once; he left to get married—I gave him a very bad character, but the clergyman believed him rather than me—there was 100 l. due to him on his marriage, if he could get a good character; and he applied to me, but I gave him a bad character—I took him again, because I hoped he was better—I agreed with him that he was to stay a year, if he conducted himself to my entire satisfaction, so that I could not discharge him within the year—the errand-boy was present when I had this conversation about the slippers—I never allowed the prisoner to give change out of his own pocket—there were always persons in the house to do so, my wife or my daughters—I never told him not to give change in so many words, but it was not his habit—this is a book in which he ought to enter these things—he has nothing to do with the daily expenses book.
Prisoner. The man Collins made a pair of bespoke shoes in the name of Wells—he brought them to me while I had a customer in the shop, and wanted to be paid—I said I had nothing less than a half-crown—I put my hand in my pocket and gave him a shilling, and asked if that would do.
Witness. I have no recollection of that—if he had, it was his business to have told me, and he never said a word about it.
MR. DOANE. Q. Did the prisoner ever ask for the daily expense book before the Magistrate? A. No—whether he gave change or not, there is still balf-a-crown short in what he accounted for, supposing there had been a pair sold at nine o'clock.
JAMES BARRETT . I am a grocer, and live in High-street, Shadwell. On the 8th of Jan. I marked a half-crown, and gave it to my brother Daniel—this is it.
Cross-examined. Q. What mark did you put upon it? A. Two nicks above the crown, on the reverse of the coin.
DANIEL BARRETT . I received a half-crown from my brother—I saw the mark on it, and gave it to my sister.
ANN BARRETT . I received the half-crown from my brother, and gave it to Elizabeth Murray, who was in our service, and gave her directions what to do with it.
ELIZABETH MURRAY . I was in the service of Miss Barrett. I received from her this half-crown—I took it to Mr. Chipchase's shop, to buy a pair of slippers, at nine o'clock precisely—Shadwell clock was striking—I bought a pair of slippers—they came to half-a-crown—I gave the prisoner the half-crown that was given to me by my mistress.
Cross-examined. Q. Were there any other customers in the shop? A. No.
WILLIAM KEELEY ( police-constable K 175.) I received this half-crown from the prosecutor.
JOSEPH CHIPCHASE re-examined. This is the half-crown I found on the prisoner—he lent me 3 s. 6 d. before breakfast that morning, but it was paid him again.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 34.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18430130-761]
761. HENRY SCREW was indicted for embezzlement.
ELIZA MORRISH . I am single, and deal with Mr. Gardner. On the 27th of Dec. I paid the prisoner 6 s. 10 d. for some meat I had of his master.
BENJAMIN GARDNER . I have not received this 6 s. 10 d.—it was the prisoner's business to have given it me the same evening, but he absconded.
Prisoner. He is indebted to me more than this. Witness. I am not—he is in my debt 17 s. for money he drew from me and my wife.

762. THOMAS WOOD was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of Jan., 2lbs. weight of mutton, value 1 s.; and 3/4lb. weight of pork, 6 d.; the goods of Benjamin Gardner.
BENJAMIN GARDNER . I am a butcher, and live at Ealing. The prisoner was employed for me—on the 7th of Jan. he said he was ill—after I had come from Bow-street about the last case, the prisoner said, "Master, honesty is the best policy, I will look out"—I then missed a loin of mutton, and sent my son round—the prisoner came in, and said he was very bad in his inside—there were eleven chops on the stall—he said he would go away for a minute or two—I missed the chops—I ran, and saw him running up a court into a coffee-shop—I saw a man with some chops—he was going to put them into a pan—I said, "Who gave you these?"—he said, "Your man"—I went back and took the prisoner—I did not find any more chops, or the loin of mutton.
Prisoner. I never saw them. Witness. They were there not a minute before you left.
THOMAS FISHER . I keep a coffee-shop in Charles-street. On this evening
the prisoner brought in two chops, one mutton and one pork, and the prosecutor came in immediately.
JONATHAN BUSH ( police-constable F 40.) I took the prisoner—he said he had only taken two chops for his tea, he thought there was no harm in that.
Prisoner's Defence. There was some stinking meat brought out, and I could not eat it.
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Confined Six Months.

765. THOMAS SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of Jan., I cart, value 2 l. 10 s., the goods of John Cooper.
JOHN COOPER . I am a builder, and live in Newland-street, Pimlico. On the 13th of Jan. the prisoner came and asked me to let him have my cart to sell it—I said, "No, you shall not take the cart without you bring the money first"—he had had it before for sale—he did not bring me any money for it—I did not permit him in any way to take it—he came up to me on the 21st, and said, "I am very sorry, master, I have sold your cart, and lost the money"—I said, "As soon as I can see a policeman I shall give you in charge."
FREDERICK COOPER . The prisoner came and asked if I would let him have the cart—I said, "Not without my father's permission"—he went away, then came back, and said my father had given him leave, and I let him have it.
Prisoner. His father was at the Wellington; I went there, and he gave me leave to do the best I could, and told me not to bring it home if it was late, but to take it to Mr. Digby's—the next morning I went to his buildings in the Vauxhall-road, and did not see him; in coming back, I saw him, and spoke to him; he sent my brother to know whether I had told the cart; I told him when I met him that he should not lose a farthing; I would pay 10 s. a week; I do not live a stone's throw from him.
JOHN COOPER . It was above ten days before I cound find where he lived—I lost the cart on the 13th, and he did not come to me till the 21st.
Witnesses for the Defence.
STEPHEN SMITH . I am the prisoner's brother. The prosecutor sent down to me, to know if my brother had sold the cart, as he had bad it long enough to sell—I said I did not know.
GEORGE CURRY . About six weeks ago I met the prisoner in George-street—he said he was going to sell the cart and horse—he sold the horse, but could not sell the cart—he brought it back, and the prosecutor's son let him in at the gate.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430130-756a]
756. GEORGE TAYLOR, DAVID KING , and WILLIAM WATSON were indicted for stealing, on the 13th of Jan., 350lbs. weight of lead, value 2 l. 10 s.; 850lbs. weight of iron, 2 l.; and 1 sink, 10 s.; the goods of Sir Robert Fitz Wygram, Bart., and fixed to a building.—2nd COUNT, not stating it to be fixed.—Four other COUNTS, stating it to belong to William Reid and others, and William Blencowe.
MESSRS. BODKIN and DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM THORNTON . I am a painter, and live in Hatton-wull. Enrly in the morning of Thursday, the 20th of July, I saw King come out of No. 27, with part of a cast-iron furnace—there was a van at the door, and King gave it to the van-man—Taylor then brought out some iron bars of the furnace, and gave them to the van-man—Taylor then came out, accompanied by Watson, with a sack containing the lead of a water-closet—he gave that to the vanman—Watson and King brought out the lead lining of a cistern—I went up to Mr. Knot, at Mr. Reed's, the brewhouse—I then found Fink, the policeman—he came to the house.
JOHN FINK ( police-constable G 47.) Thornton made a communication to me—I went to No. 27, Hatton-wall—I found a van at the door—I saw Taylor bring out a sack—I got into the van, and found it contained lead—it appeared to be the bottom of a water-cistern—I found three other sacks in the van, containing pipes, and part of a water-closet—I fitted these things to what remained on the premises, and they corresponded exactly—I went to the house—Taylor shut the door in my face—I got another officer, and got into the house—I there found Taylor—he said I had no right to interfere with people moving their own property in open day-light—I saw Watson in the shop, and King in the parlour—I asked Watson what he was doing—he said he merely came in to be shaved—King said he was a lodger, and they had nothing to do with the moving, they were only employed by the landlord to move the things—there was not the slightest appearance of shaving—some part of the shutters were down, and some up—I sent for Mr. Knott, the managing clerk of the brewery—King then said he wanted something to drink, and ordered coffee of the woman in the parlour, and they all partook of it—I went over the premises—in the back-house the whole of the roof was off, and the furnace and iron-work was gone—the lead I found fitted it—Mr. Knott came, and gave the prisoners in charge—as we were going
along, Watson said he assisted in moving the things, and was to hare something to eat and drink for his trouble—there was 350Ibs. of lead in the van, a stone sink, and 850lbs. of iron.
JOHN KNOTT . I am managing clerk to William Reid and others, brewers—No. 27, Hatton-wall, is their freehold property. On the 13th of Jan. the policeman sent for me about half-past nine o'clock—I went, and found them in the state described, and the van loaded—the damage is to the amount of 60 l.
Taylor. Q. Did you not come into my shop, and represent yourself as the landlord? A. As acting for the landlord—you refused to let me see the premises when I came—the neighbours said you had been stripping lead from this roof, and I came, and asked to see it—you said you had orders not to let any one see it.
Taylor's Defence. When I took this shop, this landlord had applied to the house of call for a hairdresser; I was directed to this house, and was kept till twelve o'clock; when Cameron came he wanted to detain the house, and to employ me in it; I did not like it; he allowed me the shop at 5 s. a-week, and I was annoyed by one and another representing themselves as landlord; I left it, and had been out a day, and Cameron coaxed me back again; I came back in the morning of this transaction; I did not lodge in the place; I came in, and saw this van at the door; the landlord had told me the night before, that he had some men coming; he had lodged in the house, and that morning he asked me if I would lend a hand to these things; they were all in the passage; at first I refused, but he said he would pay me for so doing.
King's Defence. He came, and asked me to move a few things from the passage to the van, and I did so.
Watson's Defence. I went there, and found the landlord; he asked me to assist in moving these things; I had not been there above ten minutes before the policeman came.
JOHN KNOTT re-examined. I have seen a person In the place, who said his name was Cameron.
TAYLOR — GUILTY . Aged 24.
KING — GUILTY . Aged 46.
WATSON — GUILTY . Aged 30.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18430130-757a]

758. GEORGE RATHBONE was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of Jan., 12 shirts, value 1 l. 1 s., the goods of Eliza Ann Plum.
EDWARD SULLINGS . I am in the employ of Mr. Peggs, a confectioner, in Whitechapel-road. On the 21st of Jan., about twelve o'clock at night, I was in the shop of Eliza Ann Plum—I saw the prisoner take twelve shirts from the counter, and throw them down outside, and two boys outside took them—one of them dropped the bundle—I ran, but did not take either of them—I took the prisoner in the shop.
CHARLOTTE PLUM . I live with my daughter Eliza Ann Plum, who keeps a hosier's shop in Whitechapel-road. There were three bundles taken—the other two are quite lost—I picked up the bundle that was dropped just outside the door—I am quite sure these are my daughter's shirts.
Prisoner. I went to buy a shoe-lace, and while I was there, some one must have come in, and got these; there was no one outside; the lady left me at the door, and I did not move. Witness. I was in the act of giving him
change—I saw something, and ran to the door, and took this bundle up—I turned to the prisoner, and told him he was one of the boys, but I did not know that he was the one that took them—they had been on the opposite counter to me, about three yards from the door—the prisoner must have turned round to get them—there was no one in the shop but the prisoner and I—there had been another, but he went out before the prisoner came in.
EDWARD SULLINGS re-examined. I ran after the other two, and Mrs. Plum detained the prisoner in the shop—I am sure I saw the prisoner move the bundle to the other two.
GUILTY . ** Aged 18.— Transported for Seven Years.

760. THOMAS HIGGINS was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of Jan., 1 till, value 1 s.; 15 pence, 45 halfpence, and 65 farthings; the property of William Birnie: and that he bad been before convicted of felony.
JOHN BALLAM . I am journeyman to Mr. William Birnie. On the 19th of Jan. I was in the bakehouse, and heard my mistress call—I ran up, ran out, and saw the prisoner running with the till under his arm—a witness stopped him—I came up and found the till on him—it was my master's, and there were pence, halfpence, and farthings in it.
WILLIAM DENNIS . I stopped the prisoner with the till under his arm—he was running as fast as he could—I asked where he got it—he said some boy brought it out of the shop and put it on bis head—I did not see any one but him.
JANET BIRNIE . This is my son's till—his name is William Birnie—I saw it safe that morning, and it contained pence, halfpence, and farthings.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going by, a young man stopped me, and said, "Here is a box, you may have what is in it;" I was going along, and a gentleman stopped me; this lady said she saw me in the shop; I was never in it in my life.
WILLIAM HIGH ( police-constable K 242.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Transported for Seven Years—Convict Ship.

not go in with him. Witness. You pawned the counterpane while the man was at the door.

762. JAMES DONNELLY was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of Jan., 1 pint and a half of gin, value 2 s., the goods of Richard Blake.
JANE BLAKE . I am the wife of Richard Blake—the prisoner was in my service. On the 31st of Jan. I came down in the morning, and my little boy told me something—I found a pewter pot and a stone bottle full of gin.
GEORGE BLAKE . I live with my parents. I saw the prisoner take two bottles off the end of the counter, draw a pint of gin, and put it into the bottles—I told my mother, and she found it under the counter.
Prisoner. I got the funnel from him; I was going to take it to the shop; I said I would pay for the gin when the money came; he gave me the bottles. Witness. He took the pot and drew the gin out of the tap on the counter—he saw I was watching him—I gave him the funnel.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430130-763a]
763. JOHN COLLINS and JAMES WILLIAMS were indicted for stealing, on the 16th of Jan., 330lbs. weight of pork, value 5 l. 12 s.; and 1 cask, 2 s.; the goods of John Hutley.
EDWARD HANSON . I am carman to Mr. John Hutley, a wholesale cheesemonger, in Broad-street, St. Giles's. I put out a cask of pork from half-past six to seven o'clock on the 16th of Jan.—I saw it safe about seven, and a little after eight it was missing—the pork was worth 5 l. 12 s., and the cask 2 s.,—this is the cask—here is my master's mark on the head of it
WILLIAM CHADWICK ( police-sergeant F 2.) I was on duty in High-street on the 16th of Jan., just before eight o'clock, nearly opposite the prosecutor's shop—I saw the prisoner Collins and another man go to the prosecutor's shop, roll away a cask, and take it to the comer of George-street—they then went and spoke to Williams, who was walking up and down in sight of the prosecutor's shop—I am sure he was in sight of it when Collins and the other man were rolling the cask away—he did not touch it, but they left it and went and spoke to Williams—he walked to the shop, looked in, and then came to them again—then they commenced rolling it away—when they got to George-street, I saw a truck—Collins had hold of the bottom of the cask in the truck, and the other man had hold of the handle to pull it down—they then saw me—the other man ran away, and I caught hold of Collins, and asked what he was going to do with it—he said he did not know—I expect Williams was at that time against the shop.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Had Williams anything with him? A. No—I had not seen him before.
WILLIAM CHADWICK . I am a police-inspector. I stopped Williams near the prosecutor's shop—I told him he must come to the station—he said he knew nothing about it—I had not said any thing to him previously.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you been to Mr. Hutley's shop before you went to Williams? A. I had—I went and told him he must come with me to the station—that was directly after I came from the shop—he was about three or lour yards from the shop.
ELLEN REARDON . I keep an apple-stall opposite the prosecutor's shop. Williams came to me about a quarter-past seven o'clock—he said, "Did you see a wagon with eight horses?"—I said, "No"—he said he came up three times a week, and he wanted to take six of them—he put his hand on the cask, and then he put his foot, and rolled it a little—I did not see Collins.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know Williams? A. No—I never saw him before—I have been sitting in that street about fifteen years—Williams was talking to me about ten minutes—there were not a great many persons about—I never told anybody what I had heard till next morning, when the shopkeeper asked me about it—when Williams pushed the cask, I went away—I generally stand in one place in St. Giles's—I was not there when the officer took Collins—I went away two or three minutes after Williams spoke to me.
STEPHEN CALCOTT . Collins hired a truck of me that evening.
Collins's Defence. I was coming along St. Giles's; a respectably dressed man told me to go to the Spur Inn, in the Borough, to fetch two boxes and a beds ead, for 2 s.; I agreed to do it; I went and got the truck, and when we came to the corner of George-street, he said, "We will go and have a drop of gin;" we did, and then he said, "I have got a cask to leave on the road;" he left me with the gin, and was gone six or seven minutes, and when he called me the cask was at the tail of the truck; I was lifting it up, and when the man saw the policeman he ran away and left me; I never saw Williams till he was taken.
COLLINS — GUILTY . Aged 38.— Confined Six Months.
WILLIAMS **— GUILTY . Aged 44.— Transported for Seven Years.
