[trialAccount t18430130-768]
768. PHILLIP HIBBITT was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of Dec., 1 box, value 6 d.; 25 sovereigns, 100 crowns, 200 half-crowns, 300 shillings, 200 sixpences, and 300 groats; the property of Benjamin Worthy Horne and another, his masters.—2nd COUNT, stating it to be the property of Joseph Roundtree.
MESSRS. BODKIN and PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN CASSON . I live with Mr. Joseph Roundtree, a grocer, on the Pavement, at York. On the 27th of Dec, I sent a box to Mr. Stereker, of Budge-row, containing 75 l. in silver, and 25 l. in gold—I counted it myself, and saw it put into the box—the silver was wrapped in 10 l. parcels—there were 3 l. 10 s. in fourpenny-pieces—it was all packed up in the box, and some shavings put into it—the box was about six inches long and five deep—I saw it corded, and directed the only card which was nailed on was to William Stereker, No. 13, Budge-row, London—I gave it to Christopher Robinson, to take to the railway station.
CHRISTOPHER ROBINSON . I am in the service of Mr. Roundtree, of the Pavement, York. On the 27th of Dec., a box was delivered to me by Casson—it was directed "William Stereker, Budge-row, London"—I nailed the lid on, and took it to the railway station.
HENRY RODGERS . I am passing-clerk of the York station. On the 27th of Dec., I received a box directed to Mr. Stereker—I entered it in the way-bill, "Box, Stereker"—this is the way-bill—I gave it to the guard of the carriage.
THOMAS BRAMLEY . I am guard of the York and North Midland train. I received the parcel to go to Budge-row, and this way-bill—I took the box of the clerk—I put it in the van, and locked it, to come to London.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Do you really remember that particular box? A. Yes—it was Tuesday evening when I put it into the van—I was asked about it on Friday, at York.
JOHN COOPER . I am clerk of the parcels' office of the London and Birmingham Railway Company, Euston-square. The parcels are called over to me from the way-bill—I remember this way-bill being received on the 28th of
Dec.—I saw the name of "Stereker, box," on it—I wrote on it, Budge-row, according to the direction on the parcel.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see the box yourself? A. I did not—I cannot say how many men were employed at the time this train came—perhaps there might be four or five men within the compass of three or four yards—all the parcels are pitched in a heap on the floor, and each man picks out his own—Mr. Davis is managing clerk for Messrs. Horne and Chaplin—I have beard that he refuses to take silver from the men.
JAMES SMITH . I am porter to the London and Birmingham Railway Company at Euston-square. On the 28th of Dec. I was present when the parcels were called over which arrived by the train from York—they were handed to one of Home and Chaplin's men, (I do not remember which)—each of them fetch them away as they are called over.
JAMES THORGOOD . I am in the employ of Home and Chaplin, carriers. I live in Seymour-street, Euston-square—the prisoner was in their employ on the 28th of Dec.—I remember leaving the railway station at half-past seven o'clock that morning with the prisoner—we had a cart—there were parcels in the cart—the prisoner drove the horse, and I sat by his side—I remember a box being put in at Euston-square station, directed to Mr. Stereker, Budge-row—I sorted that with other parcels—the direction was nailed on with five tacks, and tied round with cord—it appeared to be a rather heavy box—the cart was driven to the Old Bailey—I remember there seeing the parcel—I left the cart to deliver a parcel in Prujean-square—I was gone about five or ten minutes—when I returned I found the cart was standing at Mr. Fenn's door, in Newgate-street—the prisoner was not with it, but standing at the corner of the Magpie and Stump—I asked why he left the cart—he said he left some one in charge of it—I did not see any one in charge of it—the prisoner and I then went to the cart—he asked if I would go and do something in Paternoster-row, and I asked him to take a parcel to Fisher's, in Newgate-street—I left him in Newgate-street, and I took the cart to Paternoster-row—he appointed to meet me at the corner of Queen-street, Cheapside—when I left him, he turned round to go towards the Magpie and Stump—when I got to Paternoster-row, I looked for the box, and it was gone—I had seen it in the cart when we stopped in the Old Bailey—after delivering the parcels in Paternoster-row I went to Queen-street, and saw the prisoner there—his brother and Cottrell were with him—I asked him whether we had not a parcel directed for Mr. Stereker, Budge-row—he said, "No, that was yesterday, or the morning before"—I said, "I think we had one this morning"—he did not go any further with the cart, he said he was not very well—he had had rather too much to drink, and he asked me if I would go with the cart—Cotterell was to go with me—it was the prisoner's duty to have gone through with the cart, and delivered the parcels with me—in the evening of that day I found, at the bottom of the cart, the direction of "Mr. Stereker, Budge-row"—I put it in the bottom of the cart again—the next morning I told the prisoner I had found a direction to Mr. Stereker, Budge-row—he said that was the name he had got one charged to in his book, and asked what I had done with the direction—I said I had left it at the bottom of the cart—he asked me to get it him—I told him I would, as soon as we got rid of some of the parcels—I found it the same morning, and gave it to the prisoner—he tore it up, and told me to swear that I never saw the parcel—on the Saturday I communicated this to my brother—I and he went to the railway station.
Cross-examined. Q. About how many parcels were there when you put them in the cart? A. Between fifty and sixty—this was rather a dirty box, the cord was tied round tight, I took particular notice of it—the direction
was nailed on with five nails—I did not hear the gentleman call out this parcel—it was with the other parcels when I came to the station—it is a common thing, when we go out, for one to take a direction of two or three streets, and another some others—I delivered a box, wrapped round with cloth, at Pru-jean-square, and five or six parcels in Paternoster-row—none before I got there—I missed the box before that, on my first stopping—it had been on the right-hand side of the cart—there were other parcels by the side of it—it was standing on the top of parcels, there was nothing upon it—I found Cottrell in Queen-street, and the prisoner's brother—I did not hear Cottrell undertake to do the prisoner's work for him—he did do it—he was standing at the side of the horse when I asked the prisoner if we bad not a parcel for Budge-row—I said I could not see it in the cart—I missed it, but I was not certain whether the prisoner had taken it—I told him, "There is a parcel gone, that I am sure of"—we joined company again about four or five hours after, in Gray's Inn-road, and then he appeared to have recovered from his drunkenness—the cart had one parcel in it then—Cottrell rode in the cart—I found the direction at the bottom of the cart, between two and three o'clock—Cottrell got out a little after three—he was inside when I arrived at the Globe-inn, and then the cart was empty, except one parcel—I cannot say where Cottrell sat, because I was driving, and did not look round to see—he was not sitting by the side of me, he must have been inside, where this direction was—I did not go and tell any one, "Here is the direction of the box that is lost"—it was nine at night that I found it, when I was in Bishopsgate-street—on Friday, about three, Mr. Dawson, our superintendent, asked me something about it—I told him I did not know any thing about it—I did not tell about my finding a direction, and putting it back—I told my brother something on Saturday—there was a great stir about the negligence of the porter about this parcel—I told my brother, because I had told Mr. Dawson a falsehood about it—there was no straw at the bottom of the cart.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Why did you tell Mr. Dawson an untruth that you after-repented of? A. Because the prisoner told me I should get into trouble if I said anything.
GEORGE KENT . I am a blind-maker, living in Kensington-row, Gray's Inn-road. I went to the Pinder of Wakefield, in Gray's Inn-road for change—the landlady said she could not give it me—I got change from a man there—I cannot say exactly what it consisted of, but a great deal of it was fourpenny-pieces—the man who gave me change was in company with another—I should know the other man—I believe the prisoner is the man who gave me the change.
Cross-examined. Q. Will you undertake to swear to him? A. I will not.
WILLIAM STOWTON . I keep the Globe, in Darby-street, King's-cross. I remember, on Wednesday, the 28th of Dec., Thorogood coming from a cart with a man—he had some communication with the prisoner there, who had been there before—Barker was with him—they came between two and three o'clock—the prisoner was not sober—the prisoner asked me to change 2 l. or 3 l. worth of silver—I only took 1 l.—there were 12 s. or 14 s. worth of four-penny-pieces among it—I said, "I have so many joes already, I won't take any more"—I gave one sovereign for 1 l. of silver.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you take any fourpenny pieces? A. Not one—I saw them.
THOMAS BARKER . On the 28th of Dec. I went with the prisoner to the Pinder of Wakefield—he had 1 l. worth of silver—I gave change for a sovereign, because the landlady would not take it—I gave that 1 l. worth of silver for the sovereign—the prisoner put the silver there—it consisted of shillings and 10 s. or 11 s. worth of fourpenny-pieces—we had been to the Globe before—we took them there because they would not take them at the Globe.
Cross-examined. Q. What was the whole amount of fourpenny-pieces? A. I think there were 10 s. worth—I live in Greville-mews.
WILLIAM THOROGOOD . My brother made a communication to me, on Saturday, about something that had happened to Messrs. Chaplin and Horne, in consequence of which I went up to the station, and made a statement there.
JOHN SAVAGE . I keep the Rising Sun, in Somers-town. A. few days after Christmas I gave the prisoner a 5 l. note, and he gave me 5 l. of silver—there were no fourpenny-pieces among them.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you known him? A. He has been in the habit of bringing me change all the last summer—I always thought him to be a well-conducted, honest man.
FREDERICK ALDHOUSE . I am clerk to Home and Chaplin. The prisoner paid me his accounts up to the 27th of Dec.—we always asked them to bring at much gold as they could.
BENJAMIN COTTRELL . I am porter at the Cross Keys, Wood-street. On the 28th of Dec. I drove the prisoner's cart—I first saw him between Wood-street and Milk-street—he was talking to somebody—he asked me to go with the cart and the boy—I refused to go at first, but afterwards consented, from the persuasion of the prisoner's brother—I went down Cheapside to the corner of Queen-street.
Cross-examined. Q. Was not the reason the brother gave that he was not fit to do duty, as he had been drinking too much? A. Yes; he was intoxicated—it was very near ten o'clock in the morning—the boy did not say to me that he had lost a parcel.
JAMES BARRY . I am superintendent of the police of the London and Bir-mingham Railway. I took the prisoner, on the 2nd of Jan., at Mr. Horne's private house, in Bedford-row—he said he was very much distressed, and had pawned his watch to make up his accounts, which I afterwards found to be untrue—I found 2 l. 10 s. in gold, 1 l. 0 s. 2 d. in silver, and some copper on him.
THOMAS THOROGOOD re-examined. I do not think we took any fourpenny-pieces on the 28th—we do not generally get more than six or seven in a day—he had not drank anything with me on the road—he was quite sober when he got to the Old Bailey.
(Edward Way, of Sun-street, Bishopsgate; and—Ewell, potato-dealer, Pit field-street, Hoxton; gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18430130-769]
769. HENRY LONSDALE and GEORGE ROBINS were indicted for stealing, on the 26th of Jan., 1 sixpence, the money of Thomas Marriott, the master of Lonsdale.
MESSRS. BODKIN and DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
ESTHER MARRIOTT . I am the wife of Thomas Marriott, and carry on business in High-street, Bloomsbury; Lonsdale was in our service, as barman for about three months. On the 26th of Dec. eight shillings and three sixpences, marked, were given to me by my husband, about five o'clock in the afternoon—I cleared the tills, and put the marked money in—I left the bar—there was 6 s. 6 d. in one till, and two shillings and two sixpences in the other—I saw Beresford, the inspector, in front of the bar after I put the money in—three customers came to the counter—I saw them served—I did not see what they had—after they were served, I ran to the tills, and there was no disturbance in the tills, and no increase—the silver was not moved—my attention was only drawn to the silver.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Where was the copper? A. In a separate till by itself—it is the same drawer, but there are partitions.
HENRY BERESFORD . I am an inspector of the C division. On this day I went with a gentleman to the prosecutor's house, a few minutes before five o'clock, in consequence of a communication I had with him a few days before—I was in disguise—I had been in seven or eight minutes, when two persons came to the wholesale counter—one called for a glass of spirits—a quartern of liquor was drawn by Lonsdale—while he was serving them across the counter, directly he drew it, he whispered to them—one of them put down a shilling—he dropped it into the till—the moment the money was given—he gave a shilling back and some halfpence—Robins then came in—they were all three together—Lonsdale, without asking a question, or any thing being said on either side, took a small liquor-glass, drew it full, and set it on the counter—they were all close together—Robins put down a sixpence on the counter—Lonsdale took it up and dropped it into the till—he then put his left hand into the halfpence, shook them, and never looked at his hand at all, but put some halfpence down, and took out the sixpence, and laid it down beside them—Robins took them up, and as he left the bar, put them into his right hand trowsers pocket—I went out at another door close by, and seized him with his hand in his pocket—I found 6 3/4 d. in copper, and a sixpence on him—I then took Lonsdale—in going down Queen-street, Robins asked him whether he thought it would hurt his character, or whether he could get bail—he said, "Hold your tongue, you b----y fool; whatever you have to say, say it irt another place."
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Where were you? A. In front of the bar—I had something to drink—Mr. Aldhouse, by my directions, called for it—I should say I was about three yards from where Robins was standing—it is a cross counter, and comes to an angle—I had my eye directed to what was going on, I swear I saw this sixpence, given by Robins, put into the till—Lonsdale took the halfpence out with his left hand, and took the sixpence out with his right.
HENRY ALDHOUSE . I was there by direction of Mr. Beresford—I have heard what he has stated—it is correct.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Did Lonsdale ring the six-pence? A. No, he put it into the till directly—I believe it was laid on the counter, and taken up—I know he dropped it into the till—I saw him pick it up—it was laid on the counter, to the best of my knowledge.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Are you sure it was a sixpence that was given? A. Yes, I am sure it was not a shilling—I was three yards off.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430130-770]
770. JAMES WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of Jan., 4 shillings, the monies of William Michael Davis.
MESSRS. BODKIN and PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
ROBERT LINCOLN . I am in the service of William Michael Davis, of the Blue Posts, Berwick-street. I had been there seven months—I had been there when a man named Ashton was barman—he went away in Oct.—I had seen the prisoner there during the time Ashton was there—I knew him—I met him going up Broad-street, nearly three months before he was taken into custody at the latter end of Oct.—he asked me if I wanted any money "worked" for me—I said no, I did not want any—he said it would be a great deal to my interest if I worked any—I said no, I never worked any, and did not wish to have any worked for me; that was all I said to him at that time, and we parted—I had heard "worked money" spoken of by my master, and had
read about it in the newspaper—I communicated to my master what had passed between me and the prisoner—about six weeks after my first conversation the prisoner came to my master's house—my master was in the bar at the time—he did not say anything then—on the 5th of Jan, my master gave me directions, in consequence of which I went to Lambeth-square, New-cut, where I thought I could find the prisoner—he was not in the way, am I wrote this note by my master's direction, and left it for him—( read —"Mr. Williams will greatly oblige me by calling on me at Mr. Davis's, Blue Posts, Berwick-street, as I have got a little business for you to do for me upon what you spoke to me about a little while ago")—I left it about five in the evening—about half-past seven he and his wife came to my master's house—I saw them outside the door—the prisoner came and patted me on the shoulder—I said I did not expect to see him down so soon—he said directly he got the note he came down—I asked if he could come on Saturday evening—he said perhaps I could give him something that same evening—I said he could come in if he liked—he came in, and bought some liquor with a six-pence—I gave him the proper change—he came a second time the same evening—I drew him a glass of liquor—he put down a shilling, and said, "On to it now"—I did not give him any more money than he was entitled to then—on the following Sunday I saw him in the street, and walked with him along Compton-street, and down Covent Garden-market, into the Strand—I went to the Red Lion there, and had a conversation with him about Ashton—an arrangement was made between me and him that he was to come down that evening, and come in once or twice in the evening, and I was to give him what I could each time—he said he was to put down a shilling, I was to take it up, make a pretence of putting it into the till, take out two or three more, and place it on the counter, end he was to take it up, and if he was to come in again, I was to rub my arm, if not, I was to put out my finger—I told my master all that had passed—the prisoner came in twice during that evening—on the Monday he was to come again—I saw some money marked, and put into the till—he came between seven and eight in the evening—an officer was sent for—the officer had arrived when the prisoner first came in that evening—I did not give him any money the first time—he came in again and bought a pennyworth of gin—he put down a shilling—I then gave him four marked shillings, the shilling he gave me, and 3 1/2 d.—directly my hand was off it, he took it off the counter and put it into his right hand pocket, and was going to walk off with it, when he was seized by the officer.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You seem to know the meaning of working money? A. I had heard it spoken of many times in the house, not among the pot-boys, but by my master—it was the common talk of the shop before the prisoner mentioned it—my master spoke of it when he suspected Ashton of robbing him—he said he could not think how the money could be worked for him to lose the money he did by Ashton—I am quite sure he said that—I mentioned the conversation I had with the prisoner to my master the same day, and nothing further was done—I am not related to my master—I had not been a public-house boy before I lived with Mr. Davis—I was boots at an hotel—I never heard of working money till my own master told me what it was—none of the conversation between me and the prisoner took place in my master's house—the Red Lion is a house that the prisoner proposed to go to on the Sunday—there were several people standing in that house.
WILLIAM MICHAEL DAVIS . I am Lincoln's master. In consequence of information from him, I marked ten shillings on Monday, the 9th of Jan., and put them into the till—some of them were afterwards produced to me by
Beresford, the officer—Lincoln had previously made communications to me on the prisoner's propositions to him—he acted in this with my knowledge and consent, and by my directions.
Cross-examined. Q. You told him to do all that he has done? A. Yes—I gave him directions to give the prisoner the money in the way he has done—I do not recollect that I told him to write the letter, but I told him to call upon the prisoner and renew the connexion—I first heard from him about this some time in the month of Oct.—I cannot say the day—it was the day the prisoner communicated with him—it was between two and three months before the letter was written—I had not an opportunity of having anybody placed to hear any conversation between him and the prisoner, but after the first conversation I felt an anxious wish to bring the prisoner to justice.
HENRY BERESFORD . I am an inspector of the C division of police. On the afternoon of the 9th of January I saw the boy Lincoln come out, and the prisoner was standing at the corner of Compton-street—I went on the following evening to the house—the prisoner came in—I searched him, and found on him five shillings and some copper—the next morning he wanted to have some coffee—I said, "No, I must keep this money"—he said, "It is my own money, I brought out six shillings, and I changed one shilling"—when I first seized him I found the money in his pocket—I showed it to Mr. Davis, and he identified four shillings of it as his money—the prisoner had just got to the door, when I seized him—he was not out.
Cross-examined. Q. And in fact he had got the money in bis hand? A. It was in his pocket—he had his hand in his pocket—I took his hand out and put my hand in, and took the money—I cannot say whether his hand was closed—I told him to take his hand out—I watched him all the time—when he received the money from the boy he closed his hand upon it, and put it into his pocket—I found on him the five shillings and 6 1/2 d. in copper.
MR. DAVIS re-examined. These are four of the shillings I marked and put into the till with six others, which I have in my pocket.
GUILTY. Aged 30.— Judgment Respited.
Fifth Jury, before Edward Bullock, Esq.

772. AMELIA GILLINGS was indicted for obtaining goods by false pretences.
JANE ANN SHEPHERD . I am barmaid at the Dover Castle, in St. George's in the East, which is kept by Mr. Abraham Watson. The prisoner came there on the 6th of Jan., about half-past eight o'clock (I never saw her before)—she said, "I want half a pint of gin and a pot of porter for Mr. Mace, next door to the baker's shop"—I served her—she came again, and said she wanted another half-pint of gin and a pot of porter for Mr. Mace, who was out, and as soon as he came in he would send over the money, and she had it—she came again for another half-pint of gin and another pot of beer—I let her have it—I saw Mr. Mace afterwards—the next morning I saw the prisoner come with the empty pots—I asked her how she came to come for the gin and porter for Mr. Mace, when he did not send her—she said she knew Mr. Mace did not send, but a gentleman whom he knew did.
JOHN MACE . I am a lighterman, and live at No. 27, Church-row, opposite the Dover Castle—I deal there for beer—I don't know the prisoner—I did not send her there for gin or beer on the 6th of Jan.
GEORGE CARR ( police-constable K 166.) I was sent for to the Dover Castle on the 7th of Jan.—I received the prisoner in custody—as we were going along she said she had fetched the things, and intended to pay for them at dinner time; that Mr. Mace did not send her, hut a friend of his did—I said, "Who was that friend?"—she said, "I will not tell—I fetched it myself, and I don't wish to get any body else into trouble."
Prisoner. I am very sorry for what I have done. I had but 2 s. for it
GUILTY . Aged 35.— Confined One Month.

773. HENRY HUNT was indicted for unlawfully obtaining 2lbs. of beef steaks by false pretences.

[trialAccount t18430130-774]
774. PHILIP GRIFFITHS was indicted for assaulting Stephen Gibbs and William Munns, on the 4th of Nov., with intent to stab, cut, and wound them, to prevent his (the prisoner's) lawful apprehension.—2nd COUNT, stating his intent to be to do some grievous bodily harm.
MR. HOWORTH conducted the Prosecution.
STEPHEN GIBBS . I am game-keeper to Mr. Anderton. I was employed at South Mimms on a manor belonging to the Marquis of Salisbury, but let to Mr. Anderton—I was at Furzefield-wood on the 4th of Nov.—I saw the prisoner cutting the underwood—there was a great quantity of it cut and lying on each side of where he was—it was worth 1 s. 6 d. or 2 s.—Munns was with me—we came up to the prisoner—he ran into the wood—we ran after him—before I got up the prisoner struck Munns with a stick, and knocked his hat off—I came up and said, "Philip, is that you?"—he said yes, and he would rip me up with a knife—he struck at me—I fell back, and the knife ran up my waistcoat and jacket and touched my chin, but it did not enter my flesh, nor fetch blood—he then took up a brick and threw it at me—he made several attempts at us with hit knife, but we kept him off with our sticks—he said he would stab the first man that came near him; and as I knew him I thought it would be as well to let him go, and not to run any further danger.
Cross-examined by MR. CROUCH. Q. What time of night was this? A. From half-past five to six—it was dark—the prisoner was thirty yards off when I first saw him—he was cutting the birch—it was getting dusk, but was not dark—I saw it was a man with a white smock on, and I knew him as soon as I got near him, before any thing took place. Munns went one way and I the other—it was all thin wood—we could see him all along—I had a stick in my hand—I struck him with my stick, to try to knock the knife out of his hand, after he had tried to stab me—he attempted to stab Munns, and then I ran up and tried to get the knife out of his hand—I did not strike him before he used the expression of ripping me up—we afterwards followed him on further—I asked where he lived; he said he had no home—I knew he lived in the village—he kept running one way and another, and swore he would rip us up—we were obliged to keep him off with sticks—the wood is composed of birch and oak—there might be acorns about, but no one has any right there—Mr. Warby rents the wood of the Marquis of Salisbury, and lets it to my master—it does not belong to Mr. White—his grounds are round the wood—a person passing through the wood might go to Mr. White's, but we allow no one to go through.
MR. HOWORTH. Q. This was on the 4th of Nov.? A. Yes, from half-past five to six o'clock—it was getting dusk—I did not know who the person was at first—I am quite sure I did not lose sight of him till I came up with him and Munns—I am sure the person I saw cutting the wood was
the prisoner—there is no foot-path through the wood to Mr. White's—I did not strike the prisoner with the stick, till after he attempted to stab me with the knife, which was after Munns had let him go—he was attempting to stab Munns in the face.
WILLIAM MUNNS . I was in Furzefield-wood on the 4th of November with Gibbs—I saw a person, and in consequence of what Gibbs said, I ran after the person—he got out of the wood, but I did not lose sight of him—I overtook him—it was the prisoner—he had a stick in his hand—he struck at me—I put my arm up, and he knocked my hat off—I ran up to him, and got the stick out of his hand—I got behind him—he got a knife out of his pocket, and said, if I did not let go of him, he would run it into me—he struck at me six or seven times—I kept behind him, and got hold of his arm—Gibbs came up, and said, "Is it you, Philip?"—he said, "Yes," and then I let him go—he attempted to stab him, and I knocked him off—he said he would not be taken by anybody—he got out of the field over the hedge, took a brick and threw it at Gibbs—I saw Gibbs hit him on the arm in the field, and try to knock the knife out of his hand—after he got over the hedge, and threw the brick, I said to Gibbs, "It is not worth while to run the risk of our lives; you know him."
Cross-examined. Q. What time was this? A. About half-past five, or from that to six o'clock—the prisoner was about forty yards off when we first saw him—I could see at that distance what he was about—after I went up to him, Gibbs came up, and addressed him by his name—I let him go—he walked away, and we pursued him again—it was after we came up to him again that Gibbs struck him on the arm—he had attempted to stab me and Gibbs—he walked away a little, and Gibbs said he should go to Mimmscage—he said he would not be taken by anybody—Gibbs did not strike him before he had attempted to stab him—Gibbs held his stick up before him, he then made a stab at him, and then Gibbs hit him with the stick.
MR. HOWORTH. Q. Did he still keep the knife in his hand? A. Yes, and kept threatening us, as I have stated.
CARTER FAREBROTHER . I am land-steward to the Marquis of Salisbury—Furzefield-wood belongs to the Marquis. Gibbs is employed as gamekeeper of that manor—I gave him directions to look to the underwood, and see that no damage was done, that no person cut any or took it away, and to detect a person who was doing so—I authorised him to detect any person whom he might find there—I had authority from his lordship to do so—that manor is under my control.
Cross-examined. Q. This wood is in the occupation of the Marquis of Salisbury? A. It is—Mr. White is the tenant of the farm, but not of the wood—his land is on one side—a person coming from Mr. White's might come through the wood by trespass—there is no footpath—I gave Gibbs authority to watch the wood, in a verbal manner and in writing.
STEPHEN GIBBS re-examined. Q. Did you receive an authority in writing from Mr. Farebrother? A. Yes—I really do not know what has become of it—I searched for it, and could not find it.
Cross-examined. Q. How long is it since you received that written authority? A. I think it must be twelve months ago—I should think it has been lost these six weeks—I had an authority when this took place—it was lying on the mantel-piece then, but it got knocked down by the children, and got burnt—I did not see it burnt—the woman where I lodge said so—I did not expect it would be asked for—I searched for it a day or two after the case was before the Magistrate.
MR. HOWORTH. Q. You were asked for it? A. Yes—I went home and asked mistress for it, and could not find it.
GUILTY on the second Count. Aged 29.— Confined Six Month.
[trialAccount t18430130-775]
775. HANNAH PEARTON was indicted for obtaining goods by false pretences.
JAMES OSBORNE . I am shopman to John Fox Kemp, a grocer, at Uxbridge. I did not know the prisoner till she came on the 31st of Jan., about one o'clock, and said she wanted 1/2lb. of black tea and 1/4lb. of green tea, 2lbs. of butter, 61bs. of sugar, half a bar of soap, and 1/2lb. of tobacco, for Mr. Stilwell—I asked if it was for Mr. Arthur Stilwell—the said it was for Mr. Arthur Stilwell, of Moorcroft-house—I knew him, and then asked if she had brought the book—she said she had not, but she requested a bill, which I made out—she took the goods, went away, and left the bill on the counter—after she was gone I had some suspicion—I sent Gutteridge after her.
WILLIAM GUTTERIDGE . I am shopman to Mr. Kemp. I was sent after the prisoner—I had seen Osborne serve her—she is the person—I went to Mr. Stilwell's, and then after the prisoner—I knew where she lived—I knocked, and she came to the door—I asked her if she had been to Uxbridge—she denied having been there at all—I went to the house again with the officer—I asked her if she had been to Uxbridge—she said she had—her father asked her where the goods were—she brought them down stairs, and I gave her in charge.
GEORGE GODFREY . I am a constable. I went with Gutteridge to the prisoner's—Gutteridge asked if she had been to Uxbridge—the said, "Yes"—he asked if she had been, and got these goods by false pretence—the said, "Yes"—we asked her where they were—she said, up stairs in her bed-room, and she brought them down, and they were delivered to me.
JAMES OSBORNE re-examined. These goods are my master's.
ARTHUR STILWELL . I live at Moorcroft-house, Uxbridge. The prisoner was in our service six years ago—I did not send her for these article—they were not sent for for my house at all.
Prisoner. I am very sorry; I did it through distress.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Two Months.

777. BENJAMIN HAYCOCK was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of Feb., 1 pair of boots, value 11 s., the goods of Stephen Woodcock.
STEPHEN WOODCOCK . I keep a shoe-shop on Great Saffron-hill. On the evening of the 2nd of Feb., the prisoner came with three other persons, one of whom was named Simpson, who came for a pair of shoes he had left to be mended—I attended to him, and after that the prisoner asked me to measure him for shoes—I measured him, and then one of the others—the prisoner then left the shop, and my daughter told me something—I had missed a pair of boots from my door—I went, and found the prisoner in the White Horse—I accused him of stealing a pair of boots—he denied it—I said I would fetch my daughter—he then slipped off his jacket, and put it on the settle—I gave charge of him—I afterwards saw the boots in the officer's hand.
CHARLOTTE ELIZABETH WOODCOCK . I am six years of age. I know the necessity of speaking the truth—I remember the prisoner coming to my father's, while my father was measuring Spriggins—the prisoner had these boots in his
hand, which were hanging inside the door—I did not see what he did with them—he left the shop—I saw him put them under his jacket.
JOHN MARTIN . I live next door to the White Horse. I was in the yard between six and seven o'clock in the evening, on the 2nd of Feb.—I found this pair of boots on the wall.
CHARLES ELLIOTT ( police-constable G 139.) I saw the boots in the possession of Martin—I took the prisoner into custody.
NOT GUILTY .
NEW COURT.— Tuesday, February 7 th, 1843.

[trialAccount t18430130-778]
778. THOMAS WHITEHEAD was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of Jan., 2 trusses of hay, value 5 s., the goods of John Miller, his master; and BENJAMIN SPICER , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN PARSFIELD . I live in Church-street, Lisson-grove, next door to Mr. Miller, who deals in corn and hay—Whitehead has been in his employ for the last month—on Wednesday evening, the 18th of Jan., I saw him take a truss of clover hay out of Mr. Miller's barn, and put it down against Spicer's door, who lives in Duke-mews, next door but one to Miller's stable—Spicer was standing at his door at the time—I made my way towards them—they appeared to be in conversation—I said to Whitehead, " Tom, I think I shall go and get a pint of beer"—Whitehead then made his way from Spicer's, and I could neither see the clover, nor Spicer—I did not go to get any beer, but went and stood at the corner—I then saw Whitehead come again out of the barn with a truss of bent hay on his shoulder, which he took to Spicer's door—I then saw Spicer bring the truss of bent hay from the door up into Earl-street—I saw Spicer's man with the truss of clover hay on his back—I went to Mr. Miller's shopman, and told him what I had seen.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Did you see Spicer at the door? A. No, I do not know where he was then—I was standing at the corner of Duke's-mews, and in about five or six minutes I saw Spicer with the bent hay on his back—he came from the door with it—I did not see him take it from the door—I did riot see him take it up—I had seen Spicer's man with a truss before that.
MR. PAYNE. Q. How near was Spicer to his own door? A. About five or six yards off.
THOMAS HENRY THOMPSON ( police-sergeant D 4.) On Saturday night, the 21st of Jan., I went to Mr. Miller's counting-house, in consequence of information—Whitehead came into the counting-house while I was there—I said I was going to take him into custody—he asked what for—I said, "For stealing a truss of clover, and a truss of bent hay"—he said, "Why you astonish me"—he asked when—I said, "On Wednesday night"—he then said, "I know nothing about it"—I said he need not say any thing, I had a witness who saw him take two trusses from his master's to Spicer's door—he then said, "I will tell you the truth; I did take them to Spicer's, and Spicer received them"—on the road to the station, he said Spicer had been round to him that evening, and asked if he could have four or five more trusses, and he (Whitehead) told him he could not let him have them, but Spicer was in the habit of having a good deal of loose hay—I took him to the station, and
he returned to No. 2, Duke's-mews, where Spicer lives—I went up stairs, and found him in bed—I told him to get up, Mr. Miller wanted him—he asked his wife if she had paid for the oats and chaff that night—I said it was not about the oats and chaff that I took him, but on suspicion of receiving a truss of clover, and a truss of bent hay—he said he had bad no hay, and did not know Mr. Miller's man—I said a witness saw him, and saw the hay come to his door, that Whitehead said he had taken the hay to him, and he had received it—he said he did not, he never had any hay of him—I said Whitehead had told me that he had been round that evening to know if he would let him have four or five more trusses—he said he had not been to Whitehead, and had not seen him—I took him to the station, and the two prisoners were there together—the inspector asked him, and Spicer said he knew nothing about it—Whitehead said something which I did not hear—Spicer then said, "Is that them? I will pay Mr. Miller for them if he will go next door, the money lies there"—I was present when the depositions were taken—that is Mr. Rawlinson's signature to this deposition—they were all signed in my presence—( read)—"The prisoner Spicer says, 'I sever received them, and have not had a truss of hay in my place for three weeks.' J. RAWLINSON."
JOHN MILLER . I am a corn-dealer, and live in Lisson-grove North. Whitehead was a chaff-cutter, and helper in my service—he had no authority to take any clover hay, or bent hay, without my order, or that of my shopman.
Cross-examined. Q. Is this where your business is carried on? A. Yes, in this stable, at the back of my house.
CHARLES WARD . I am shopman to the prosecutor. I did not authorise Whitehead to take these trusses of hay.
Whitehead's Defence. The keys of the warehouse are left with me ill day, and when the carters come at any time I serve then; sometimes I get an order, and sometimes not.
WHITEHEAD — GUILTY . Aged 40,— Confined Six Months.
SPICER — GUILTY . Aged 41.— Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18430130-779]
779. WILLIAM JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of Jan., 20 handkerchiefs, value 1 l. 10 s., the goods of Scope Fowler.—2nd Count, stating it to be 8 yards of silk; and that he been before convicted of felony.
ROBERT MILLER . I am in the employ of Mr. Scope Fowler. On the 27th of Jan., about nine o'clock in the evening, the prisoner came into his shop—I showed him some shirt-collars; he bought one and paid for it—I had some silk handerchiefs there—I saw him take them, and put them under a coat which he had on hts arm—he went out—I ran and saw him—I lost sight of him—I then got sight of him again, and then he had neither count nor handkerchiefs—he had an opportunity of parting with them—there were twenty of them.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Had you seen him before? A. Yes, I had seen him about the street—I followed him to Regent-street—the turning which leads to Regent-street is three or four yards from my master's shop—I followed him directly—he had got to the corner of Maddox-street—he was standing at the corner these, and I went to him—I can swear to him.
JOHN EDWARD RICHARDS . I was going up Regent-street, and saw the prisoner with a coat on his arm, and something red underneath it.
GEORGE TREW ( police-constable H 125.) I produce a certificate of the
prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—he is the person who was convicted by the name of William Trott.
GUILTY . Aged 29.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18430130-780]
780. MARY FORD was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of Jan., 25 yards of Orleans cloth, value 12 s. 6 d., the goods of Henry Lee.
JOHN WITTLE . I am shopman to Mr. Henry Lee, a linendraper, in Leather-lane. About five o'clock in the evening, on the 20th of Jan., I missed 25 yards of Orleans cloth from inside the door—I went, and saw the prisoner standing outside, near the door—I gave her into custody.
JOSEPH BAILEY . I live in Upper North-place, Gray's-inn-road. About five o'clock that evening I saw the prisoner and another woman standing at the corner of the prosecutor's shop, at the corner of Baldwin's-gardens—the prisoner took the cloth from the right-hand side of the door, and gave it to the other woman—I am sure the prisoner is the woman that took it—I went into the shop, and the other woman was gone.
Prisoner. Q. Did you touch me on the back? A. No.
Prisoner's Defence. He touched me on the back, and said, "I saw you take something, and give it to that woman, who is gone across the road;" I said, "If you saw a woman go across the road, why did you not stop her?"
GUILTY . * Aged 24.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18430130-781]
781. JAMES JONES and WILLIAM KEEFE were indicted for stealing, on the 25th of Jan., 12 cigars, value 3 s., the goods of William Henry Johnson: and ALFRED ARNOLD , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
WILLIAM HENRY JOHNSON . I am a tobacconist, and live in Oxford-street. About ten o'clock on Wednesday morning, the 25th of Jan., Jones and Keefe came into my shop—Jones drew open the lid of a box on the counter, and took out twelve cigars, which he passed over to Keefe—Keefe put them into his coat, and ran away—I took Jones into custody—they had not asked for any cigars—when I went into the shop they asked for a pennyworth of tobacco—I am sure I saw Jones pass them over to Keefe.
GEORGE BECK . On Wednesday morning, the 25th of Jan., I was in a coffee-shop in Bell-street. Arnold was there—Keefe came in, and said, "Do you want to buy some cigars?"—Arnold said, "Let me look; I will buy them, as many as you have got"—Keefe took them out of his pocket, and said, "Here they are then"—Arnold took them into the parlour, and brought him out some halfpence, about 5 d. I think there was—he said they were a halfpenny a-piece.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You take a cigar at a coffee-shop, like other young gentlemen? A. Not unless I buy it—I do not buy them in a coffee-shop at all—I am fifteen years old—I pay 1 d. a piece for them—I do not get them at three for 2 d.—I have been here before about two old pairs of shoes—two boys got me into it—I have never been in custody but then—I was in the coffee-shop having a drop of coffee—I help my father at printing—it was a quarter past ten o'clock when I was at the coffee-shop—I saw Keefe and a boy named Nickaby there—I do not know what has become of Nickaby—the officer did not speak to me about this—he went to my father, and acquainted him that I was in bad company—that was on Wednesday night—he took me home to my father, and asked me where Keefe was—that was the first time I said anything about this—I remained in the coffee-shop about an hour that morning before I went home to my father—I dined with him, but said nothing about this.
JOHN GUYATT . I am an errand-boy. I met Hall, the constable, about
six o'clock, on that Wednesday evening—he asked me about Keefe—I went to the coffee-shop, where Arnold is waiter, and Keefe was sitting at the table—I went and told Hall that Keefe was there—I then went back into the house—as I was going in, Arnold came out, and met the constable, who Cold him he wanted Keefe—Arnold then came in, and told Keefe that Hall wanted him, and he was to go out at the back door and scale the wall.
Cross-examined. Q. How long had you been in the coffee-shop? A. I only went in to see if Keefe was there—I am not acquainted with the constable, but he knew me by sight—he met me in the street that day—I go to Colonel Berners' to clean knives and shoes.
JOHN HALL ( police-constable D 64.) From information I received, I went on Thursday night to a lodging-house, and found Keefe in bed—I said I wanted him, for stealing some cigars in Oxford-street—he said he knew nothing about it—on the way to the station, he said he had met Jones in Oxford-street, at Mr. Johnson's shop; that he went in to buy a penny-worth of tooacco, then jumped up behind a carriage, and went away—I met Guyatt, and asked if he knew Keefe—he went into the coffee-shop, and said he was there.
ROBERT JACKSON ( police-sergeant D 25.) I took Arnold, for receiving cigars from Keefe—he said he had not received them—on the way to the station, he said he had received two eigars of Keefe, which he had smoked.
Cross-examined. Q. When was that? A. He did not mention the time—I told him, of course, the circumstances.
Keefe's Defence. I was coming along the street, and saw Jones coming out of the shop; he spoke to another boy; Jones said to me, "Stop, I am going home;" there was a carriage going by; I got on it, and rode away, as I wanted to make haste.
MARY ANN HARDIE . My husband keeps this coffee-shop—we do not dwell there—we dwell at No. 22, Winchester-street. I was in the coffee-shop on that Wednesday morning about seven o'clock—we have a parlour there, divided from the shop by a partition and door, with a window in the door and partition—I was in the parlour—I remember on that occasion some boys being there—Arnold was in the parlour—he went into the shop when the boys came in, to take the order—he came back to me in a few minutes—he had not taken any money then—I had seen no transaction between the boys and Arnold—if there had been I must have heard it.
COURT. Q. Are you related to Arnold? A. No, he is a friend of ours, we have known him some time—I swear he is not related to us—I have known him above twelve months, I dare say—he was in business, something in the straw line—we have kept this coffee-shop about two months—we live just by—it is a night-house, open all night—there are boys come there sometimes—my husband's name is Charles—the shop is in Bell-street, Paddington.
Re-examined. Q. Do you remember some boys coming into the shop between eight and nine o'clock, in the evening, at the time Hall the policeman came? A. There were some boys came that evening—it might be about that time—I remember the policeman coming—Arnold was in the parlour—a boy came in, and said to Keefe, I think it was, "Hall wants you"—Arnold was then gone outside—Keefe got up immediately, and ran through the side-door into the street—Arnold was outside, talking to Hall—Hall then came in, he searched for the boy, and said to Arnold, "You have let him go"—Arnold said, "I have not let him go"—on the Friday following, two policemen came, and called for Arnold—I did not hear what they said.
THOMAS HENRY STOVEL . I am a biscuit-baker, and live in Winchesterrow. On the 26th of Jan. I was at Hardie's coffee-shop, about eight o'clock in the evening—I went to take a cup of coffee—a boy came in, and
said to Keefe, "Hall is after you"—Keefe rose from his seat, and went from the shop into the parlour, opened the door, cut into the passage and out into the street, and escaped—Arnold was not in the shop.
COURT. Q. Do you attend this shop much? A. No, I have been in three or four times—it is a day and a night shop.
(Thomas Stevens, a boot-maker; and James Charlton, a watchmaker, in Lisson-grove, gave Arnold a good character.)
JONES — GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Three Months.
KEEFE *— GUILTY . Aged 16.
ARNOLD — GUILTY . Aged 32.
Transported for Seven Years.
Convict Ship.

788. NATHAN DANIELS was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of Jan., 4 1/2 lbs. weight of solder, value 3 s.; 1 metal cock, 5 d.; 5 lbs. weight of copper, 1 s.; 2 hammers, 6 d.; 1 chisel, 6 d.; 1 screwdriver, 6 d.; 500 nails, 5 d.; and 1 ornament, 1 d., the property of Asher Prior, his master.
JOHN MORE . About half-past five in the evening of 23rd of Jan., I wanted a pair of pliers, I went to the shop, and saw the prisoner with his tool-basket between his feet—I said, "I want a pair of pliers," and put my hand on his basket—he put his hand on it, and said, "They are not here"—I thought there was something wrong—I said, "Let us see what you have got here?"—I took the basket, and found these things in it—I said, "How do you account for this?"—he said, he did not know, be supposed some one must have put them into his basket—they belong to Mr. Asher Prior—this hammer was found at the prisoner's house afterwards—I swear these other things are Mr. Prior's.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Did you find the pliers there? A. Yes; he said he supposed some one must have put them in for a trap for him—this hammer has the mark of" A. Prior" on it—Mr. Prior is an ironmonger, and sells these articles—I swear to this packet of nails—there is Mr. Prior's son's writing on them, and we have never sold any to the prisoner—we have sold some—I found them in a box on the landing of the first floor—he was a lodger in the house—his wife said it was his box.
COURT. Q. What was found in the house? A. The hammer, screwdriver, and the chisel—I will swear they are Mr. Asher Prior's—I saw then safe about three months ago—the prisoner told an officer in my presence, that be lodged at No. 18, Claremont-place—I went there and found the box containing these articles—there were no other lodgers that come to my master's, that I am aware of.
TIMOTHY PRIOR . I have examined these articles, they are my brother's.
WILLIAM RANDALL PRIOR . These are not the tools I sold the prisoner eighteen months ago.
JOHN CAYFORD ( police-constable E 97.) I was called to take the prisoner—I went to his house and found the hammers, the chisel, screwdriver, and nails in a box, on the landing—the prisoner directed me to go to this house.
NOT GUILTY .

785. GEORGE BROOKS and GEORGE MAYS were indicted for stealing, on the 26th of Jan., 1 tea-pot, value 10 s.; the goods of Henry Jones.
HENRY JONES . I live in Rose-street, King-street, Covent-garden. About half-past six o'clock, on the 26th of Jan., I was standing at the door, and saw Brooks pass—I went into the counting-house, and heard a cry of "Stop thief"—I went and saw the prisoners in custody, with this tea-pot—I believe it to be mine.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Is there anything you know It by? A. I have missed such an one.
RICHARD JONES . I heard the cry of "Stop thief"—I ran out—the two persons passed me, from the staircase inside the house—I immediately followed—I lost sight of Mays and followed Brooks—I am sure he was one—when Brooks got into New-street he stumbled, and this tea-pot fell from his hand—I took it up and gave it to the policeman—I believe Mays to be the other man—he resembles him in every respect—he had a frock-coat on—I did not see his face.
SAMUEL SALDER . Between half-past six and seven o'clock, I was passing up New-street, and heard a cry of "Stop thief"—in a few minutes, the prisoners brushed past me—I stopped Mays—Brooks stumbled, and a blue bag fell from him—nobody but them were running—they were hatf-a-dozen yards from the prosecutor's house.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. A clerk out of employ. I lodge in Dorset-street, Salisbury-square. I have been out of employ three months—my last place was with a hosier, in Brewer-street—I lived there four years—I left because I had a quarrel, not on a charge of dishonesty—I do not recollect exactly what it was.
(The prisoners received a good character.)
MAYS — GUILTY . Aged 19. BROOKS — GUILTY . Aged 19.
Confined Six Months.

786. MARY BURNS was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of Jan., 1 ring, value 10 s.; 1 buckle, 4 s.; 1 brooch, 7 s.; and 1 waistband, 1 s.; the goods of James Dowley: and MARGARET BURNS , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
CAROLINE DOWLEY . I live with my father, James Dowley. The prisoner Mary was in the habit of coming to clean my house—I saw these articles safe on the 6th of Jan. and missed them on the 17th—these are them—I can swear to them—I charged Mary with taking them—she said she never saw them, nor pawned them—Margaret never came to the house.
JOHN WILLIAM FORELAND . I am a pawnbroker. Margaret brought this ring to me, and asked if it was gold—I said it was—she asked if I would buy it—I said, "No"—I said the watchmaker's was the best place—Mary and another girl afterwards came and asked whether they were gold—I said I saw
them a night or two ago—she said, "My mother brought them"—the ring is worth 1 s. 6 d.
CATHERINE GREEN . I searched Margaret and found this waisband, brooch, and buckle in her bosom—I found nothing on Mary.
Mary's Defence. I was sweeping the floor; they were in the dust; I thought they were of no use.
Margaret's Defence. She brought them home; I said, "Where did you get them"—she said she picked them up—they were in the house a week.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430130-787]
787. JOHN BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of Jan., 1 handkerchief, value 2 s., the goods of Thomas Hitchcock, from his person.
THOMAS HITCHCOCK . I live in Frith-street, Soho. About three o'clock on the 31st of Jan., I was in Titchfield-street, and had occasion to stop at a dead wall—I felt a twitch at my pocket—I turned my head and saw the prisoner with my handkerchief in his hand-he started off—I followed him, crying, "Stop thief"—he was stopped sixty or seventy yards up the street, and he threw the handkerchief down—I am sure he is the person-there are no marks on it—I had it not a minute before—it corresponds with mine in every respect.
Prisoner. Q. Did I have it in my hand? A. Yes, in your hand.
Prisoner. I was coming to Crown-street with a message; I was past the time, ran fast, and struck the gentleman with my left hand; the handkerchief was never found on me; I never touched it; there was nobody in the street, but there were two lads who followed the prosecutor.
THOMAS HITCHCOCK . No such thing, there was no one in the street.
GUILTY . * Aged 18.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18430130-788]
788. JAMES PILCHER was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
ALEXANDER ARCHIBALD . I am an accountant, residing in Southampton-row Edgeware-road, Marylebone. The prisoner was one of my clerks for twelve months—it was his duty to collect debts for various persons who employed me-it was his duty to enter what he received in the cash-book when he returned, and to account to me every evening-if he did not come Home that night, then the next morning-I engaged him to collect some money from Mr. Cripps, a staymaker, in Hanway-street—this is the book in which he made his entries—if he received 30 s. on the 31st of May from Mr. Cripps, he has only accounted to me for 1 l. he has not accounted to me for 1 l. 10 s. received from Mr. Cripps on the 6th of June—he has only accounted for 1 l., and on the 28th of July, only for 10 s.—in consequence of something that occurred on the 24th of Dec., I gave the prisoner into custody—I brought the policeman into my office, and said, "I give this man in charge for embezzlemen"—he said, "Give me a littele time, and I will settle it"—I said, "No, Mr. Pilcher, I have told you of this before; this is the third time, and nobody will employ me if I do not punish you"—he said, "For the sake of my family, will you not arrange it?"—I said, "It does not admit of arrangement; I cannot do it."
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. There were some terms entered into with you and the prisoner, at the time you engaged him? A. he deposited 30 l. in my hands—there was a writing signed—that sum was paid as security for any deficiency there might be in his accounts—this paper is drawn up by the prisoner, and signed by himself—( read)—"Mr. James Pilcher agrees to serve Mr. Archilbld as his clerk, and make himself generally useful, for three years, at 52 l. per annum, paid weekly; hours of attendance, from
nine o'clock in the morning till eight in the evening; reasonable time to be allowed for meals. Mr. Pitcher's executors and guardians place in the hands of Mr. Archibald's banker, the sum of 30 l., for any default in the accounts by Mr. Pilcher, or for penalty, should he be engaged by other persons, and the same sum of 30 l., and interest, to be returned to Mr. Pilcher, provided his accounts are correct, otherwise such part of the 30 l. to be appropriated in liquidation of the same. Mr. Pilcher to be allowed a commission on all busi-ness he may bring to Mr. Archibald."
Q. Was that 30 l. paid in? A. Yes, and I have it still at my banker's—I have never paid the interest, it has never been settled about that—the 30 l. if paid in at my banker's, as my money—I do not know whether all these entries in this book are in the prisoner's handwriting—whatever cash he received himself he entered, and what other clerks paid him over he entered—it may be that some of the others have entered it, but he was the cashier—on 31st of May, 6th of June, and 28th of July—he had ten or twelve people in a day to call on to receive money—they were all small sums—they were entered every day—they were never in arrears beyond the next morning—I have lived at my present residence about five years—I have been an accountant for twenty years—I was a man of property before—when I first came to London I commenced as commission-agent—I was a blue-manufacturer—I was a bankrupt ten years ago, as a coal-merchant and bill-broker—I got my certificate six or seven years after—I have not been in any bankruptcy or insolvency since—I know nothing of the prisoner's family or connexion—he told me he had a wife—he has never asked me to go over the accounts with him, and settle them—I had a suspicion about Miss Cripps about three weeks before I took the prisoner into custody—I began to suspect him, and then I examined my books, and found he had altered them, and been carrying on a systematic system of robbery—I was horror-struck, because I had treated the man more like a brother than a clerk—I then went round to the different parties, and took a copy of their receipts—I became acquainted with this account of Miss Cripps about two days before the 24th of Dec.—I went down to Miss Cripps, and took a copy of the accounts—I had occasion to be in the City on Christmas-eve, and had discovered other things of the prisoner, and I thought it right to arrest him—he had asked me in the morning, before I went out, if he could get away on the Monday, as he was going boxing—when I returned I brought an officer, and gave him into custody—I went before Mr. Rawlinson, the Magistrate, about this affair of Miss Cripps, on the 26th of Dec, and then I begged a remand for a week, that I might get ray evidence up—he was remanded, and at the end of that week the Magistrate asked if I had any other parties—I said, "Yes, a great many," and he said he had better remand him—I produced seven or eight of these parties, but only two of them were examined, (Mr. Devaux of Great Distaff-lane, and Miss Cripps)—the Magistrate did not tell me, when he had heard all my accusation, that it was a matter of account—he said, "Could you not arrange it?"—the Magistrate was not certain what it war—the prisoner's wife came to see me on this business.
NOT GUILTY .
(There were two other indictments against the prisoner, on which Mr. Doane offered no evidence.)

789. EDWARD LEWIS was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of Nov., 1 plane, value 5 s., the goods of Ireland Thurgood.
(The prisoner being deaf and dumb, had the evidence communicated to him by signs.)
MR. CROUCH conducted the Prosecution.
ANN THURGOOD . I am the wife of William Ireland Thurgood—we live in Brick-lane, St. Luke's. On the 2nd of Nov. my husband was at work two streets off, and the prisoner came to my house, as if from my husband, to get a plane—he went outside the door, and beckoned to the street where he saw my husband, and made a motion of a plane on the side of the door—he pointed to the shop where the tools were kept—I understood by his motions that he wanted a plane—I asked him if he came from my husband, and he made signs that he had—I gave him the plane, believing he had come from my husband—he went the road towards where my husband was, but he ran up a contrary street—I ran after him, but he got out of my sight—the plane has not been found—I never saw the prisoner again till the 23rd of Jan.
COURT. Q. May you not be mistaken as to his coming from your husband? A. No—he went outside the street-door, and beckoned to the street.
WILLIAM IRELAND THURGOOD . I did not send the prisoner for this plane—I knew him—he never went on errands for me—I was on a scaffolding—he passed, I nodded to him and he to me.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430130-790]
790. PATRICK COCHRANE and WILLIAM MERCER were indicted for stealing, on the 27th of Jan., 18 cigars, value 1 s., the goods of Samuel Stephen Newland; and that Mercer had been before convicted of felony.
HARRIET NEWLAND . I am the wife of Samuel Stephen Newland, of Nas-sau-street, Commercial-road. I was in my back-shop on the 26th of Jan., and saw Mercer come in—I went in to serve him—when I got outside my room-door he went out—I went to my counter, and saw the lid off my cigars, and there was only one left—there had been from a dozen and a half to two dozen—I went to my door, and saw Mercer outside, and Cochrane stood next to the next neighbour's—I said, "You thieving vagabonds, you have got my cigars"—they made a run, and there was no person near—the first person I saw was my husband—I will swear the prisoners are the two persons I saw, and they were shuffling their hands, as if they were parting something.
JOSEPH HARROW . I live in Grenville-street, Somers-town. I was going to my sister's, and saw the two prisoners running down James-street, one after the other, and no one but them—that is the first turning past the prosecutor's—my attention was directed to them by hearing one of them say, "Go on, go on"—I crossed and stopped them both—Mercer said, "What do you stop me for? I was crying, Stop thief"—I went to the spot where it appeared there had been a separation, and I found one cigar on the pavement.
GEORGE HAYES ( police-constable K 54.) I took a lantern, and found fifteen cigars in the middle of the road, as if they had been thrown from a hat or cap.
WILLIAM WISEMAN ( police-constable K 156.) I took Mercer—on our way be said, "I only took two or three;" and in reply to an observation of Cochrane, he said, "I suppose we shall have six months"—I have seen the prisoners together before.
Cochrane. I had nothing to do with the cigars.
Mercer. I was not in her shop.
THOMAS BARTLETT ( police-constable K 286.) I produce a certificate of Mercer's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—he is the person
COCHRANE — GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Six Months.
MERCER — GUILTY . Aged 17.— Transported for Seven Years.—Convict Ship.

793. EDWARD MAYNARD was indicted for obtaining goods by false pretences.
DANIEL EDDS . I am a timber-merchant. On the 18th of Jan., about half-past three o'clock in the afternoon, the prisoner, who was a stranger, came and said he wanted to purchase some timber for his master—he said his master's name was Bardon, in the Mile-end-road, near Bow, and he wanted some stocks and other things for his master—I let him have forty coach-stocks, believing he was employed by Mr. Bardon, or I should not have let him had them—I sent my carman with the stocks, and he could not find a Mr. Bardon.

CHARLES WALKER . I took the stocks, and could not find the person—I was bringing them home—I met the prisoner at the turnpike-gate—he took me down Dog-row, and unloaded them at a little wheeler's shop—that is near Mile-end-road—I have not been there since for the money.
JOHN FRENCH . I am a wheelwright. The prisoner asked my permission to leave these stocks at my shop—I said he might if it was not for long—they remained about half an hour, and then they were taken away—I know no such person as Mr. Bardon.
GEORGE HARLEY . I bought the stocks of another man, who was in company pany with the prisoner, and he gave the receipt.
Prisoner. It was Mr. Bardon who sold them to this witness—Mr. Bardon and I went and looked out some goods—Mr. Edds asked his name and address, and he gave it him; "Mile-end, near Bow," and he gave me 1 s. for looking out the goods.
MR. EDDS. No, I had no note or address given me.

798. JAMES YOUNG was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of Jan., 2 coppers, value 1 l. 13 s., the goods of William Burtt Wilton.
WILLIAM BURRT WILTON . I am a plumber, and live in High-street, Deptford. On the 23rd of Jan. my servant placed four coppers outside my window; two of them were taken—I have examined these two, and have no doubt whatever that they are mine.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What do you know them by? A. By a chalk mark which I placed on them—here is the mark on one of them now—I was at home all day.
THOMAS COOK . About six or seven this evening I was in King-street, Deptford—I saw a man go by with two coppers on his head—he was about the size of the prisoner—he had corderoy trowsers on, a black waistcoat, and fustian sleeves to his jacket—I did not see his face.
JOHN BREENAN ( police-constable R 67.) About half-past five on the 23rd of Jan. I was on duty—I saw a man resembling the prisoner with a copper on his head—I followed him down Old King-street—in coming near to Fishing Smack-alley I lost sight of him all at once—I returned and met the prosecutor, who said that two coppers were stolen from outside his shop window—about half an hour after the prisoner came out of that alley and down King-street—I took him into custody, and told him I suspected him to be the man that stole two coppers from High-street—he said it was not him—I asked his name—he said Williams, and he lived in London—I went to his house, and on Tuesday the 24th found these two coppers in the cellar under the apartment where he lived.
Cross-examined. Q. You never saw him there? A. No, the house is in Old King-street, Deptford—there was no number.
BENJAMIN LOVELL ( police-sergeant K 15.) The prisoner lived in Old King-street, Deptford—a lodging-house adjoins his.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you ever see him in the house? A. Yes, many times—he lived in the front-room, down stairs—a person could get from the adjoining house to where he lived—I saw some footsteps from a piece of waste ground at the back, which leads from Fishing Smack-alley—there were other persons living in the house—his wife bad been confined nearly a month.
COURT. Q. He had this whole house? A. Yes—he used to let it out—no one lives in the cellar—there is a parcel of lumber in it—Fishing Smack-alley is about 100 yards from his house—there were traces from that alley to his premises, and over his fence on the top of the pig-sty.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430130-799]
799. RICHARD TAYLOR was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of Jan., 1 handkerchief, value 4 s. 6 d., the goods of Caroline Eliza Gill.
CAROLINE ELIZA GILL . I am an unfortunate girl. The prisoner came to lodge with me on Sunday night, the 8th of Jan., and left between seven and eight o'clock next morning—I missed a silk handkerchief from a chair at the foot of the bed—this is it, I am quite sure—he was quite sober—he paid me—I am sure he is the man—he was with me from half-past eleven till a quarter before eight—this handkerchief was bought and made a present to me on the New Year's-day.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. You met him in the street? A.
No—I was standing at my lodging-house—I got out of bed just after he left, and missed my handkerchief—I hemmed one side of it myself.
JOHN SPARKS LOVICK . I am a pawnbroker. I produce this handkerchief—it was pawned by the prisoner, to the best of my knowledge, about eight o'clock in the morning of the 9th of Jan., for 2 s.—I am positive he is the man.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 18.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined One Month.

804. WILLIAM SMART was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of Oct., 1 hammer, value 2 s., the goods of William Hiskins.
WILLIAM SMART . I am in the employ of Mr. Graham, a builder. On the 15th of Oct. I missed my hammer—this is it—it has my mark upon it.
Prisoner. Q. Where did you leave it? A. On Mr. Stone's wharf.
GEORGE HARRIS ( police-constable R 158.) I searched the prisoner's box on another charge, and found this hammer.
Prisoner. I found it in Mr. Graham's yard—it was taken into my house, and thrown into a basket with some old tools—no one called for it.
WILLIAM HISKINS re-examined. I left it at Mr. Stone's yard at night, and when I came back next morning it was missing from the rent of the tools—they were covered over with an apron—the prisoner was carting bricks to these premises—it is a private wharf, locked up—other persons might have had access to the wharf.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430130-805]
805. WILLIAM SMART was again indicted for stealing, on the 19th of Jan., 56lbs. weight of hay, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of George Hall Graham; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
JAMES MOULTON . I am in the employ of Mr. George Hall Graham, a builder, at Woolwich. I watched his yard on Thursday night, the 19th of Jan.—I placed myself in the corner of the yard, and bad put some paper into a truss of hay in the yard—on that evening I missed a truss of hay from the yard—I communicated the circumstance to my master—the constable brought me some hay on the 22nd, and showed me a paper, which was what I had placed in the hay—it has my name on it.
GEORGE HARRIS . I found this paper in the manger of the stable where the prisoner's horses stand—there was a little hay in the rack—I compared it with Mr. Graham's hay, and, as far as I could judge, it corresponded.
Prisoner. I purchased the truss of hay at Mr. Sears'. Witness. He did not tell me that at the time—his stable is near Mr. Graham's.
Prisoner. There is another horse in the stable, and how that paper came there I do not know.
OBADIAH BELTON . I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY .—Aged 63.
[trialAccount t18430130-806]
806. WILLIAM SMART was again indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of Jan., 56lbs. weight of hay, value 2 s. 6 d., the good of George Hall Graham.
JAMES MOULTON . On the 22nd of Jan. I saw the prisoner take this truus of hay away, and throw it over the fence—he then took it into his stable—I got an officer, and took the truss of hay down, and found in it these two tickets, which I had put there.
JOHN WADE . I am a hay-binder. I found this truss—I believe it to be Mr. Graham's property.
GUILTY . Aged 63.— Transported for Seven Years.

807. JOHN SAWYER was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of Jan., 1 pair of shoes, value 2 s. 3 d., the goods of Joseph Mead.
JOSEPH MEAD . I am a shoemaker, and live in Trafalgar-road, Greenwich. The prisoner was my journeyman—in consequence of information, I went and called the prisoner back from across the road, and accused him of having some of my property—he said he had not—I said I had lost a great deal—my wife came out, and said it was no use his denying it, she saw them in his pocket—he pulled these shoes out of his pocket—he had no business at all with them—they are mine.
GUILTY . * Aged 28.— Confined Six Months.

811. WILLIAM TAYLOR, JOSEPH WILLEY, JOHN RYAN , and JOHN DONOGHUE , were indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Phillip Penn, on the 23rd of Jan., at St. George the Martyr, Southwark, and stealing therein, 1 looking-glass and frame, value 2 l. 2 s.; 1 counterpane, 10 s.; 3 spoons, 12 s.; 4 shirts, 12 s.; 3 pairs of stays, 10 s.; 1 cloak, 15 s.; 2 necklaces, 1 l. 2 s.; 5 petticoats, 5 s.; 2 sheets, 16 s.; 3 frocks, 8 s.; 3 bed-gowns, 3 s.; 3 shifts, 4 s.; 1 yard of flannel, 1 s.; 2 pairs of drawers, 1 s.; 3 aprons, 1 s.; 3 pinafores, 1 s.; 6 handkerchiefs, 2 s.; 1 pair of trowsers, 2 s. 6 d.; 3 caps, 1 s.; 1 towel, 3 d.; 1 waistcoat, 6 d.; and 1 pair of stockings, 9 d.; his goods.
WILLIAM KELLY . I am a hatter. On Monday erening, the 23rd of Jan., I went to Mr. Penn's—he requested me to attend to the parties in his tap-room—I was at the bar, turned round, and saw Taylor going out with a keg under his arm—I took it from him, and gave it to Penn—about a quarter of an hour after I went into the tap-room, with a pot of beer, to a person named Sculley, the prisoner Willey and a man named Vickery were there—Willey called me on one side to the fire—he was in company with one of the sweeps—(Taylor and Ryan are sweeps)—I cannot say which, and a man named Vickery—he said they were going up stairs to do some business, and if I saw any thing I would not say any thing—it was spoken to frighten me, and I seemed to take no notice, being fearful of the manner the words were expressed to me—I was fearful to tell them what my determination was—I told them it was nothing to do with me what business they were going to do up stairs, and as soon as I had an opportunity I went out from the tap-room to the bar—Mr. Penn being engaged at the bar with parties, I went into the kitchen immediately, and there told the servant-girl.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Where do you carry on business as a hatter? A. Nowhere at present—I was apprentice and journeyman to Mr. Anderson, in Edward-street—I get my living by my wife's work—I have not been in the police force within these eighteen months—I resigned—I was fearful of getting dismissed—I got quarrelling and fighting—I should have had an order to quit—I had been in the force from February till August—the conversation between me and Willey took place in the tap-room—I cannot exactly say whether there were twelve persons there—I do not suppose there were fifteen—I should say about ten—there might be more or less—I did not tell any one of those ten people what Willey said to me—I was at tht Duke's Head the following morning—I did not see Willey there—I did not know of his being there—I did not see him there the following evening, nor till I saw him at Union-hall—I did not know of his being there.
PHILIP PENN . I am a publican, and keep the Duke's Head, Mint-street, Borough, in the parish of St. George the Martyr, Southwark. On Monday, the 23rd of Jan., about seven o'clock in the evening, the servant came up to me—I saw her go up stairs—she came down, and fainted away—I went up and saw the bed-room stripped, the bed-clothes stripped off the bed, and
the window open—I came down into my own bar again—soon afterwards some boys came in—I asked a man named Vickery to come up stairs, and bring a light, which he did—I went into the bed-room, got out of the window on to the leads, and saw the looking-glass there—Vickery and I came down, and sent for a policeman—I was sent for up Harrow-street, where the things were found—as I was going up the street, Donoghue came up to me and said, "Halloa, what is the matter?" or something similar to that; "I can tell you where the things are; what will you stand?"—I do not recollect saying anything to him before that, to my knowledge—I cannot say who spoke first—I had not spoken to him before he said, "Halloa, what is the matter?"—I was running up the street—I told him I had been robbed, and he said, "I think I can tell you where the things are that were taken out of the house".—(I did not say what I had been robbed of)—I told him I would give him a glass of anything to drink if he did—we went up to the van, at the top of the street—I got up, looked into the van, and saw the things exactly in the place he said they were—I saw linen sheets and shirts there that I had just missed from the bed-room—I told the man with the van to take care of the things, and bring them back to my house—I went back with Donoghue, and he had a glass of spirits—one of the boys that came in said Donoghue was throwing the clothes up into the van, and I gave him into custody on that charge.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you known Donoghue? A. Yes, twelve months—I should say he was very fresh indeed—what I gave him did not hurt him—I will not swear what he took—it was seven o'clock or half-past—it was dark out of doors.
GRACE PENN . I am the prosecutor's wife. I left home at a quarter-past three o'clock—I left the bed-room door and the door on the stairs locked, and my keys hanging in the bar—when I came home, about twenty minutes or half-past seven o'clock, the house was in confusion—I found the staircasedoor unlocked, not broken open, and the bed-room door unlocked too—it had not been forced—the property was all brought down into the kitchen—there was dirty and clean linen, a quantity of children's things, some ribbon, and other things in the looking-glass drawer—I believe the value altogether is 9 l. or 10 l.—when I left the bed-room the sash-window was fastened by a bolt in the centre frame, and a brass screw in the end—it was secured tight—there was no pane broken or cracked—when I came back the whole of a pane was nearly out—a person could get at the screw in that way—the bolt was undone, and the screw drawn out—that was the way the persons got in—the house is in the parish of St. George the Martyr.
Cross-examined. Q. How do you know the parish? A. We pay rates, and that name is on all the papers—my husband, myself, three children, (the eldest nine years old,) and the servant, all live in the house—the key in the bar was exposed, but no one could reach it—the maid-servant does not come into the bar without her master tells her—I cannot say whether my husband was away from the bar—ours is a back bed-room—there is a gate half-way up the stairs—that was locked, and the bed-room too—the keys of both hung together—the other bed-room had not been disturbed—the key of that hung in the bar with the others.
SAMUEL WARD . I am fifteen years old, and am out of work; I was in work, about twelve months ago, at Mr. Bartlett's a grocer, in Cannon-street; my mother supports me. On the 23rd of January, between six and seven o'clock in the evening, I saw the prisoner Ryan chuck the things over the railing—another man caught them, and went towards a van with them, which was at the other end of the street—Ryan was on the top of Mr. Penn's leads,
throwing white things like linen over the railing—I went and told Mr. Penn—he went up stairs—I went out—I saw nothing more—I do not know who the other man was, his hack was towards me—I left Mr. Bartlett's service because I stopped away two or three days, and he discharged me.
WILLIAM CARLTON . I am fourteen years old, I work for my father as a bricklayer.
