I was standing on the opposite side of the way, and saw Ryan throw the things over the railing—I was not with Ward—I saw a man catch them, but did not see his face—I saw Ryan's face quite well by the blase of a farrier's forge—I went in and told Mr. Penn—I stood by the bar while he went up stairs—Ward came in directly after me—the two sweeps, Taylor and Ryan, came from the backyard through the passage—I knew them by sight—I am sure it was them—I did not know their names before they went out—they had nothing with them.
Taylor. Q. Where was you when I came from the backyard? A. Standing by the bar, I could not see which of you came in out of the yard first—you were together, and went out at the front-door—I remained in the house.
WALTER WILLIAMS . I am fifteen years old, and am a pearl-worker. I saw Ryan chuck the things into the van—I have seen him before, going into Penn's house, and know him by sight—he was at the other end of the street by the van, about two minutes' walk from Penn's, not out of sight of the house—he was throwing white things, like linen, into the van—saw Do-noghue by the side of him—I knew him before, he lived opposite Penn's—I live at the bottom of the street—I went and called my father, he came out with a light, and looked in the van and found the things in it—he sent my mother up to Penn's house to tell him—there was nobody there when my father came out with a light, they had gone away—I did not see Donoghue handle any thing, nor speak to the other one—he was close to the van.
Cross-examined. Q. What time was it? A. Past seven o'clock, it was dark—Donoghue did nothing at all—I did not speak to him, he was very tipsy.
COURT. Q. Whose van was it? A. My father's—there was no horse to it—I was examined three times before the Magistrate, about the van every time—the van was standing where it usually does when it is not wanted, by our door—my father has not a horse of his own, be hires one—the van is used for moving goods.
JOHN WILLIAMS . I am the father of last witness, I live within a few doors of Mr. Penn. My son gave me information—I went out with a light, saw the linen and things in the van—I sent my wife to tell Penn—he came to the van—I tied the things up, and carried them home for him, into a place behind the bar.
THOMAS CHURCH . I am a policeman. At ten minutes after nine o'clock, from information I received, I went to Penn's house—I saw the prisoner Taylor with two women, standing near the back-door which leads into the yard—Taylor immediately went into the yard, leaving the women—I followed him, and found him in the water-closet—I could not see him, he being a sweep, and it being dark—I called out, "I want you"—he came out, and I took him to the station—I returned immediately afterwards to Penn's house, searched the water-closet which I took him from, and found this knife thrown down the water-closet, and on the blade of it are several pieces of putty—I can almost swear it was putty, from the state it was then in—I afterwards went up stairs into the room, and fitted the knife to some marks in the middle pane of a window, and the point of the knife corresponded exactly with the marks where the pane had been broken, by which means the robbery had been effected—the putty had been scraped out on one side, and at the bottom there was two marks on the edge of the frame—I saw marks of soot on the
bed-curtains and on the mattress—I do not know any of the prisoners—Taylor was black, like a sweep—I saw marks of white on his knees, shoulders, and arms, at the station, such as a person would have by climbing up a white wall—there were also marks of black on the inside of Penns wall, such as sweep would make by getting up—marks of a person's toes—the wall is about twelve feet high—there is a urinal in the yard, a water-closet door, and a wall above, then the leads, so that it is like a pair of stairs—he could get on the leads very easily, and on that lead the window opens.
Cross-examined. Q. Whose feet would you swear made the marks? A. I cannot swear—they appeared to be marks of toes—I do not swear they were—I almost swear the marks on the knife were marks of putty—it is a common sort of a knife—I fitted it in the wood where the putty was scraped away, and it corresponded—no doubt any common knife would produce the same mark.
AMBROSE MUGFORD . I am a policeman. I was at the station, about half-past seven o'clock, when a boy came—I was ordered to go up to Mr. Penn's house—I went, and Penn gave Ryan into custody—I took him to the station—I was going back to the house, and saw a brother constable with Donoghne, and assisted in taking him to the station—I went to Perm's house, and brought the goods to the station in a bundle—I have seen Taylor and Ryan before—Taylor is a sweep, and is generally black—I never saw Ryan with a black face before.
Taylor's Defence. His statement was quite different at Union-hall; they convicted one another there; one of the boys said he saw Ryan throw things over the wall, and a man underneath in a brown coat caught them, and ran up to the van; one of the other boys said he saw Ryan throw them into the van himself; he did not say so at Union-hall; one swore it was a man in a brown coat, and the other said it was Ryan; I never had the knife in my possession; I had been in the house an hour and a half; I went backwards, and had not been there five minutes before the policeman came.
TAYLOR — GUILTY . Aged 21.
RYAN — GUILTY . Aged 22.
Transported for Ten Years.
WILLEY — NOT GUILTY .
DONOGHUE — NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430130-812]
812. WILLIAM TAYLOR was again indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of Jan., 1 keg, value 15 s.; and 1 quart of peppermint, 2 s. 8 d.; the goods of Philip Penn.
PHILIP PENN . I keep the Duke's Head. On the 23rd of Jan., about half-past six o'clock, I was in the bar—Kelly brought me a keg—I said, "Who took it?"—he said, "Taylor"—I took it from him, and put it behind the bar—there was about a pint of peppermint in it—it was mine—I saw it that evening on a corner of a shelf inside the bar—it could be reached from outside the bar, from the staircase—it had a brass tap to it—I went for a policeman, but could not find one.
Prisoner. Q. What did Kelly say to you? A. He at first told me he picked it up off the floor—he then said he took it from underneath your arm—I never saw you till afterwards—I did not go into the tap-room—the other robbery was committed about ten minutes after, and then I hardly knew what I was about—I was busy at the time—there were other people outside the bar, but not inside.
WILLIAM KELLY . I was waiting at the bar for a pot of beer, which Mr. Penn was going to draw for a customer—I saw Taylor going in the direction of the door with the keg under his arm—I immediately took it from him, and gave it to Mr. Penn, telling him who I took it from—he was not drunk.
Prisoner. Q. What did you say to Mr. Penn when you took it from me? A. That I took it from under your arm—I placed it on the ground after I took it from you, and took it up again—Mr. Penn could not leave the bar to give you in charge—I did not say at Union-hall that the reason I did not say anything was because I did not like to have a piece of work—that was about the other thing.
JURY. Q. Did he make any resistance when you attempted to take the keg from him? A. No, he went away from the bar as soon as I took the keg from him—he might have gone into the tap-room—I saw him again that evening frequently in and out of the tap-room till he was taken—I did not say any more about the keg—I did not molest him—I had nothing to do with it—he did not have any thing to drink that I know of—I cannot say how long he stopped in the tap-room—I received nothing for my services that night, I merely did it out of kindness, Mr. Penn requesting me previous to the robbery being committed—he had a previous knowledge of me—when I took the keg from the prisoner he was in the house going towards the street-door—it was about a quarter of an hoar before the other robbery took place.
THOMAS CHURCHILL . I am a policeman. I produce the keg.
MR. PENN re-examined. This it my keg.
Prisoner's Defence. It is all false what Kelly says; I never went out till I heard the robbery was committed, and then I went to see what was the matter; the keg was taken before. I got the white on my clothes at the station by sitting against the boards there.
THOMAS CHURCHILL re-examined. The wall of the police-cell is not white—there is part white considerably above where the prisoner's knees could possibly get—there is iron four or five feet high—I noticed the white when I took him at Penn's house.
GUILTY .
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18430130-813]
813. THOMAS MAY was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of Jan., at Camberwell, 9 forks, value 5 l.; 13 spoons, 5 l.; and 1 knife, 8 s.; the goods of George Glenny Anderson, in his dwelling-house.
RACHEL HART . I am servant to Mr. George Glenny Anderson, of Dulwich-common, in the parish of Camberwell. On the 80th of Jan. I went out of the kitchen into the pantry, and while there heard a noise of the plate in the kitchen—I had left some silver forks and spoons on the dresser, amounting to between 8 l. and 9 l.—I went into the kitchen, and saw the prisoner with the plate in both his hands, all out of the basket—I pushed him back to the dresser, and he dropped part of it in the basket, and part out—I seized hold of him, and asked what brought him there—he said he wag doing nothing—he said after that he was begging—I gave him into custody.
Prisoner. I went into the kitchen to look for some bread; I happened to knock against the tray, the servant came in, and said I had taken the plate; I had not touched it. Witness. He had moved all the plate from where I had left it—I had not known him before—the boy had just gone out for a can of water, and left the door open—the plate was in a little, deep black tray, which I used to keep the knives in—I had put it there to wash it—I did not knock any of it down on the ground—I am positive he had it in his hand.
CHARLES SIMMONS . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner into custody—the prosecutor's house is in the parish of Camberwell—we have only got one spoon here, the lady would not let her bring the rest—I saw it at the time.
GUILTY . * Aged 15.— Transported for Ten Years.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18430130-814]
814. FREDERICK CHARLES BURNHAM BLACKWELL, alias Frederick Burnham Blackwell , was indicted for feloniously forging and uttering, at Camberwell, on the 30th of Dec., a certain deed, purporting to be an indenture, made in the year 1840, between one Richard Constable, Elizabeth Hollis Constable, and Joseph Stanley, with intent to defraud the said Elizabeth Hollis Constable.—4 other COUNTS, varying the manner of laying the charge.—2 other COUNTS, stating his intent to be to defraud Eliza Watts.—2 other COUNTS, stating his intent to be to defraud Richard Constable.—6 other COUNTS, for forging and uttering a certain written instrument, purporting to be a deed, with the like intents.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and B ODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
RICHARD BIRCH . I am a solicitor; until lately I was in partnership with Mr. Sutcliffe; we carried on business in New Bridge-street, Black friars—we were professionally concerned for the late Richard Constable—I believe our engagements with him commenced in May, 1838; he was at that time residing in Manor-street, Kennington—he had a life-interest in a place called Mount Pleasant, at Lewisham, at that time—I prepared his will—I cannot say when—he died on the 31st of December, 1840—this is the probate of the will—( looking at it)—after the execution of his will I prepared a deed of settlement, by way of conveyance of his property; that must have been about Feb., 1839—I prepared the deeds on behalf of Mr. Constable—these are the deeds—I prepared this deed of the 1st Feb., 1839, and here is another of the 4th and 5th—they were prepared at the same time—I attended the execution of them—all these instruments assume to dispose of his real property—Mr. Constable was introduced to us as a client, by a Mr. Joseph Stanley—I do not know when Stanley died—from time to time, during Mr. Constable's lifetime, Stanley saw me on the part of Mr. Constable, about his affairs—Mr. Constable was very old and infirm—I have no doubt that Stanley came to me in July, 1840—he came, I think, in Aug., 1840, and gave me instructions about preparing a deed—I prepared the deed, in pursuance of written directions, which I believed were given by Mr. Constable—I had a letter, which I believed, at that time, to have been written by Mr. Constable—that letter was handed, with all Mr. Constable's other papers, to Messrs. Hale, Boys, and Austen, who succeeded us in the business—I am not attesting witness to all these instruments, only to these two of the 1st of Feb., and 4th and 5th of Feb.—I think I am acquainted with the handwriting of Mr. Constable—I have seen him write, and have bad frequent letters from him—I also attested his will in January, 1839, and a mortgage-deed, in 1838, the transfer of a mortgage—this is the letter which Stanley brought to me in August, as from Mr. Constable ( marked A)—in consequence of that letter I prepared the draft of a deed—Stanley brought me back the draft—it remained in my office till Dec.—I got it engrossed by Stanley's direction—this is the deed—( looking at the deed charged to be forged)—it was engrossed on the 30th of Dec., 1840, and handed to Stanley on the 31st—I am quite sure of that—I cannot form any idea as to what time of the day Stanley had the deed from us—it was the last day in the year, and we closed our office rather earlier than usual that evening, but I cannot remember the time—our office opened at ten, or a quarter to ten o'clock, and closed, I should think, at four—at the time it was given to Stanley, it was not executed by any body—it had just come from the stationer's—I cannot remember how soon I saw the deed again—it must have been a few days—it was brought to me by Stanley, and I rather think Stanley signed the deed in my presence, but I am not sure—when he brought it, it was executed as
it now appears, signed by Mr. Constable, and attested, as to the signature of Mr. Constable and his daughter, by the prisoner—I did not see that done, it was brought back in that state—I cannot be certain whether Stanley signed it in my presence—I remember baring some conversation with Stanley about the deed—I think he signed it—he was a party to the deed—his signature is to the deed as a party to it—I know the prisoner—I do not think I saw him at all during Mr. Constable's lifetime—after his death, and the deposit of that deed with us by Stanley, I saw the prisoner at my office some few times—he came, relative to the Lewisham affair, and the affairs of Mr. Constable generally—he came from Stanley—he assigned Stanley's illness as a reason for his coming instead of Stanley—he told me where Stanley lived, when I was desired to go down and see Stanley at Mount Pleasant—I went down, and found Stanley there—that must have been in the summer of 1841—it must have been shortly before his death, which was in August, 1841—I remember the prisoner calling at my office while Stanley was ill, when Miss Constable was there, and I introduced them—I told Miss Constable that the prisoner was a nephew of Stanley's, and she said in his presence she was glad at seeing him for the first time—after introducing them, I left them to talk by themselves—Miss Constable was very desirous of knowing the state of Stanley's health—before that I bad not looked at the deed with any attention—I was not aware that the prisoner purported to be the attesting witness to Miss Constable's signature to the deed—on that occasion Miss Constable expressed herself to him as a person she had not seen before—something was said about Stanley's being ill—Miss Constable desired to know where he was to be found, and to be informed of his state of health from time to time—the prisoner did not tell her where he was to be found—he did not give hit address, but promised to inform her from time to time of his health—I do not know what he said about the address, but it was concealed both from me and her—the forged deed remained at my office until Stanley's death—after Stanley's death, the prisoner called on me, and produced a paper, which he described as being Stanley's will—after I had read the will, and discovered that Stanley had been devising this gentleman's estate to the prisoner, I explained to the prisoner, or to Mr. Jones, a professional gentleman, who was with him, that Stanley had no interest in the estate beyond the payment of a debt that was secured by this deed—at that time I considered this deed to be a genuine document—he produced the original will at the time—it had not been proved—I still retained the deed in my possession—I had a lien on it for costs incurred in the preparation—application was made to a Judge for an order to compel me to deliver it up, which was refused—my costs were something above 100 l. I ultimately delivered up the deed without an order—I cannot give the date when the deed left my possession—it was delivered up on the payment of my costs due from Mr. Constable.
Q. The application about that deed to the Judge occurred on the 1st of Dec., 1841? A. Then the deed must have gone out of our hands a few days after that—I was not present when the deed was handed to the party—I had examined it before it left my office, to see how it was executed—at the time I parted with it to the prisoner it had the same attestation and execution to it as it has now—it purported to be signed by Richard Constable, Elizabeth Hollis Constable, and Joseph Stanley—at the time I introduced the prisoner to Miss Constable I had paid no attention to the mode in which the deed was attested.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. How long before you received instructions to prepare the deed had you acted as attorney for Richard Constable? A. We acted for him from May, 1838—his business was conducted principally through the medium of Stanley—I have seen Mr. Constable—I
dare say I also received notes from him upon the subject of which I afterwards saw him—Miss Constable was living with her father at that time—I think he always wrote his own letters—I do not know of Miss Constable's sometimes being his amanuensis—I saw her frequently at the house in the course of my communications with her fathers, and he used to come on the subject of her father's affairs as well as Stanley—those visits of hers did not continue up to within a short time of Mr. Constable's death—I should say I saw nothing of her from Trinity Term, 1840, until her father's death-there was then a bill of foreclosure in Chancery with respect to the mortgage—this deed purports to be an arrangement for paying off the demands on Mr. Constable, and also paying off the mortgage—if that had been carried out, it would no doubt have relieved him from his embarrassments—it would depend, of course, on the value of the property, supposing it realised the amount, he would have the surplus, if any—I bad not been consulted by Mr. Constable himself as to the best means of proceeding to get out of his difficulties—the occasions on which I saw him were on business—Stanley lived in the house with him part of the time when I was doing business with him.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do you mean at Kennington or in West-square? A. I do not know of his living at Kennington—he lived at Mount Pleasant with him.
MR. CHAMBERS. Q. And Miss Constable too? A. Yes—the last time I saw Mr. Constable must have been in the early part of 1840—I transacted his business myself-I drew the draft of this deed myself—it was settled by a conveyancer—I think the draft was ready to be perused in Aug., 1840—I did not go down to Lewis ham with it—I gave it to Stanley—I cannot say how long he kept it—I know the business stood over till Dec.—at that time the proceedings in Chancery could not go on-I declined patting in the answer—the cause stood still for that time—I have looked at all these signatures to the deed—I think this one of Stanley's I saw him write—I will not be sure—I cannot say how long it was after the 31st of Dec, 1840, that I saw the prisoner—I think the first time I saw him was in consequence of his having made a bill of exchange payable and lodging some money with us—I think it must have been some months after Mr. Constable's death-the deed has blanks in it—I was applied to by Stanley to perfect this deed immediately after I got it, after it was returned executed—I have never been applied to by anybody else to fill up the blanks—I told Stanley we could not supply these blanks unless we had the authority of Miss Constable-I got that authority—this paper ( marked B) purports to be dated Feb., 1841—at that time I had the deed in my possession—I saw Miss Constable after this letter was delivered, not repeatedly—I saw her very few times after receiving that letter, two or three times—I think I must have seen her in the summer of 1841, but I will not swear that—the blanks have not been filled up—I did not tell her I would not fill up the blanks—I never spoke to her at all on the subject of that letter—I did not ask Stanley to get her authority to fill up the blanks—I refused to do it-after this letter purporting to be Miss Constable's authority came, I took advice as to the propriety of stamping the deed and filling up the blanks, and found it would be injudicious-it was in consequence of that advice that I did not do so—here is a sum of 53 l. 16 s. 8 d. referred to in this letter—there had been a sum of about 50 l. paid, which had been borrowed of my partner, not of the firm—that was paid by Stanley on the 24th of Dec, 1840—perhaps there might be interest on it-Stanley had borrowed 60 l. of Mr. Sutcliffe, and deposited the deeds of the Lewisham estate—he borrowed it as Mr. Constable's agent, as he assumed—the 53 l. was paid to my partner—I did not make the slightest reference to this letter when I saw Miss Constable-I did not expect there was any thing
to he said about the deed—I had no suspicion—I never accounted to Miss Constable for not filling up the blanks after receiving this letter—I should have mentioned it if I had been asked about it, but I never was—in my interviews with Miss Constable, no reference was made to the deed or the letter—her attention was never called to such a letter purporting to come from her.
Q. Then were you never applied to again to fill up the blanks? A. I think Stanley, after the receipt of that letter, mentioned the subject, and there the matter dropped, as far as I was concerned—when the prisoner was introduced to Miss Constable I think he was there on business of Mr. Constable's—the prisoner and Miss Constable never met at my office but that once to my knowledge—I think I never saw them together after that—I think the deed and the papers were delivered up without a Judge's order—we had retained them for our bill against Mr. Constable—when that was paid we delivered them up—the bill was paid to our clerk, who is here—at the time I received the deed back from Stanley, apparently executed, I believed it to be genuine—I made an affidavit, in which I stated die time the deed came out of my possession—that affidavit was read to Mr. Constable's medical attendant—that must have been five or six weeks after they paid the bill—that was when I first questioned the validity of the deed, in consequence of what the medical man said to me—the last payment was 41 l. odd—that was some time after the 1st of Dec., 1841—I do not believe I ever applied to the prisoner for payment of our account—I did not apply to anybody—Hale and Co. had applied to us for the bill—they paid the bill that they might get the papers out of our hands—the 4 l. odd was the first bill that was paid, and the other bill for 16 l. was for business done for Mr. Constable—I did not endeavour to raise a loan on the deed while it was in my possession—I entered into negociation for a loan, on the old title, in Trinity Term, 1840, never after that—that money was to be raised for the purpose of paying off the mortgage—I was not then in possession of Mr. Constable's deeds—there were no deeds of Mr. Constable's in our possession, except a deed left as a deposit for 41 l.—we wanted to get money to pay off the incumbrances on Mr. Constable's estate—we endeavoured to do that—I do not think I saw Mr. Constable on that subject, nor Miss Constable—all that took place was with Stanley—I do not remember ever seeing Miss Constable at any time when there was a question about getting money to pay off her father's incumbrances—I do not think she ever evinced any wish of that kind—I believe all the conversation was with Stanley. Q. What was the object of your seeing her at all? A. When she came, she came for advice on particular points, in reference to giving a notice to quit, and those sort of things—I rather think I did though, try to get money after Dec. 1840—if yoa will give me the bill, I can tell—this is my bill against Stanley—oh, yes, I remember I saw Mr. Vigors, of Chancery-lane, on the 23rd of Feb. 1841—that was soon after the deed came into my possession—I saw Mr. Vigors for the purpose of raising money to pay off all the incumbrances—I saw Stanley on that subject, but not Miss Constable—I did not succeed in raising the loan—I should say this was not one of the deeds upon which it was to be raised—it was supposed Miss Constable was in a situation to give a mortgage on the freehold estates, in concurrence with Stanley—it must have been on that deed, if Stanley had any thing to do with it—it was not carried into execution, and we were not successful in raising the money—there was another attempt made, I see, on the 22nd of March—that must have been after I got this letter, authorising me to fill up the blanks.
Q. Did not you attempt to raise money on this deed? A. The deed itself was not mentioned—we considered we could give a good mortgage on the estate, with the concurrence of Stanley and Miss Constable—Aldridge, the
mortgagee, was to be paid off—we should have indorsed something on the deed in discharge of our debt—I believe this letter ( marked C) to be Mr. Constable's handwriting—the signature to this (D) is very unlike his—I never remember seeing this letter before—I really cannot say; I have a doubt about it, or else I should say at once—I have seen Miss Constable write—it is not like hers—I think it is not hers—I should say this was (E)—allow me to look at the one I have a doubt about ( again looking at D)—I now think that is Mr. Constable's—I believe these are his (F and G).
Q. Did you know of Stanley's being engaged to Miss Constable? A. I never knew it from her—I never conversed with her on the subject—I knew of the fact is another way—I believed it to be a fact—I did know of it as a fact—I understood they were engaged to be married.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. who from? A. From the gentleman who introduced Mr. Stanley to me.
MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Was that while Mr. Constable was alive? A. Yes—I never talked to Mr. Constable about it—I cannot give the date when I last saw Mr. Constable—it must have been some time prior to Trinity Term, 1840—I dare say I looked carefully at the deed when it was delivered back to me—I cannot remember—I dare say I examined it to see that it was properly done—I cannot recollect whether I looked at the attestation, but I have no doubt I did.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. During the time you were negociating for the purpose of raising money on the title to Mr. Constable's estate, had you any reason to believe that there was any question as to the validity of this deed? A. Oh no, Miss Constable never called my attention to the letter of authority or to the deed—she never spoke to me on the subject.
THOMAS JOHN COWARD . In 1841 I was clerk to Messrs. Suteliffe and Birch. I knew the late Joseph Stanley very well—at the latter part of 1841 some proceedings were taken by Hale, Boys, and Austen, of Ely-place, to obtain possession of some papers in our possession—on the evening of the lstof Dec. 1841 I saw the prisoner and a clerk of Hale, Boys, and Austen, at our office, and a third person, came, whom I believe to be Mr. Newhall—Hale and Co.'s clerk said he came for the deed which we had in our possession, and to pay us the costs—the prisoner was close to him, and in hearing, the whole time—he was all the time in the same office—the clerk said the application was made on behalf of the executors of the late Joseph Stanley—I am sure the prisoner was by at that time—the clerk paid me the costs, which amounted to 168 l. 15 s., and the deed was given up—it was handed over to Messrs. Hale's clerk, in the prisoner's presence—this is the deed—( looking at it.)
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Was that the only instrument you banded over? A. Yes, on that evening—I should think I was half an hour before I handed it over—I was not in conversation with Messrs. Hales' clerk for some time in the prisoner's absence—I will not swear that the prisoner was not absent for a minute, but he was not absent for some time—I do not think he was absent for a minute; he was not absent for half an hour certainly.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. By whose directions did you deliver that deed to Messrs. Hales' clerk? A. By Mr. Birch's directions—he was in an inner office at the time—the prisoner was in the office all the time, and joined in the conversation, not with Mr. Birch—I dare say he said something about the deed, but I do not know precisely—at the time the clerk said he came from the executors of Mr. Stanley, I knew that the prisoner was one of those executors—I had seen him there before—he had not been on the subject of this deed that I know of, but on the subject of the property to which the deed referred.
WILLIAM JOHN MARTIN . I am clerk to Messrs. Hale, Boys, and Austen. I am subscribing witness to this agreement—I saw it executed—( This agreement not being stamped was not read.)
JOSEPH FRY HEAKES . I am clerk to Mr. Kirk, of Symons-inn—he is attorney for this prosecution—I produce an examined copy of an answer in Chancery in the cause of Aldridge and Blackwell—I examined it with the original in the office.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Is a person named Fluker a fellow-clerk of yours? A. Yes. THOMAS HILL. I am a draper, and live in Southampton-street, Camber-well—I know the prisoner—he was living with me as shopman in 1840—I know his handwriting—I have seen it many times—the signature "F. C. B. Black well" to the attestation of this deed is his writing; also the words "by the within-named Richard Constable and Elizabeth Hollis Constable" I should say are his, ( attestation read) "Signed, sealed, and delivered by the within-named Richard Constable and Elizabeth Hollis Constable, in the presence of F. C. B. Blaekwell and Lavinia Blackwell."
JOHN CHARLES WEAVER LEVER , M. D. I reside in Bridgehouse-place, Southwark. I was acquainted with the late Mr. Richard Constable—I attended him professionally up to the time of his death—he died the last day of 1840—at that time he lived in West-square, Southwark—I also knew Joseph Stanley—on the eve of Mr. Constable's death Stanley came to my house to fetch me to see Mr. Constable; in consequence of what he said, I accompanied him back to Mr. Constable's house—we got to Mr. Constable's house a few moments after the clock struck seven—the clock was striking seven as we entered West-square—when I got into the house Mr. Constable was just dead—I had seen him on the previous day and on that morning alive, I should think between eleven and twelve o'clock in the forenoon—I cannot exactly state the time—he was then dying—he was decidedly not capable of retaining a pen in his hand—he was not sensible.
COURT. Q. Was he so far in a dying state as not to be likely to recover his senses? A. Certainly he was not likely to recover them—I considered him a dying man from the 28th or 29th of the month.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. What state was be in on the night of the 30th? A. Unable to answer questions put to him—I am speaking within bounds when I say that for thirty-six hours before his death he was incapable of answering a question—it was both a physical and mental incapacity—he had inflammation of the lungs, and the brain was affected as well—there was no defect of voice—he could speak before his death, but he was more or less delirious from the 24th of December—for twenty-four hours before hit death he decidedly could not have written his name, or retain a pen in his hand—I know his handwriting—I have often seen him sign his name—the signature to this deed is certainly not his handwriting—I was the attesting witness to one will which he executed, not the will the probate of which has been produced—I afterwards heard of Stanley's death—Mr. Birch told me of it—in consequence of something which I heard I afterwards called at the office of Hale, Boys, and Austen, and had tome conversation with somebody there.
COURT. Q. Was there any occasion on which you heard Mr. Birch read an affidavit, as it was called? A. He read extracts from an affidavit on one occasion in my presence, upon which I called Mr. Birch's attention to the state of Mr. Constable.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. How long altogether had you attended Mr. Constable previous to his death? A. When he was ill, for
five or six years previously—he was, I believe, ninety years of age—I have received letters from him; I received one within six months of his death, in his own handwriting—I do not know his daughter's handwriting—I never received any letters from her—I have known her the same time as I had known her father—she lived with him, I believe, the whole time—I have attended her on several occasions when she was living with her father—she was in the room with him when I saw him on the morning of the 31st of Dec., the whole time—I am sure of that—I cannot state whether I found her there when I went in the morning—I do not recollect her coming into the room—I know she was there, because I remember telling her that her father would not live, most likely, twenty-four or forty-eight hours—I can undertake to swear that I told her that on the morning of the 31st of Dec.—that was in Mr. Constaable's room, I am certain of that—I found her in Mr. Constable's room when I got there in the morning—she remained there the whole time I was there—I remained there, perhaps, a quarter of an hour that evening—I know Mr. Constable's handwriting perfectly well—this letter ( marked A) is very much like his writing—I cannot swear that it is or is not his; I should say it was not his—one part of the signature is very much like it, and the other part is not—the "Richard" is very much like his, the remaining part of the last word is unlike his—I certainly do not believe that to be his handwriting—let me look at it again—I have not read the letter—the whole of the word "Constable's I believe not to be his handwriting—I believe the "Richard" to be very much like his—I do not believe this (C) to be his handwriting, nor this (D)—I believe this (E) to be his, and these (H and F)—I do not believe this (G) to be his.
Q. When had you any suspicion of anything wrong?—was it when you beard something read out in your presence by Mr. Birch? A. Yes; my suspicions were first attracted by that circumstance—I cannot fix the date of that—it was after the 5th of Nov.; I think the beginning of Dec.; I cannot say exactly—it was on a Friday, either the first or second Friday in Dec.—I had a bill against the executors of Mr. Constable for my attendance on him, I think it was about 30 l.—that has not been paid—I have requested payment of it—I have requested payment from Miss Constable herself, as executrix under the will—I first requested payment from her a few weeks, perhaps a month, after her father's death, when she was getting together the bills that were due—I afterwards applied to the prisoner, before 1 knew of the existence of this deed—it was about the 6th or 7th of Nov. that I first applied to him—I have seen him once or twice—I have applied to him verbally and otherwise, perhaps, three or four times—I wrote to him after I heard the affidavit read of the existence of this deed.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you make any application to him after you knew of this deed, except in writing? A. No.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Had you and he any personal dispute about it? A. None at all—when I applied to him in writing I did not get my money, or any answer—it was not then for the first time that I mentioned my suspicions to anybody—I had mentioned them to Mr. Hale before—that was after my endeavours to get the bill from the prisoner—when I first applied to the prisoner I did not know of the existence of this deed—I knew of Stanley's death, and had read of Stanley's will, in Doctors' Commons, leaving his property to his nephews and nieces—I had no dispute with the prisoner about a picture—I requested him to give me one, which Mr. Constable had presented me with in his lifetime.
Q. What made you apply to the prisoner for Mr. Constable's picture? A. Because I understood from Mr. Birch that he had come into possession of the property—he promised to send me the picture—he did not do so—I have
since got it from Miss Constable, perhaps two months ago—I first saw Miss Constable after writing to the prisoner last summer—this is the letter I wrote to the prisoner—( produced.)
Q. Had you at that time the slightest intention of saying one single word about your suspicions, if you had been paid the amount of your bill? A. Certainly I had—I should not have moved in the case, paid or not, unless I bad been subpœnaed or summoned—I cannot swear that I did not mention the case to anybody after writing this letter—I may have mentioned the case, but I should not have forwarded the case unless subpœnaed or summoned—I should not have come forward in the matter at all—I was acquainted with Miss Constable as long as I knew her father—I certainly thought that a deed of this kind would materially affect her interests—I certainly did not mean to do anything about it unless summoned or subpœnaed—I should have kept perfectly quiet up to the present moment, unless I had been summoned or subpœnaed—I did not, on the prisoner not paying my bill, go and make a communication without being summoned or subpœnaed—I might have mentioned it—I cannot tell to whom—I went to Mr. Hale before I wrote that letter—I did not make any communication after Writing that letter, till one of Mr. Kirk's clerks called on me for my information—I cannot tell how he came to do that—I think I had not made any communications previously—I was first summoned to Union-hall—I cannot say whether I had made any communication to anybody previous to that—I might have talked of it, but it must have been in the course of conversation, not to forward any criminal proceedings—my bill has not been paid—Miss Constable has not promised to pay it—I have not applied to her for payment since this affair—I did not apply to her for the picture—she left it at a house for me—I have had no communication about the payment of my bill since, with anybody—I made no further application after receiving no answer to my letter—I let the thing rest until I was summoned to Union-hall.
MR. CLARAKSON. Q. How long have you been practising as a doctor of medicine? A. Six months—I was admitted as a member of the College of Physicians last June—before that I was a member of the College of Surgeons, and of the Apothecaries' Company as well—I have been practising in London nine or ten years—the payment or non-payment of the 30 l. would not have altered the evidence I have to give—Mr. Constable had made me a present of the picture about eighteen months before his death—I had failed to remove it from his house during the time he was at Lewisham—Mr. Stanley had often promised to bring it to town, but he never did—after his death I applied to the prisoner, as Stanley's representative—about two months ago Miss Constable sent it to a place for me, from which I removed it
WILLIAM LISTER . I am a cloth-factor, and live in Basinghall-street, City. I am a member of the Common Council—I was an old acquaintance of the late Mr. Richard Constable—I know his handwriting—do not believe the signature to this deed to be his handwriting.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Did you ever see Mr. Constable write on parchment? A. I do not know that I have—I am confident this is not his—I have letters in my pocket which I received from him—I have been looking at those letters—I do not see anything tremulous in this signature—I do not think it is at all like his—it is totally unlike it—it does not bear the least resemblance, in any one of the letters—I do not think it would deceive me for an instant—I have a letter of his in my pocket, dated as late as May, 1840, and one of Nov. 1839—he was in the habit of corresponding with me up to as late as May, 1840, on matters of business, relative to his estate—the signature to this letter ( marked E) is more like his
writing, but I have my doubts of it, I do not think it is his—that I think is his ( marked D) and this ( marked A); I have doubts upon this ( marked C)—I do not recollect ever seeing any such a flourish to the "C"—the finish to the "e" resembles his more—I have not been in the habit of seeing him sign his name "Richard," only "R"—this is "Richard" at length, and I do not recollect ever seeing his "C" with such a flourish underneath—I cannot say that this is his signature (H)—the "e" does not finish in his usual way, and the top part of the "C" is different to what he used to make—I doubt that being his—I know Miss Constable—I have seen her at her father's, when I have called to see him—she used to pass by the name of Watts—the last time I called to see them was the 10th of Feb., 1840—I have not seen him or her since—they were then at Lewisham—I do not know her handwriting—I never saw her write, nor ever received letters from her—I do not consider the signature to this (G) to be Mr. Constable's—I knew Stanley in the wool-trade—I met him at Mr. Constable's latterly.
---- THOMPSON, M. D. I am a nephew of the late Mr. Richard Constable. I knew his handwriting very well—I certainly think the signature, "Richard Constable," to this deed, is not his writing—there is less of flourish in it than he always used—it is certainly an imitation of his writing, but it is clearly not his—I have not a doubt about it.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. You have seen that before, have you not? A. I saw it here on Friday—I looked at it for some seconds or minutes, as I have looked at it now—I do not think I ever saw my uncle sign a deed—it does bear some resemblance to his writing—I never should have suspected it to have been his, if I had seen it—I should certainly say it if clearly not his—when it was put into my hand I had been informed that the question was, whether it was a forgery or no forgery—it bears some resemblance to his writing; not a strong resemblance; I think it is an imitation—I can swear I think it is not my uncle's writing—this paper marked (C) is a good deal more like his writing—I should believe it was his, except that it is "Richard" at length, and forty-nine times out of fifty it was "R" only—I think it is his, with that exception—I cannot certainly say whether I ever saw him sign "Richard"—it bears a very close resemblance to his writing—this paper marked (A) I should take as his writing—I have no doubt about it—the word "Constable" is very like his, I think it is his writing.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. I believe you live at No. 129, Piccadilly? A. Yes—my uncle resided at Lewisbam formerly, and latterly at Kensington—he was the brother of the late Mr. Abraham Constable, who lived at Lewisham many years, and was a magistrate there—this property originally belonged to him—he built the house.
FREDERICK JOSEPH WRIGHT . I am keeper of the Records of the Court of Chancery—I produce the original answer of Frederick Charles Burnham Blackwell and Phœbe Blackwell to a bill—here is the answer, and here the bill—there was also an answer of Miss Constable, that is not here—it could have been if it was wanted.
THOMAS HILL re-examined. I believe the signature to this answer to be the prisoner's handwriting. ( The following extract was here read front the answer:)—"But they do allege, as the fact is, that the said Richard Constable did in his life-time execute an indenture, bearing date 30th Dec., 1840, under which the said Joseph Stanley claimed to be entitled to the equity of redemption in the said mortgage.
JOB JONES . I am a linen-draper, and live at Islington. The prisoner was apprenticed to me—he served about four years and a quarter—I know his handwriting—I saw very little of it while he was with me, not sufficient to
swear to it—the attestation to this deed seems very much like hit writing—I cannot swear to it—it is something similar—I should believe and rather think it was his:—( The following extract from the Bill in Chancery, was here read:—"And whether they do not allege, and insist that the said Richard Constable in his lifetime, and Elizabeth Hollis Constable since, have or not executed the said deed to Joseph Stanley deceased, and other documents Under which be became or claimed to be entitled to some estate and interest in the said mortgage.")
CHARLOTTE STANLEY . I am the wife of John Stanley, a surgeon, and live in Commercial-road, Lambeth—he is the son of the late Joseph Stanley—I was acquainted with Joseph Stanley—he was the prisoner's uncle—I knew him in the years 1836, 1837, and 1838—he was very badly off at that time—I frequently lent him money—he was in a state of poverty during the years 1835, 1836, 1837, and 1838—I know that by his coming to me, and borrowing 1 s. and 6 d.—he wan in the habit of doing to—I know the prisoner—I never saw him and Stanley together in the Fleet—I have been frequently in the Fleet, but I never saw Stanley there—he went to the coffee-room—I never saw him and the prisoner together—I do not know whether the prisoner was in the habit of associating with Stanley—I saw the prisoner's sister, Phœbe, at the time her father lay dead—I am not aware that the prisoner was in the habit of associating with Phœbe.
DR. LEVER re-examined. I did not see Stanley at any time on the 31st of Dec., until he called at my house—I did not see him at Mr. Constable's house on the 30th—I never saw the prisoner at Mr. Constable's.
MR. CHAMBERS. Q. About how long altogether, putting both your visits together, do you think you were in the house that day? A. Half or three quarters of an hour, including both visits—his daughter nursed him.
CHARLOTTE STANLEY re-examined. I belive the prisoner was aware that Joseph Stanley was in great poverty, from different conversations that I have heard when I have been with some part of the family—I never learnt it from the prisoner himself—I was very little with him—I have seen very little of him—I have heard that Stanley was in the habit of going to the Fleet Prison, but I did not see him—William Blackwell, the prisoner's brother, was in the Fleet—I do not know from the prisoner, or from anybody in his presence, whether he knew of Stanley's going there—I have heard him speak ill of Stanley.
MARY WAGNOR . I did live at No. 19, Liverpool-street, Camden-town, but now live at No. 16, Stanmore-street, St. Paneras-road. I knew Joseph Stanley—he was in poverty—he came to me the year the Queen was crowned, in the summer time, and was there about seven or eight months—I cannot say exactly to a month, but he left me about Easter, and I was very glad to get rid of him—I never saw the prisoner.
ELIZABETH JENKINSON . I live at No. 15, Swinton-street, Gray's-inn-road. The late Joseph Stanley lodged at my father's house for about ten weeks, in the year 1838—during that time his circumstances were very poor indeed—his wife and three children lived with him—they appeared very pool in their circumstances, and their clothing was poor—the children were constantly coming into our kitchen complaining of hunger, and their clothing exhibited poverty in the extreme—this was about the middle of April, 1838—he was to pay 12 s. a week for his lodging, which he did for the first three weeks, and afterwards we had it by instalments—I know nothing of the prisoner—I never saw him before—I remember Stanley's wife being confined—she had no doctor until afterwards, and no nurse was spoken to—she had her sister to nurse her afterwards—there were very few clothes for the child.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Is 1838 the last time you speak of Stanley, within your knowledge? A. It is—he did not go about with ragged clothes like a beggar, but his children and his wife did—he generally went out decently dressed, like a respectable person.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Had you any idea how he got his living at that time? A. He told us he was a wool-stapler.
---- YACKNEY. I am an engineering surveyor, and live in London-road, Southwark. I knew the late Joseph Stanley from eighteen to twenty years—in 1836, I dined with him at one of his son's, Mr. John Stanley, in Hercules-buildings, Lambeth—I knew him, and saw him in the years 1835, 1836, 1837, and 1838—I think I saw him up to about the latter part of Sept, 1838—he did not appear to be a person of property, on the contrary—the first time I met with him he appeared very needy, and said he was travelling, selling cigars and tea by commission—I should say his circumstances were very indifferent—there was every appearance of poverty.
Q. Did you ever know him to be in a condition to lend 800 l. or 1000 l. A. I should rather question such a thing, certainly—I should say certainly not.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. What period of 1838 do you speak of? A. That was the last time I saw him—it was in the City, at the latter part of Sept., I should say about the 20th—I never had any dealings or transactions with him, beyond sometimes giving him a glass of stout, and a sandwich—that was the last refreshment we had together—I never let him go by without speaking, because I was always soliciting him to do something for his son—he was engaged in Chancery matters at that time.
EDMUND MESSENGER . I am a tailor, and live at Camberwell. I know the prisoner, and have often seen him write—the signature, "F. C. B. Blackwell," attesting this deed, I believe to be the prisoner's handwriting; also the writing above, "By the within named Richard Constable and Elizabeth Hollis Constable, in the presence of"—that is all in his handwriting.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. How long have you known him? A. From the year 1839—I did not live with him then—he was introduced to me by a person named Smith—I was arrested for debt in 1840—the prisoner did not become bail for me on that occasion—I drew a bill on him about that time, for money which he owed me for clothes, and it was never paid—I went to live with him at Croydon, and assisted him in his shop there—that was two years after I drew the bill on him—he has never paid me the money, I could not receive it, for when I failed I made an assignment of my property—he did not make any charge against me after I had been with him about three months—he did not give me into custody—he did not charge me with robbing him of 4 l. 16 s., nor did he give me into custody in the presence of one Thomas Clack—that I swear—he never threatened to give me into custody—he said there was a balance of 4 l. 16 s. or 4 l. 18 s. due, and we proved afterwards that there was no deficiency whatever—he did not, to my knowledge, say that I had appropriated it to my own use—he did not threaten to give me into custody on account of that deficiency, or any thing to that effect, to my knowledge—this is the very first I ever heard of it—he did not charge me with embezzlement—I did not leave immediately after that—I remained two or three months after he mentioned about the 4 l. 16 s.—I assisted him when I was in business—I have often seen him write.
ARTHUR WIGTON GRANT . I am a cowkeeper, and have lived at Lewisham all my life. I knew Joseph Stanley, and know the prisoner—after Mr. Constable left Mount Pleasant-house, the prisoner came to live there with two females—I cannot say in what year that was—it is about two years ago, or rather better, perhaps—I do not know that I ever saw Stanley there with
them—I remember the time when the furniture was taken away from Mount Pleasant—I did not see Stanley with the prisoner on that occasion—a carrier of the name of Lane removed part of the furniture, in the prisoner's presence—that was on the 4th of June, 1841—I purchased some little articles of the prisoner from the yard, and paid him for them—that was on the 4th of June—I have seen him write—I believe the signature to this deed to be his writing—I cannot speak to the words, "By the within-named," &c.—( looking at a paper)—I got this piece of paper from the prisoner on the 4th of June, the day it is dated—I agreed with the prisoner to purchase a field of grass—that was one of Mr. Constable's grass-fields—on my agreeing to purchase it, he wrote that paper—I paid him 5 l. on the day it was purchased, and was to pay 5 l. more on the 26th of December following—I did not pay that according to agreement—I imagined that he had a proper right to the grass, or else I should not have purchased it—I cut the grass, and made it on the premises, and stacked it on my own—I cannot say that I ever saw the prisoner and Stanley together—I have very often seen Stanley at Mount Pleasant, not during the time the prisoner was living there, but previously—I believe Stanley lived, at Mount Pleasant—he very often used to go up and down the lane.
ELIZABETH HOLLIS CONSTABLE . I am the daughter of the late Richard Constable. I resided with him down to the time of his death—he died on the 31st of Dec., 1840, in West-square, Lambeth—he was quite unconscious on the day he died, and also the day before—I knew Joseph Stanley—he came to my father's house, in West-square, the day my father died—I am not quite certain that he was not there the day before—I cannot recollect, but I do not think he was—previous to that I had not seen him for a very considerable time, perhaps three weeks—it was about half-past six o'clock in, the evening when he came on the 31st—he saw my father—I requested him to go for Dr. Lever, and he went—he was a very short time in the house before he went for the doctor—a few minutes, perhaps—he was in my sight all the time—when he came back my father had expired—I can scarcely swear that he was there on the 30th, but I think he was.
Q. Are you able to say whether any business was transacted, any signing of deeds, or anything of that sort, either on the 30th or 31st, by your father? A. Decidedly not—the signature to this deed is not my father's, nor is this mine—this letter ( marked A) is not my father's writing—nor is this one, ( marked B) mine—my father generally signed his name "Richard" in full—the signature to this (A) is "Rich" abbreviated—it is a good imitation of my father's writing, but it is not his—I first saw the prisoner at Mr. Birch's office, I should say at the latter end of June, 1841—that was before I went to Ireland—I afterwards accepted a situation in a nobleman's family in Ireland, and was absent from England for some time—I came over to England on hearing of Stanley's death, and had some interviews with the prisoner, not many—I claimed some furniture in the house at Lewisham, and general property—in pursuance of that, I went to the prisoner's house in Crawford-street—I returned to Ireland, I believe, from my visit to England; and returned again for good on the 18th of Nov., 1841—I went to Crawford-street about a week before Christmas, 1841—the prisoner was carrying on business there at that time—I remember an occasion when there was a conversation respecting this deed—the prisoner's mother and wife were present then—the conversation began with an inquiry respecting the furniture, and they were indignant and rude about it—his wife said that they had a right to the furniture, for that my father owed them 800 l.—the prisoner was not present at that time—he came in afterwards—he was in and out of the room—he might have heard the
conversation—I do not know whether he did or not—he and his wife left the house with me; and as we were going along Crawford-street, I said it was quite time that we had a perfect understanding respecting the furniture and property that had been removed from Lewisham—they said they considered the furniture theirs, for that my father owed Mr. Stanley 800 l., and Mr. Stanley had left everything to them—I said it was untrue, he never owed him anything—Mrs. Blackwell said he did, for they had a deed for it—I said, "You have no such thing, no such deed"—the prisoner said they had, for he and his wife had witnessed it—I replied, if they had, it was a forgery, for they never saw my father—the prisoner seemed very much alarmed on that, turned himself round, and said, "You don't mean to say that you charge us with forgery?"—I said, "If there is such a thing existing, it is a forgery"—Mrs. Blackwell said, "No, it will be no forgery, unless we swear to it"—I said I felt myself very ill treated, and I should not let it rest where it was—the prisoner said he was going to Chatham, where he had a house of business, and should return in three days—in the course of conversation about witnessing the deed, he said that his uncle had filled in the vacant names, and they had witnessed it at his (the prisoner's) house, at Camberwell—the prisoner kept a draper's shop at Camberwell—my father was never there.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. When were you lucky enough to have this conversation? A. About a week before Christmas, 1841, in Crawford-street—I rather think there were lodgers in the house there—I mentioned this conversation to Mr. Petch, and I mentioned it to Mr. Boys, the following year, in 1842—I mentioned it immediately to my friends, of whom Mr. Petch was one—he is a housekeeper, in St. John-street—I had the prisoner taken up a very short time ago—I never saw him after this conversation, to have an opportunity of doing so before—I was not indicted by him before I charged him—either be or his wife appeared against me at Union Hall—I appeared at Union Hall on a charge of having committed a forcible entry—that was at the instance of the prisoner's sister—she appeared there—it was after that that I had the prisoner taken up—I was not taken into custody—I had gone to a house in which the prisoner's sister lived—I went with Mr. Fluker, the attorney's clerk, not with other persons—the house was not broken into in my presence—I found the door broken.
Q. Did you not go in the dusk of the evening, and after you had sent persons forward, find the door broken open? A. I sent no person forward—I was invited by Miss Ann Blackwell to take away all the furniture I found there—she brought me the keys to unlock all the doors—I do not know whether that was after the onter door was broken open—I did not know whether there was any door broken open—the door was open—I do not say it was broken open—I found it open—I found Miss Ann Blackwell there, and Phœbe Blackwell, and her nieces—I saw no one else—I saw Mr. Fluker—there were a great number of persons—there was no riot—we had applied to Mr. Cottingham for a warrant, and he considered it was the subject of an action—we were not three minutes about the premises before I found the door open—I will swear my friends were not wailing there the whole afternoon to get in—Mr. Fluker was not, to my knowledge.
Q. How came you to go there that evening? A. I had been there for some days before—the property was mine, and they were conveying it stealthily away—I had been watching for some days before—I had not been watching that afternoon—I was visiting at a house opposite, at Mr. Clifford's, the assistant overseer—Rutland, the officer, was in the house with me—I did not know who he was then, but I afterwards knew he was an officer—I had taken tea at Mr. Clifford's—I hud been there in the course of the afternoon,
and walking to and fro in the street—I had been there some time watching my property—I was there perhaps six or seven hours, or more, to see that nothing was taken away—Mr. Clifford advised me to remain there, and if I saw any one taking property away to detain it—it was in consequence of what he said that I walked to and fro to see what was going on—I went out, bearing cries of "Murder"—I had been sitting quietly in the parlour—the cries of "Murder" came from Sarah Blackwell, Miss Blackwell's niece—she said it was her—when I went over I found a number of persons in the house—Phœbe Black well was sent to prison—I have been indicted for a forcible entry—I went to Union-hall, and I dare say I was held to bail—( looking at the deed)—I think this signature, "Richard Constable," is not at all like my father's, not the least—it does not bear the least resemblance—I saw it at Union-hall—I had not my glasses on then—two deeds were put into my hand at the same moment, and I could not tell one from the other—I put on my glasses at Union-hall, but I had a very transient glance of the deed there—I have since seen it, and I then said I could not tell one from the other.
Q. When have you since seen it? A. At Union-hall—I do not know who showed it to me—I saw it at Union-hall before the Magistrate—I cannot recollect who showed it to me—the room was full of gentlemen—somebody showed it to me.
Q. Then first of all you doubted, then you put on your spectacles, and still doubted, and you have since seen it, and you do not doubt? A. Yes—it is not at all like my father's signature, nor is this signature at all like mine—it is of course an attempt, but it is not mine—they are both attempts—I am convinced it is an attempt, because it is a forged deed—it may bear a resemblance to my signature, but it is not mine—I have seen the deed, and examined it—( looking at a deed of confirmation)—this is my signature—I have seen this before—I executed this at Mr. Boys', in Ely-place—I signed it—I said at Union-hall that I did not recollect signing it—I could not till one from the other—I have always recollected signing a deed at Mr. Boys' office—I was always perfectly conscious that I bad done so—I signed it on the 14th of Dec., 1841—the prisoner had taken me to Mr. Boys', but he had not at that time a retainer from me as my solicitor—in the March following he wrote me a letter, to require that I would give him a few lines, appointing him my solicitor, a written retainer—at the time this deed was executed, the prisoner, Mr. Boys, (Mr. Martin, his clerk, I think,) and Mr. Austin, his partner, were present—Mr. Austin was, I am sure, and I think Mr. Martin was—I went to their office for the purpose of executing that deed—I cannot say that I knew the nature of the deed before I was called upon to execute it—I did not execute it without some hesitation—I asked Mr. Boys to explain it to me—it was not read to me, and he said it was necessary that it should be read, and he read a part of it, which included the name of Phœbe Blackwell—I remarked to him that I had never beard of Phœbe Blackwell before, and it was a very extraordinary thing to give a benefit to a person I had never seen or heard of—he said she was Mr. Blackwell's sister, and was so mixed up with her uncle's affairs, that it would make great confusion to separate them—(this was before I was charged with violently breaking into the house and taking the furniture)—I inquired of Mr. Boys if he knew this person, I having never seen her—he said he did not know much of her, or he did not know her; that he understood she was a lame person, and resided in Derbyshire—I had asked to have the contents of the deed explained, and they were explained so far—I did not know what I was signing.
Q. How came you, a person of your mature age, and apparent care and caution, to sign a deed when you did not know what it contained? A. The
prisoner came to me, on my return from Ireland, about the 19th of Nov., 1841, and after speaking of his uncle's death, he inquired what I was going to do respecting the freehold at Lewisham—I said, "I really hardly know what to do with it"—he said, did I know any gentleman who would relieve the free-hold from its difficulties; there was a mortgage of 2000 l. due on it, and the interest—I said no, I did not know any gentleman that was so inclined—he then inquired if he paid off the mortgage, would I give him an interest in it?—I said it was a very pretty place, and I would give any gentleman an interest in it, who had the means of enjoying it—he inquired what I could live upon as an income—I said my views were humble, and a small income would support me—he said, could I live on 30 l. a-year?—I said "Yes, I could"—he said if he were disposed to relieve it from its difficulties, would I give him an interest in it?—he said that he could do so—I inquired what his means were—he said he had 800 l. his own property; that he had four shops of business, the one he resided in, one at Croydon, one in Crawford-street, and one at Chatham, and that in the course of a few months he should have a very respectable wholesale warehouse in Wood-street; that his brother would lend him 1000 l., and that he had so much interest in the City, that he could procure 3000 l. or 4000 l. at two hours' notice at any time—I said if he could do that, I could certainly see no objection to give him an interest in it—(at this time I had not seen the deed in question)—he inquired what the incumbrances were—I told him there was the bill of costs due to Mr. Parker; there was 2000 l. owing to the mortgagee, with the interest accruing thereon; there were thirteen guineas owing to the Bridge-house estate, for ground-rent for a field; that the house I had insured as long as I could for 1500 l., but it was now falling in—he said that all those things he would immediately set smooth, that it was a very pretty place, and that in a fortnight or three weeks' time I should see it in as good condition as I had ever seen it before—(there are twelve acres of land to it)—I lent him a pencil, and he calculated what the sums would amount to, and said it would be no inconvenience to him, he could get it done very easily, and left me—I forgot to say that he likewise mentioned that Ely-place, Holbora, belonged to his family, and that they had a very great interest in one side of Earl-street, Blackfriars, if it did not wholly belong to them—no one was present at this conversation—(it occurred in the house of Mrs. Gibbs, No. 4, Kent-place, Pimlico, opposite the end of Lower Belgrave-street—Mrs. Gibbs is a very old friend of mine, and was thirteen years house-keeper to the late Sir William Stirling—on my return from Ireland I wrote to the prisoner, and he wrote to say he would call on me, which he did)—when he had calculated what the amount would be, he said he would see Mr. Boys, of Ely-place, on the subject—I attended at Mr. Boys's, I believe, a day or two afterwards, but the next evening I went to the prisoner in Southampton-street, as I wanted to talk to him about the furniture—he had come so late, that the evening was too far gone to enter on the subject, and I went to him next evening to talk about the furniture, and general property—there were papers of great value to me—when I got there he was not at home—a Mr. Newhall came in soon after I arrived, and after waiting some time, and complaining that the prisoner never kept his appointments, he left the bouse for a very short time—the prisoner returned home in the meantime, but I did not talk about the papers and other matters of consequence—Mr. Newhall came in again, and complained that Mr. Birch had behaved excessively ill, and said that he was no lawyer, and did not know what he was about—that seemed to be conversation between themselves—they seemed to have been to Mr. Birch on some occasion, and were dissatisfied with him—he said Mr. Birch was a fool; that he had prepared a deed of reversion for the Thompson family
to sign, which is a numerous branch; that he had shown no consideration, and therefore the deed was invalid, and was of no use—I did not say one word to the prisoner about the furniture, or other matters upon which I went to see him—I was there perhaps an hour, or more—I went late, because I would not interrupt his business, his evenings I considered more at leisure—I waited for some time—Mr. Newhall said that Mr. Birch had erred in his judgment; that he understood what my very liberal proposal had been to Mr. Blackwell, and in the formation of another deed, great care must be taken not to commit such an error—I left the house without mentioning the subject on which I went—Mr. Newhall broke in quite on another subject, said Mr. Stanley had robbed him, and that he had been often deceived by him, and I had no opportunity of saying any thing about the furniture—he said Mr. Stanley had made false representations to him, and if the prisoner did not turn out an honest man he should suffer very considerably, for he had put 800 l. worth of goods into bis hands—I do not think I went to Mr. Boys the next day—I went there a few days after the prisoner's visit to me at Pimlico—I did not mention any thing about the furniture and other important matters at Mr. Boys's—I did not doubt the prisoner's honesty at the moment, until I had fully executed the deed, and then they shuffled altogether about the furniture, and would not give any part of it up—previous to going to Mr. Boys, the prisoner had given me his promise that the furniture, and other things that belonged to me should be given up; that it was in the possession of his mother, who lived in Camberwell, but was at that time in Derbyshire, and he apologised for not being able to go with me at that time—that was between the 6th of Sept., when I arrived in London, and that day month, when I was in Ireland again—the prisoner wrote to me in Ireland, and his address was Southampton-street—I was very anxious to know what he had done with the property generally, and when I came to England I took a cab from the Euston station, and went through London to Southampton-street, though I was very much fatigued—I saw the prisoner standing in his shop—I did not know till then that he was a draper—the moment he recognised me, having seen me at Mr. Birch's, he apologised for not being able to ask me into his sitting-room, as there were some gentlemen there on particular business—I inquired after his uncle, whose death had taken place in the interval, and which was the cause of my coming to London—he said he had died in his house; that he had had every care taken of him; that he was a very distressed man, that if he had not done so, he must either have died in the street, or have been taken to the workhouse—this conversation was in the prisoner's shop, in Southampton-street—I do not know whether we were quite alone—there might have been some of his men in the shop—I dare say there were—two women passed while I sat there, who I have since discovered to be his wife, and Phoebe Blackwell—they were not there—they passed through into the inner room, and shut the door after them.
Q. Now when you were at Mr. Boys' office, do you mean to say thai this deed, to which you then affixed your signature, was not distinctly explained to you in all its parts? A. It was not—I do not know the points of it at all—I know nothing that it contains more than this, that Phoebe Blackwell's name was contained in it, and I objected to her name, not knowing her—I afterwards agreed that her name should remain, as I had a great deal of confidence in the prisoner, and Mr. Boys said, "It will make a great deal of confusion if we strike her out, and it will not affect your interests at all"—I said, "I know nothing of this man except being Mr. Stanley's nephew, and he has the appearance of an honest man," but I said, "Does it not seem very odd, Mr. Boys, to give an interest to a person I never saw?"
Q. Is that all you knew with respect to the deed, that it gives an interest
in something to Miss Phoebe Blackwell? A. The prisoner in the mean time had brought the draft to me, two or three evenings after I had been to Mr. Boys—it was all done within a few days—the prisoner called on me twice at Pimlico—he brought me the draft, and said he had brought it for my perusal—I said, "Mr. Blackwell, I cannot understand it, it is a great roll of paper, I shall not understand one-half of it, and if you comply with your promises to me, that is all I require," and he took it away with him again—it was soon after I arrived in England that he brought the draft to me, and soon after his first visit to me at Pimlico—no one was present when he brought the draft to me—he begged that no one might be present.
Q. When did he beg that? A. On the evening that he first proposed it—I proposed that I should name his offer to some of ray friends—he said, "No, don't do that, you will be vacillating about it, you won't find any one else that will make you a better offer, and don't mention it to any one"—I did not open or look at the draft at all—I did not make inquiries respecting the furniture when he brought the draft—I paid a great many visits to Southampton-street after that—it was before he brought the draft that I went there and saw Mr. Newhall there—that was between his first and second visit to me at Pimlico—I did not ask him about the furniture when be brought the draft, because he came so late in the evening and hurried away again—I do not know how long he was there—he scarcely sat down—when I saw the roll of paper I said, "This is all leather and prunella to me, I shall not understand it; and if you make good your promises to me that is all I shall require"—I had told Mr. Boys the same.
Q. When you went respecting the deed, you said be need not explain it, it was all leather and prunella to you? A. Yes.
Q. Then you signed it, making that observation, did you? A. It was not ready to sign at that time—Mr. Boys appointed me to go there one day, and there was a mistake in the stamp, and then he appointed for me to come on the next evening—I went next evening—the prisoner came and fetched me from Pimlico in a cab, and I then signed the deed—that was on the 14th of Dec.—it was then that the observation was made about Phoebe Blackwell's name being in it.
Q. Was any portion of it read then? A. I said that I thought it was of no use to read it, I did not care about it; if Mr. Blackwell fulfilled his engagements, as I had no doubt he would, I did not want to hear any thing about it—Mr. Boys said he never allowed any lady or gentleman to sign any thing in his office without reading it, and in that case he read a part of it—I believe that was what Mr. Boys said—he read the part attaching to Phoebe Blackwell and her brother—I said to Mr. Boys, "Stop, sir, if you please, I don't want such a name as Phoebe Blackwell, I know nothing about her," and he then made the remark about her being so mixed up with her uncle's affairs.
COURT. Q. Was any copy of this deed delivered to you? A. No.
MR. CHAMBERS. Q. What did the deed say with respect to the prisoner and Phoebe Blackwell? I cannot recollect what it was he read—I recollect Phoebe Blackwell's name was introduced, and I objected to it—it was a small portion of it that was read—after reading it he said, "Are you satisfied?"—I said, "I do not know that I have any reason to be dissatisfied; I dare say I shall find this young man very honourable, and that is all I shall require"—Mr. Boys said, "It won't affect your interests at all" and he then invited me round to his desk to sign it—I was going to sign it, he called Mr. Martin, his clerk, I think, and he said, "Stop, Miss Constable, before you sign it I
will call my partner, Mr. Austen, in to witness it"—he did so, and it was signed.
Q. Did you believe that it would not affect your interests at all when you signed it? A. Except, so far that it would relieve me from all the difficulties I was labouring under—I was frightened to move—I considered, by affixing my signature, that I was conveying the freehold estate to the prisoner for a consideration, for my living—I was under an impression that it would be a great benefit to me—the prisoner had firmly impressed on my mind that it would not affect my interests, and not to mention it to Mr. Boys—if the prisoner had performed his promise honestly he would have had a great deal to pay—he is a very young man.
Q. You have spoken in high terms of Stanley; at that time had you any reason to alter your opinion of Stanley's honour? A. The first doubt I had thrown up on Stanley's honour was by Mr. Newhall, who very much astonished me in depreciating him in every way—up to that time I had not the least doubt of Stanley—I had a very high opinion of him, and so had my father—he died with that impression—Stanley had been very much trusted by my father and myself, we placed the greatest confidence in him—he was constantly with us during the time we resided at Lewisham—it was a lone place, he resided in the house—my father was eighty-seven years of age—we had known Stanley ever since the year 1810—he was then a rich merchant, in King's Arms-yard, Coleman-street, in the wool trade—when he afterwards came to us, he represented that he had been some years in America—he was trusted and confided in by my father and myself from the moment of his putting his foot into our house, from the year 1837, and then he represented that he had just arrived in England—he resided in our house at Lewisham—we went from town to Lewisham—my father died in West-square—Stanley did not reside in that house—we had been there from the period of leaving our house in the June preceding—the house, Mount Pleasant, at Lewisham, was let furnished to a Mr. Freeman, a solicitor, of Coleman-street—I never saw Newhall till I saw him at the prisoner's house—I saw him after that—I have seen him about three times in the course of my life.
Q. Look at that paper, ( marked X) whose handwriting is that? only look at the signature, if you please? A. I have seen what the subject-matter is; I think it is my signature—( looking at an account)—this is neither my writing or my signature—I see what it is, I have heard of it before—I have not seen what the contents are—Mr. Newhall told a gentleman in the gallery of this Court, the other day, that I had signed an agreement for 1,200 l.—I have never seen this paper before.
Q. Then, how do you know that this has anything to do with Mr. Newhall? A. Why, there is his name attached to it—I do not know what it alludes to—Mr. Newhall said there was an acknowledgment for 1,200 l.
Q. Did you ever see Mr. Newhall on the subject of any sum of money owing to Mr. Stanley? A. I never saw Newhall bnt once at the prisoner's—I have seen him in his own counting-house, in the presence of Mr. Petch—whatever transaction took place with the prisoner relating to Mount Pleasant was in his own house—there was a transaction with Mr. Newhall and the prisoner relating to Mount Pleasant—no mention was made of any claim of Stanley's of money due to him from my father or myself, or to the prisoner—an account was drawn out by Mr. Newhall regarding the deed Mr. Boys was going to prepare—he said, as Mr. Birch had fallen into so great an error in preparing a deed of reversion, it must be carefully guarded against, and he consulted with the prisoner what sum should be entered to make the next
deed I was about to execute legal—it was stated that there had been no consideration shown in the former deed.
Q. Was it stated also that the sums had not been filled up in the former deed? A.
