I know no deed but the one the Thompsons had executed—I know nothing about sums in a former deed—I never saw the Thompsons' deed of reversion, but they told me it was without consideration, therefore it was of no use—it was not stated that the sums had not been filled up in a former deed, I will swear that—the account was drawn out to insert a certain sum to make the deed that I was about to convey to the prisoner valid—the prisoner proposed that it should be 800 l.—Newhall said no, he thought two sums of 400 l. would be much better—I know of no account—there was no memorandum, or any paper written, in my presence.
Q. Did you not just now say there was an account? A. That is the account that you have; that is the one I heard Mr. Newhall had spoken of the other day, when he said he had an account for 1,200 l.—when I was at the prisoner's, the prisoner, Newhall, and myself were present, and alone—I saw Newhall writing, but not on such an account as that; it was a small piece of paper, which he took out of his pocket, the back of a letter, or something he was making memorandums, and certain sums were named—the prisoner said it should be 800 l., that would be sufficient—they first talked of 1,000 l.—Newhall thought it would be too much, and then the prisoner said it should be 800 l.—Newhall said he thought two sums of 400 l. each would do much better—that was to make the deed I was going to give to the prisoner valid—Newhall said that a deed without a consideration was of no use—what memorandum there was he made himself with his pencil, and Mrs. Blackwell objected to it—she put her head just inside the room; she was not in the room, but she called the prisoner out of the room—I heard her say something to him, and he said, "Oh, no, you may trust him with anything," and she went down stairs again—I did not write at all, I will swear that—the memorandum was made with a pencil, which Newball took out of bis own pocket.
Q. About how long were you arranging all this? A. The arrangement, whatever it was, was not more than an hour altogether; I dare say it was an hour—Mr. Newhall, in the prisoner's presence, went into the history of the prisoner's uncle, and the prisoner sat quite dumb—he came to me next evening, and I said I thought Mr. Newhall's conduct with respect to his uncle was very extraordinary indeed—he said he thought so too; he never was so astonished at anything in his life; and be then again repeated that his uncle had no funds, and he would have died in the workhouse if it had not been for him.
Q. Just look at this paper, you may read it carefully? A. There is not one word of truth in it—the sums of money that appear there as paid, were paid out of a sum of 300 l., for which we had sold our house at Kennington, in which my father had resided for fifty-two years, and I can give you the number of every note that was paid—it was in 50 l. notes.
COURT. Q. What is this paper then? A. It is a paper stating that Mr. Stanley has paid all these sums, every shilling of which was put into his pocket, and independent of that he makes a charge here for a sow and eight pigs, Which I paid myself to the man, and he never saw the man—it is entirely a fictitious account—we gave him 30 l. for the carpets—"acceptances for 25 l." we gave him—the stoves he bought of a Mr. Bell, in the new street that turns out by the Bank, I forget the name—he had 50 l. for that—the house painting was 115 l., of which we paid every shilling—we paid Collett 40 l. ourselves—"balance of a bill of 6 l. 15 s. 9 d.," we owed no such balance—"a bill of costs, 22 l. 19 s.," which we paid ourselves—he had the money—"Ssmith's bill,
for bell-hanging, 44 l. "—that was a man in Lewisham—we paid him, and it was paid by a bill which was taken up at Davies', the wax-chandler's, in St. Martin's-lane—"paid on account of furniture, bought at the sale at Shooter's-hill, 17 l. 12 s. cow and calf, 18 l. 15 s.; ditto, ditto, 19 l.; a red cow, 13 l. 5 s. "—they did not come to half the money—we have the account of them—they were bought of Mr. Longdon, of Shooter's-hill—it is true that Stanley accompanied us, but my father paid the money from his own pocket—from his own funds—here is "expenses to Wales, 50 l.," there is not a word of truth in it at all—this paper is not Stanley's writing, nor is it mine.
MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Now Miss Constable, I ask you distinctly, whether you ever settled an account with Mr. Newhall? A. I did not—no acconnt of any kind was ever prepared by him in my presence, more than some memorandum which he made, which I understood was two sums of 400 l. each, and nothing more that I was aware of—I have no doubt this ( the letter marked C) is my father's handwriting, and this also (D)—these letters have been picked out from hundreds of others, which have been stolen from our private papers, at the house at Lewisham—boxes, trunks, and drawers full—these letters are some of them.
COURT. Q. In saying, that the letter marked "D" is genuine, do you speak from the signature alone, or from the body of the letter? A. I have looked at them both, one is a letter written to Mr. Stanley in Wales.—( One letter was dated 1838, the other 1839.)
MR. BODKIN. Q. When your father and you first became acquainted with Stanley, he was a merchant of opulence in London? A. He was—in the year 1837, he occasionally called at our house at Kennington, but he did not become an inmate of our house till we went to Lewisham which was about June, 1838—he came to us with the intention of paying 2 l. a week for his board—that was his own proposal—he was very delicate about it—he said he would not come on any other terms—he never paid a farthing from the time he came, till he quitted—he wanted assistance constantly—he seemed to be straitened in his circumstances at that time, but he showed us a document, which would entitle him in a short time to 80,000 l.—it was a skin, which had much the apperance of one of these deeds—we never got any produce from that security.
Q. Were you aware of the existence of any such deed as this charged to be forged, when you had the conversation with the prisoner, in which he proposed that you should be allowed 30 l. a year, and that that was to form the foundation of some other deed to be prepared and executed? A. Certainly not—the conversation at which Newhall was present, was at the time it was agreed that the consideration should be first 1,000 l., then 800 l., and then two sums of 400 l. each, but I did not agree to any of them—it was their own suggestion—this account was not shown to me then—no paper was shown to me at all—there were no papers on either side—this is the first time I have ever seen such an account—I was certainly not aware of the existence of this forged deed when I executed this other deed—the prisoner made the items for the deed that I did execute—there were two small estates, one at Caermarthen and one at Huntingdon, and he remarked to me, "Here are two small properties, what will you do with them?" "Why," I said, "they are totally unencumbered, Mr. Black well?"—he said, "Well, so they are, but would you have any objection to my including them, and then I can act as your agent? I will collect the rents for you, and you will never have any trouble," and they were included—the 30 l. was to be founded on that part of the property which was under mortgage, independent of the Huntingdon and Caermarthen property—it was when I went to sign the deed, that I asked a
question about Phoebe Blackwell, and Mr. Boys told me she was a lame person residing in Derbyshire—I have since seen her, and she is not at all a person answering that description, she is a tall hale woman—the furniture at the house at Lewisham was worth about 600 l., but then the wine and plate, and other property was locked up, and not included—the house had been let furnished, at five guineas a week—I do not know what has become of the plate—I have never seen any of the furniture, except what I have been indicted about, and which I got, but that is so dreadfully mutilated, and in such an imperfect state, that I sold it to a broker for 3 l.—I was indicted for getting that by a forcible entry—there was no great quantity of plate left in the house—a little service, and nothing more—my father had a great objection to having much—it was worth, I should think, 30 l.—we had eighteen dozen of foreign wine there, and forty gallons of home made wine—I have not got any of that back—I understand they were very highly dissipated, and drank very extravagantly—the prisoner promised that when I was settled, and was willing to receive the furniture, he would give it all up—that was shortly before I signed this deed—after I had signed the deed, I made a great many applications for the furniture, but never got anything—this paper is my handwriting—it is the retainer, Which I gave Mr. Boys—it bears date 20th of Dec., 1841, but I did not sign it till March.
Q. Then in March you had a paper given yon, dated back to December, which you signed? A. Yes, I did not send the retainer by post; I took it to Mr. Boys.
MR. BIRCH re-examined. Q. Upon whose application did you part with the deed said to be forged? A. The application was in writing in the first instance—this is it—it is signed by the prisoner.
THOMAS HILL re-examined. This signature, "F. C. B. Blackwell," is the prisoner's writing.
MR. BIRCH re-examined. This is dated, "Southampton-street, Camberwell, 27th of Oct. 1841," and is addressed "Messrs. Sutcliffe and Birch"—it is—( reads)—"Gentlemen, As devisees and executors of the will of the late Mr. Joseph Stanley, deceased, we request and hereby authorise you to deliver the papers and documents which have belonged to him and his estate, particularly the deeds relating to the Lewisham estate, to Messrs. Hale, Boys, and Austen, on their receipt; and we will thank you to furnish them or us with the particulars of the demand you have against the late Mr. Stanley's estate."—This is signed by the prisoner and Phoebe Blackwell—that ultimately led to giving up the forged deed; but something else passed between the letter and the giving up of the deed—on the receipt of this letter, I think, we sent the prisoner Stanley's bill, and then we gave up all the papers belonging to Stanley, but we did not give up the deed in question, as we had not received the bill against Mr. Constable—Hale, Boys, and Austen then took out a summons, which I hold in my hand—they were then acting for the prisoner—it is a summons before a judge, to show cause why we did not give up the deed—the judge discharged the summons with costs—he said we had a clear lien on the deed for the debt against Constable, on the assumption that Constable having ordered the deed to be prepared would have had to pay the expense of it—Hale, Boys, and Austen ultimately paid the amount, and the deed was given up—I think I was in the way when the forged deed was taken away—I was not in the room when it was given up—I was in my own room—I did not see the parties who came to claim it—I gave Coward, my clerk, instructions to give up the deed—I authorized him to part with it, in consequence of the communication which he made to me—I gave it him for that purpose—I knew from Coward that the prisoner
was then in the outer office—I had not actual knowledge of it—after Mr. Freeman quitted Mount Pleasant, the prisoner, his family, and Stanley, were in possession of it—I do not think Stanley died there—I went to see him there when he was very ill—the prisoner and hit family did not live there after Stanley's death.
( The instrument charged to be forged, was without stamp or date. It purported to be an indenture of release and conveyance of certain hereditaments and premises from Richard Constable and Elizabeth Constable to Joseph Stanley, on trust, to pay to Aldridge, the mortgagee, 2000 l.: also certain legal expenses to Messrs. Parker, and Messrs. Sutcliffe and Birch; and to the said Joseph Stanley, a debt of ( leaving a blank for the amount) with interest there-on; the residue of the proceeds, if any, to be divided between the said Richard Constable and Elizabeth Hollis Constable.)
Evidence for the Defence.
The Letters marked A, and B, put in by MR. CHAMBERS, were here read:—(A) "London, 3rd of Aug. 1840.—Gentlemen, I have looked over M Stanley's account, and find at different times money advanced by him to the amount of 822 l. 12 s., therefore it will be necessary that a trusdeed should, without delay, be drawn up to secure it, and also that my other creditors should be paid in full what is justly owing to them. I am, gentlemen, your obedient servant, RICHARD CONSTABLE. To Messrs. Sutcliffe and Birch."
(B) "London, 18th of Feb. 1841. Gentlemen, I hereby authorize and direct you to supply the blanks in the trust-deed executed by my late father, Richard Constable, Esq., on the 31st of Dec. last, by inserting the sum of 822 l. 12 s. as the debt owing to Mr. Stanley, and the sum of—, as being due to yourselves. I remain, gentlemen, yours obediently, ELIZABETH HOLLIS CONSTABLE. P.S. There are several sums due to Mr. Stanley on an account, 53 l. 16 s. 8 d. paid to yourselves, and taxes due at Mount Pleasant for the same amount, 30 l. on my own account, and also servant's wages from Oct. 1840 to the present time." Annexed to this was the following:—"London, 24th of Dec. 1840.—Received of Mr. Stanley 51 l. 16 s. 8 d., being a loan of 50 l. lent on account of Mr. Constable on the 24th of Aug. 1839, and now repaid with interest from that period, for Messrs. Sutcliffe and Birch, 50 l., interest 16 s. 8 d. 51 l. 16 s. 8 d."
THOMAS NEWHALL . I live in Wood-street, Cheapside. In the year 1841, I had some transacttons with the prisoner, the result of which was that he was indebted to me about 1,258 l. altogether—my trading account, I think was about 300 l.—I took a shop in Southampton-street, Camberwell, for a nephew of mine—the prisoner afterwards had that shop from me and my partner—I believe part of the sum I have mentioned was the consideration for obtaining that shop, and there was one or two dishonoured bills—I knew a person named Stanley—I Cannot exactly remember to a month or so when the prisoner took possession of the Camberwell shop, but I think it was about Aug.—I think in 1841.
Q. Did you on any occasion go down and see Miss Constable there? A. I did—I went over to see Blackwell respecting my accounts—I think, as near as I can remember, that must have been some time about Dec., 1841, but I have not made myself acquainted with the dates—it was Dec. or Jan.—I will not be positive which—I was introduced to Miss Constable on that occasion—I was very anxious on account of my account being so very large, and I was introduced to Miss Constable respecting the amount of the property down at Lewisham—a conversation took place between myself, the prisoner,
and Miss Constable, on the subject of the property—it was stated either by Miss Constable or the prisoner, I cannot swear which, that the property at Lewisham had been disposed of to the amount of 4,500 l., or guineas, to ascertain the real value, and that the prisoner was in a position to pay my account—whichever stated that, the other heard it—I believe, as far as I can possibly state, Miss Constable was appealed to by the prisoner—Miss Constable stated to me that there was a certain deed conveying the property from Mr. Constable to Stanley; that she was perfectly aware that Mr. Stanley or herself, I will not be positive which, had given instructions to Mr. Birch to fill up a deed—I had been endeavouring to ascertain the value of the property, that I might take security from the prisoner.
Q. Did she say who were the parties to that deed? A. As far as my memory serves me, I understood that she had witnessed a deed conveying this property, which ought to be filled up—that she was a party to the deed—I will not say a witness—that she had witnessed, or was a party to a deed which required to be filled up, I believe, for the amount of 800 l. and upwards (I had not at that time put an execution into the prisoner's house)—the prisoner or Miss Constable, when both were present, in the room, informed me that such a deed was in the hands of Mr. Birch, and I believe the prisoner said he had not the means to settle Mr. Birch's account—I went to the prisoner's house again, and saw Miss Constable, I believe about a week or ten days after; during that time I had advanced the prisoner the money to take the deeds out of Mr. Birch's hands—on the second occasion that I saw Miss Constable, I made a memorandum—this is it—( looking at the account produced)—it is my writing; I copied that from receipts which were placed before me, which I was informed Miss Constable was in possession of—Miss Constable and the prisoner together, informed me so—I believe the prisoner produced the receipts—he laid them before me, that they were from Miss Constable—that was not in Miss Constable's presence—I copied them in another room—they were both present after I copied them, when I laid the copy of the receipts before them—I cannot say in whose handwriting the part beginning, "I have carefully compared," is—I saw Miss Constable write the words "Elizabeth Hollis Constable" to this account—that was done after I had copied this from the receipts, and after she bad seen it—I believe she stated that the deed I have referred to was as a security for sums of money which Mr. Stanley had paid on behalf of her father, and that the receipts were for those sums—I left this account at Southampton-street on that occasion—I do not recollect having it in my possession after that evening—I have never seen it since, to the best of my recollection, till this day.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Have you got the particulars of the prisoner's account with you? A. I have not, but I think my memory will serve me—I was security to Messrs. Costers, of Aldermanbury, to the amount of 300 l. or 400 l., for goods had by the prisoner—I did not put my name to a bill—I guaranteed the account, by giving a writing for any goods they might advance to the prisoner, to the amount of about 300 l. odd—I was only guarantee, I believe, for 300 l.—I was not guarantee for more than 300 l., further than I believe, my word of honour—I am not certain whether I was guarantee for exactly 300 l.—their account was 400 l. which I paid them off in cash—I paid them in the year 1841, I believe—I cannot speak positively to the date—I paid part, I believe, in 1841, and part in 1842—I cannot say how much I paid in 1841, unless I had my ledger here—I have not looked for the purpose of giving my evidence—I paid Costers in the whole, I believe, 490 l.—I considered I was bound in honour to pay that amount—I paid it by two or three acceptances—I have not got the bills with me—they
can be produced—I paid them through the hands of Sir John Lubbock—I was also security to Walters, of Wood-street, for 118 l. odd for goods the prisoner had in his trade, and Coster's was for goods had in his trade—I have not got an invoice of either of them—I cannot say that I never saw the invoice—I might have seen it at the prisoner's—I cannot swear whether I have or not—I also became guarantee to Sargentson, Weston, and Co., Scotch warehousemen, about the same period—it was in 1841, and I think about March—I cannot swear to dates, unless I saw my ledger, and saw the time—I paid them 94 l.—I was guarantee for about 100 l.—I believe it was in 1841 that I became guarantee to them—I am not positive, without referring to my books—the next sum I paid was 160 l. to Mr. Birch, if my recollection serves me, I advanced that on the prisoner's account, to get a deed out—I never got possession of that deed till it was handed to me by my solicitor, after a confirmation deed had been granted—I received it from my solicitor, I think about Jan. or Feb., 1842—Mr. Reynold's, of No. 10, Adelphi-street, Strand, is my solicitor.
Q. Pray have you ever been in any difficulties? A. Why, I certainly have been in difficulties—I have compounded with my creditors—the last time, I suppose, was about four months ago, about three months ago—I am not certain to the date—I was not a dealer in bills—my father-in-law was security—the whole of my debts was between 3000 l. and 4000 l.—I have only paid my trade creditors—the chief creditor was my father-in-law, for money which I received from him—he guaranteed the whole of the creditors 20 s. in the pound, with interest, which every creditor was paid in full—I think my trade debts might amount to 1100 l.—I will swear they amounted to 700 l.
Q. And that your father-in-law has paid 20 s. in the pound? A. Not on that account—not on the last trading account—I mean the first.
COURT. Q. You were asked what your debts were three months ago, you said, between 3000 l. and 4000 l. and that your trade creditors amounted to 1100 l. A. No—I wish to correct that.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. How much have you paid in the pound to your creditors, with whom you last compounded? A. About 5 s.—my father-in-law has secured it—I have not paid it—it is not due for about a fortnight or three weeks—he has given bills for it—he is a woollen-draper in Lawrence-lane—the whole of my debts then were between 3000 l. and 4000 l.—Messrs. Costers was not among my debtors—they were persons from whom I had goods in my line of trade—I was never insolvent before that to my knowledge, except when I compromised with my creditors, on account of advancing money on these deeds—that is nine or ten months ago—I advanced 1250 l.—I know Mr. Holding—I do not know that I owe him any thing—my father-in-law has Undertaken to pay the whole of my debts.
Q. On your oath have you not deposited among other deeds, belonging to the prisoner, Miss Constable or Mr. Constable, the deceased, this very forged deed, for a debt you owe him? A. I signed them over to my fatherin-law—I do not know whether he signed them over to Mr. Holding, or what he has done, but he left them there as a collateral security for money that he must have had from Mr. Holding no doubt—I believe 1000 l.
Q. What is the meaning of 300 l. on the trade account? we know something about it, therefore be a little cautious. A. The 300 l. was for goods supplied to the prisoner, for which I had taken acceptances from him, which were dishonoured—(I held a warrant of attorney on the prisoner taking the estate—Blackwell and Stanley were jointly security)—my name was to them—I was not sued on them—I have been sued, during the time I
was in the trouble I have spoken of, eight or nine months ago—I might have been sued four or five times, and I was sued about three or four months ago, for a small account of about 11 l., which I do not suppose ever came into the warehouse at all—I believe those are the only times I have been sued—I cannot swear to once or twice—I could very safely swear that I have not been sued twenty times—I will not positively swear to it—I took a lease of the place at Camberwell—that lease is now disposed of to a gentleman—I do not know where it is—I suppose it is in his possession—I delivered it to a Mr. Greenfield, a draper, living at Blackheath, I think, or Greenwich—he had it immediately after the prisoner gave up the premises to the trustees to whom he assigned his property—I never had any other house in which the prisoner carried on business—I had a house in Crawford-street—I cannot state what number it was—the prisoner had those premises for about two or three months—he carried on business there—I was obliged to pay his rent—I had those premises in my own controul about three or four months—I did not take them—I became answerable for the rent to a person named Simpson—the prisoner did not take them—Mr. George Oxborough took them—he is a draper—he failed—I do not know What has become of Simpson—I know nothing of the house at Chatham—I did not know that the prisoner had a house there until he made an assignment to his creditors—I have been to the prisoner's house at Croydon—it was in the principal street—I never had anything to do with that.
Q. Were you in the habit of being referred to, for the prisoner's character? A. Yes, in the general way of business—I should say perhaps for a dozen or fifteen accounts—I think not more—I will not swear it—I cannot say whether they were all for Camberwell—I did not give references for Chatham or Croydon—I believe I have been applied to, I have no doubt—I have not given references for Camberwell, Croydon, Chatham, and Crawford-street—neither did I know that he had a business at Croydon or Chatham.
Q. Why, did you not say just now that you had been to his house at Croydon? A. I did, but immediately I found he had a house at Croydon I closed his account—I did not close my own account till a long time after that—it might be a few months after—I will swear it was two months—I cannot positively swear to the time—I never carried on a retail shop—I was concerned in a retail shop—I had a nephew, for whom I took the house at Camberwell, in which the prisoner afterwards carried on business—my nephew did not fail—I disposed of the stock to the prisoner and his uncle—I and my partner did—M'Gooch was my partner—he retired from business with a fortune—I had been in partnership with him from about eight to ten months—he was a man of property—our agreement was to bring 4000 l. or 5,000 l. into the concern—I brought in no capital at that time—my capital was then employed in a business with Mr. Hill, who I had formerly been in partnership with, but I did not know that I had no capital at that time—I entered into partnership with Mr. M'Gooch, about March, 1841, I think—Mr. Hill was a wholesale Manchester warehouseman, and carried on business at No. 29, Wood-street—he is now carrying on business in St. Martin's-lane—he has disposed of the business in Wood-street—I cannot say how—he has not been my partner for these two years—we separated because the concern did not answer—we did not fail—I do not know what you may term failing—I paid my debts.
Q. Did not Mr. Hill tell you he would have nothing more to do with you, and insist on leaving you? A. I do not think Mr. Hill made such a remark—I cannot say that he said so—I am not aware that he ever made use
of any such language—I will not swear it—in the heat of passion, what may have taken place, I know not—he did not tell me that from my connexion with the prisoner and Stanley be would have nothing more to do with me—it was regarding private affairs between him and me, respecting my bringing more money into the establishment, and Mr. Hill likewise doing so—my father-in-law objected to advancing more money as long as I was in partnership with Mr. Hill—I had brought 1500 l. into the concern—that was lost—I did not lend that to Stanley—I did not know Stanley at that time, and knew nothing about his affairs whatever—I knew he was the prisoner's uncle—he was dressed as a respectable tradesman, I should say, when I saw him at the counting-house in Wood-street.
Q. Pray what are you now? A. I am not doing anything now, and have not done anything for about four months—I have been a shopman—I have not been in a retail shop—I have not been a shopman for the last four years—I joined Mr. Hill in Sept., 1839, and I have not been a shopman since, and never in the employ of any person—I left off being a shopman on joining Mr. Hill—I am now living at No. 13, Wood-street—the house is let to a Mr. Leith—it belongs to a Mr. Melville, a patent thread-manufacturer in Wood-street—I do not lodge there—I have the upper part of the house—the warehouses are let to Mr. Leith—I am a tenant until March—I am not there to take care of the house—I paid 150 l. a year rent—I have now let the house to Mr. Leith, from the 25th of Dec. last, but he occupied it six weeks or two months previously, on condition that I remained in the house until I met with a private residence—Mr. Leith paid no rent until the 25th of Dec.—I carried on business there as a Manchester warehouseman—I never assigned my stock to my father-in-law—I did not cart it away in the night—I disposed of my stock to Messrs. Sewell and Cross—I sold the whole of it at certain prices, with a discount off—I did not-dispose of the whole by the lump, but by stating certain quantities of goods at cost price, which I disposed of to them, with a discount off—I had had some of those goods for nearly eighteen months, I should say—the whole of the stock amounted to I should say 800 l.—perhaps 200 l. or 150 l. worth I might not have had more than a month—I will swear that I had not more than 150 l. worth a month before—I did not sell any of the stock to any one besides Sewell and Cross—I sold none in what is called the slaughter-house —I never sold my stock in that way—I swear that positively, not to anybody—I did not dispose of my stock the last time I compounded with my creditors—I had the management of that stock—my father-in-law did not have it—I swear that—I went on with my regular trading, my father-in-law being security—I disposed of it in the general way of business, to various houses round London.
Q. On your solemn oath, were you not in partnership with the prisoner? A. Never, to my knowledge—I have asked my solicitor, and he distinctly tells me that the agreement then entered into, never was of any effect, and is not binding—I intended to go into partnership with him—I do not know whether it was before or after this debt which I got the deeds to cover—the agreement between the prisoner and myself, as regards the partnership, was this: I had been speaking to my father-in-law that I wanted money; Mr. M'Gooch did not bring the money which he agreed to do, into the establishment, therefore, Mr. M'Gooch withdrew from it—the prisoner proposed to me that he would bring in 4000 l. to the establishment, and join me, providing I would produce the same—I agreed with the prisoner, on this condition, that if I paid Mr. M'Gooch out of the establishment, he would not enter into any agreement with him, unless he would have it binding to forfeit 1000 l. if he did not fulfil the engagement—I do not say that I have ever had 4000 l.
in my life—I have had money at my banker's—they have not been my creditors for a single shilling—they have trusted me and discounted for me to the amount of 4000 l. or 5000 l.—there were five bills taken as security for the stock, which my bankers discounted for me, which had the joint names of Stanley and Blackwell to them—Stanley became security to my solicitor, jointly with the prisoner, for the stock which I handed over to him, belonging to my nephew—the prisoner and I entered into an agreement for the 4000 l. or 5000 l. a-piece, I think on the 1st of Nov., 1841—the prisoner was to produce 2000 l.—I will swear that I took these deeds as security after that—I will swear that I did not get these deeds for the purpose of afterwards handing them to my father-in-law—I did not owe my father-in-law any money at that time.
Q. How long had you the deeds in your possession? A. Me and my solicitor together, I believe, for about a fortnight or three weeks—then I went on to my banker's, Sir John Lubbock, and told them that I wanted a promissory note discounted—they immediately discounted me a promissory note for 700 l., and one for 300 l., and I left the deeds, as a collateral security, with Sir John Lubbock—in about a month I paid the note back, for which they allowed me the interest off—I got that money in my trade—I was then carrying business at No. 13, Wood-street, as a wholesale dealer, not with Mr. Hill—it was twelve months, or more, after the dissolution with him—I have been a great many times to the house at Camberwell to inquire for the prisoner, always wanting to get money—he owed me a very large account at that time—I have seen the prisoner and his wife there on several occasions.
Q. On the occasion as to which you have been giving evidence, did you, the prisoner, and Miss Constable, go up stairs together? A. I went up stairs to Miss Constable and the prisoner—they were there—I never saw Miss Constable, to my knowledge, till I met her at the prisoner's—I am not certain whether that was the first time, or whether it was the night before—I saw her twice at the prisoner's.
Q. Did you on that occasion state to Miss Constable that you were glad you had found the means of trimming Birch, who was no lawyer? A. I do not know that I made use of such words as " trimming Birch," or any thing of the kind—I think I could safely swear I did not—I said that I had not confidence in Mr. Birch—I did not make use of the word "trimming" —I said it was my opinion that Mr. Birch was not a clever solicitor—I had no reason but my opinion for so stating—I have frequently heard gentlemen speak of one solicitor and another, and as my solicitor I certainly should not have confidence in Mr. Birch; that was my only reason, and from his conduct towards the prisoner, in not giving him information about the deeds and things in his possession—I have no other reason that I know of—I did not say that Mr. Birch had made a mess of a deed which he had had prepared for the Thompson family—I will swear that—I never mentioned the Thompson family, neither did I know any thing of any transaction with the Thompson family—I believe at the second interview I had with Miss Constable, when I copied the receipts which were produced to me, I said that my solicitor would not take these deeds as security.
Q. What deeds do you mean? A. I mean the deed that is in blank, and Miss Constable said—(I cannot say whether she said she had written, or Mr. Stanley, but that) instructions had been given by them to fill up the blanks—Mr. Reynolds was my attorney then—he knew from Hale, Boys, and Austen, that the deed was in blank—I authorised him to get me security, or otherwise to enter the warrant of attorney, which I had on the property in Crawford-street, and Southampton-street—it was not upon that blank deed
that I advanced the money to the prisoner, hut on a confirmation deed, executed by Miss Constable, Poebe Blackwell, and the prisoner, at Hale, Boys, and Austen's, which was handed over to my solicitor—I do not know that I said that Mr. Birch had made a mess of a deed, by not putting any consideration in to it—I believe I can firmly swear, to the best of my belief, that I did not—I certainly should not like to go further than that—I did not, to my knowledge, say that I was glad Miss Constable bad fallen into the prisoner's hands, as he would be sure to improve the estates for her—I will swear I did not make use of those words—I might have stated in the course of conversation, that I was very glad Miss Constable and the prisoner had arranged their affairs—I knew nothing of their private matters—I did not at that time know that the prisoner was a pauper, and not worth a shilling—he had acceptances of mine out at that time, over due, and unpaid, in the way of trading, I should suppose to the amount of about 500 l. or 600 l.—I will swear it was not above 1000 l.—there were five bills of 78 l., and three of those were returned to my bankers dishonoured—in the way of trading, save and except those bills which were returned to me, which I had the security of the warrant of attorney for, I believe the amount was not more than 300 l.
Q. Will you swear there was not 1000 l. overdue, and unpaid at that time? A. I should say it was not exceeding 1000 l.—I cannot swear that, without you will allow me to see my ledger—there was Coster's account then not wholly paid.
Q. Did you, or the prisoner, say that you thought 800 l. would be a good sum to put in the deed? A. I am not positive—I cannot say whether I did or not—I do not remember proposing 1000 l., and the prisoner saying, "No, put in 800 l."—I only stated to the prisoner in passing, that I thought if all they said was as available as they represented it to be, as Miss Constable and the prisoner both stated, independent of the property which was at Huntingdon, and in Wales, that I thought when I was making out these copies, 1100 l. was a sufficient sum for passing over the estate from one gentleman to another, and Miss Constable replied that they could prove still further receipts if I wished to copy them, but it being then near twelve o'clock at night, I felt uneasy at my wife waiting my return—I might have proposed to have 1000 l. put into the deed, but I cannot swear it—the prisoner might have said that he thought 800 l. would be better—I did not on that say that I thought two sums of 400 l. each would be better than 800 l.—I will swear that positively—I never suggested two sums of 400 l., or any thing to that effect—I never proposed the division of the 800 l. into two sums—I never proposed that the 800 l. should be divided—I will swear that to the best of my knowledge—I believe I did not—if I must swear one way or other I should distinctly say no—I can go no further—I am a man of property now—I do not see that I am bound to say to what amount, my creditors may be hereafter influenced by it—I have been insolvent—I have never been through the Insolvent Court—I have not been insolvent twice—my father-in-law became security, and I paid my creditors off myself—I asked them to give me time, after which I paid them interest.
Q. Repeat the conversation that passed with Miss Constable, you, and the prisoner, at the house at Camberwell, respecting the value of the property at Lewisham? A. Miss Constable gave me a statement then that the house had been disposed of, and was bought in again for, I believe, (as far as my memory serves me, to the best of my knowledge,) 4,500 l., and she then stated, to further prove the value of the house, that it had been let furnished at 200 l. a year, the house and grounds—I then inquired respecting how it came into possession of Mr. Stanley, and I was then informed that there was a reversionary
deed of sixteen nephews and nieces, who bad an interest in this estate, which was the cause of its being disposed of, to ascertain the real value—that reversionary deed is now jointly with those in the hands of the bankers—to the best of my recollection I have stated all the conversation, or the greatest part of it—I do not at this moment recollect every word—the conversation was very similar at the second interview, with the exception of copying these receipts—the receipts were not produced by me to Miss Constable—I cannot say whether they were produced to her by the prisoner—he brought the receipts and the different things down from Miss Constable into the room, and left them in the room for me to copy, and I did copy out the receipts, and that is a true copy of them—I afterwards went up stairs to Miss Constable, and asked her if that was a true copy—the receipts were not produced by me in her presence—I cannot say whether I saw them produced by the prisoner.
COURT. Q. Then have you told the truth when you said that the prisoner brought down the receipts? A. I was in the lower room—the prisoner stepped down from the sitting-room, where he was in conversation with Miss Constable, and brought them—he produced the receipts to me in the kitchen, where I copied them—there were various receipts—I should say ten or fifteen or more than that.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Take that account in your hand, and be cautious how you answer; it contains a list of items in your own handwriting? A. Yes—I see some writing at the bottom.
Q. What part of that writing will you on your oath swear you saw Miss Constable write? A. "Elizabeth Hollis Constable"—I will not swear to any thing else—I wrote the paper through—after I had written it I gave it, in the presence of the prisoner and Miss Constable—I cannot say to whom I gave it—I might have laid it on the table—they were both in the room—I do not know whether I handed it to the prisoner or Miss Constable, but I saw Miss Constable write this "Elizabeth Hollis Constable," in the room—I saw her write that—I put the paper down, or gave it to the prisoner or Miss Constable, I do not know which.
Q. How soon after did you see her name to it? A. I should say from twenty minutes to half an hour—I am not quite certain whether I was in the room all the while—I was in the room with Miss Constable I should say after this paper was signed—I was in the room until she signed it—I saw her write those words—I will not swear who wrote what is above it—I do not remember.
Q. Why you never left the room until she signed it; on your solemn oath, did not the same person that wrote this name write that which is over it? A. I cannot swear—I do not remember—I do not know why the prisoner did not put his name to it as a witness as well as me—I did not ask him—I was not acquainted with these things—I put my name because I was asked by the prisoner to do so—I am not certain whether Mrs. Blackwell or Miss Blackwell were there, or who was there—I know Miss Constable and the prisoner were present.
Q. But as you were a person to be benefited by the deed, why did not you ask them to witness it? A. I did not know I was to be benefited by the deed when that paper was signed—it was a transaction between the prisoner and Miss Constable—I cannot say what it was signed for—I was waiting there to get my account, for goods supplied to the prisoner in his trade.
COURT. Q. Did not the same person who wrote as you say, "Elizabeth Hollis Constable," write those several lines which are above it, beginning, "I have carefully compared?" A. I cannot swear that the same person wrote the other, not having noticed it—had I been aware that I was to come
as a witness I should have noticed it—I noticed her signature—I heard this read over in the room.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. By whom? A. I believe, to the best of my knowledge, I read it over myself as well as the parties there—I do not know who wrote it—I did not notice who was writing this—I afterwards heard these words repeated, after I had copied this statement from the receipts which were produced to me in the kitchen—I was not up stairs the whole of the time between this paper being taken away from the kitchen and handing it over to Miss Constable—I was not in the room the whole of the time between that period and her signing it—I was called into the room to witness her signature—I was then in the room, from the time she witnessed it till we left the house together to come home—I believe I was called into the room for the purpose of witnessing it—to the best of my belief I was—I am not certain who called me in, whether it was the prisoner, a servant, or who it was—I did not witness it immediately I went into the room—perhaps it might have been two or three minutes, or something of that kind after.
Q. Was it ready signed? A. I am not aware of that—when I came in, I saw Miss Constable take a pen, and write, "Elizabeth Hollis Constable"—I can swear positively that I saw Miss Constable take a pen from the ink-stand, and write, "Elizabeth Hollis Constable"—I was called in to witness it—I am not aware that I ever left the room from the time I was called in to witness it, till Miss Constable had signed it—I was not in the room when I copied this deed—I did not leave the room from the time I was called up from copying this deed in the kitchen, I mean this copy of the receipts—when I was called up stairs to witness that, I never left the room till Miss Constable bad signed it—I never saw Miss Constable write but once—I never saw Miss Blackwell write, to my knowledge.
Q. Just look at that name, "Elizabeth Hollis Constable," the word "Hollis" particularly, and tell me whether you cannot see a tracing of pencil, or something of that sort, upon which that signature is written? A. When I was called to witness this, Miss Constable, I believe, as far as my memory now serves me, might be in the act of writing this name, and on her doing so, I immediately said, "I will not be witness to this unless I see you take the pen and write "Elizabeth Hollis Constable"—part of this might have been written by her, but I would not witness it unless I saw her take a pen from the inkstand and write it over again, not again, I did not wish to say—as far as my memory serves me, she might have written part, but I distinctly swear I saw her take a pen and write "Elizabeth Hollis Constable"—I have come here to speak the truth.
Q. Look at the word "Hollis," do not you see the tracing of a pen or pencil in that word, upon which it has been written over again? A. I certainly do see some little signing, or something or other—I do not know what it is, but I distinctly swear I saw Miss Constable take a pen and sign her name—I see something, as if it might have been slurred over, or something of that sort—I do not see any thing slurred over in the word "Elizabeth"—there is a little soil, a little grease, or something of that sort on it—I do not know whether to call it tracing, or what—there appears to be a little soiling on it.
Q. Do not you see there is a distinct mark of something hard upon which the word "Hollis" has been again written? A. There is something—I cannot distinctly state what it is, or any thing of the kind—to the best of my belief, I told Miss Constable that I would not witness the signature unless I saw her write all the words "Elizabeth Hollis Constable"—I will swear I said I would not be a witness till I saw her write her name with a pen—I do not know
why I should say such a thing, but I have some recollection of stating words to that effect.
COURT. Q. Do you mean, on your oath, to swear that when you put your name to that paper, the signature, "Elizabeth Hollis Constable," was on it? A. Elizabeth Hollis Constable's name, signed, "Elizabeth Hollis Constable," was on that paper when I put my name as a witness—the words beginning, "I have carefully compared," were certainly on that paper when I put my name to it—I believe that I read them over—I will swear when she signed it they were there—I believe I can swear positively they were there that day—to the best of my belief, I will swear that those words were on the paper when I put my name to it—I recollect the words distinctly being announced to Miss Constable that night, upon which she stated, that if I required it, she could produce further receipts from her father's cash-book.
Q. What did you want further receipts for? A. To fill up the deed—this was after the deed had been taken out of Mr. Birch's hands, and was in blank, but which Miss Constable stated ought to be filled up, for 800 l. odd she thought was the sum which her father was indebted to Joseph Stanley—I am not certain whether Mrs. Blackwell was present when this was read over—I will not swear that she did not read it over—I will not swear that she did not write this in my presence—she might have written it, but I did not notice it.
Q. Will you swear that Mrs. Blackwell did not write the whole of that "Elizabeth Hollis Constable," and all? A. If she did I did not see it—I believe that part of the name, when I was called to witness it, either "Elizabeth," or something, was written—I then distinctly said, "I will not put my name to that till I see Miss Constable take a pen, and write 'Elizabeth Hollis Constable."
Q. When did this deed, the genuineness of which is now questioned, first come into your possession? A. After the confirmation deed had been executed, I think, about January or February, 1842, as near as I can remember—my solicitor placed it in my possession—I rather think it was sent down by the clerk to me, at No. 13, Wood-street—I believe that was the way—it was left at the warehouse for me—the first time I ever saw the deed was in the handing it over by Sutcliffe and Birch's clerk to a clerk of Hale, Boys, and Austen—I believe the prisoner was present then—I got possession of it from two to three months after that—the prisoner wished to borrow some money to pay off Sutcliffe and Birch—I said, "I will not advance you more money without you will give me security of some kind."
Q. Never mind that, but tell us what the arrangement was by which you got that deed? A. That Miss Phoebe Blackwell and the prisoner should allow me to hold the deeds, as a collateral security for money which I had advanced to pay off the lien—both Phoebe Blackwell and the prisoner said that—the prisoner said that he would allow me to hold the deeds belonging to his estate, as a collateral security for the money I had advanced—I entered up a warrant of attorney on the premises in Crawford-street—the agreement was, that I was to pay Sutcliffe and Birch's bill, and to hold this deed as a collateral security for my debt—I paid Sutcliffe and Birch's bill, I think, to the best of my recollection on the 1st or 2nd of Dec., 1841, late that evening—the deeds were handed to Hale, Boys and Austen's clerk, by their clerk—I had possession of these deeds, to the best of my recollection, in Feb., 1842—they were banded to me by my solicitor.
Q. Had you intermediately any communication with the prisoner before you got the deeds? A. On the morning after he had passed them into Messrs. Birch's hands, I asked him to hand me those deeds, as a collateral
security—he made a little demur in handing them over to me—he did not hand them over to me—I never saw him write to the effect that they should be handed over to me, but he did solemnly promise that I should have and hold them, as a collateral security—this was in Dec., and I got them, to the best of my knowledge, about Feb.
MR. BOYS. I am one of the firm of Hale, Boys, and Austen, solicitors, in Ely-place. In the autumn of 1841, we were employed by Mr. and Miss Blackwell, as their solicitors—we were to get some papers out of the possession of Sutcliffe and Birch—we afterwards got those papers out of their hands—their bill was paid before the papers were delivered—I saw Miss Constable after that—she subsequently executed this deed in my presence—( looking at the confirmation deed)—I am not the attesting witness to it, Mr. Martin is the attesting witness—I see Miss Constable's signature—I saw her three times on the subject previously—the prisoner was with her on those occasions—at the time I first saw her on the subject of preparing this deed, I had seen the other deed, where there are blanks—that deed was adverted to on the first occasion—I am not sure it was produced, but either the deed or a copy was there—the title was the subject of discussion, and this blank deed, and the effect of it, was one point under consideration—I have an original memorandum of my own—this was on the 10th of Dec., 1841—the opinion of a conveyancer had been taken previous to that, and here is the case—when I saw Miss Constable, the question that we were considering with Mr. Black well and Miss Constable was the perfecting the title of this estate, in consequence of the deed in blank having been obtained from Sutcliffe and Birch—the case, which had been laid before the conveyancer, was before me, and under discussion and consideration, and I have here the minutes which I made, when the prisoner and Miss Constable were present—this was on the first occasion—the prisoner brought the case that I had before taken the opinion of counsel on—I had given it to him, if I recollect right, the day before—I explained to Miss Constable the effect of the title—I cannot state the exact words I said—I called her attention to it—two other deeds had been prepared by Sutcliffe and Birch—they were settlements, apparently on her part, of this property, and the effect of these I explained to her as well as this, and she expressed regret that the title was in that state by means of the blank deed—she fully understood that a deed was necessary—I explained to her the parties who were interested under the trust, supposing the blanks for the sums had been filled in—Stanley's name was mentioned to her, as being a party interested—I mentioned it to her—the first part of the minute that I made here is the amount of the debts due, which were to be the consideration for the deed to be prepared—I explained to her that there were certain blanks in the deed, for debts to be entered to certain parties, and I explained to her the effect of the trusts, and that Stanley was the trustee—Stanley was then dead, but was represented by the prisoner, who claimed the interest which he had under his will—we were not her solicitors, but the Blackwells'—that was the first time I had seen her, but she was so satisfied with Blackwell, that she instructed us afterwards, at a subsequent interview, to prepare this confirmation deed—we were engaged for both parties, and had the drafts for counsel.
Q. Look at that account, did you ever see that whilst the deed was preparing? A. From a pencil memorandum at the back, and from recollection, this was in the office at the time the deed was prepared—I cannot say that this account was produced at either of the three interviews with Miss Constable and the prisoner—I am not sure that it was—there is a pencil memorandum of mine, "Received, Dec, 4th, 1841"—that shows it must have been in receipt by me on Dec. 4th—this is my own writing—I think I received
it from the prisoner—I cannot say whether it was produced in the presence of Miss Constable—the figures were acted upon as the basis of the draft, for inserting the sum in this conveyance—at the first interview, I adverted to the deed in blank—I have a minute that advances were made to Mr. Constable—I cannot say who said it—both assented to it—I made a minute of what passed on the first interview—in Miss Constable's presence, it was stated, that Stanley's executors had a claim of 1,100 l., and I took down, in the presence of Mr. Blackwell and Miss Constable 1,100 l., as due to Stanley's executors—Miss Constable did not deny that that was the fact, she assented to it—I made this memorandum at the time, of what took place at that interview—I took the whole of these minutes from both of them—the exact sum which it was agreed should be inserted as the sum due from the prisoner's estate, was afterwards fixed on and afterwards inserted, according to that account, when the deed was drawn out—but in her presence 1,100 l. was what was agreed to—I also understood from her or Blackwell (she assented to it) that advances were made to Mr. Constable, and also to her, after his death, before the deed in blank was executed, and after also, by Stanley—the prisoner and Miss Constable were both present at this time—Miss Constable did not deny it at all.
COURT. Q. Do you mean that it was both before and after the deed in blank was executed, advances were made to Mr. Constable? A. No, to Mr. Constable, and to Miss Constable after his death—that the claim of Stanley's executors consisted of advances made both in Mr. Constable's life-time and after his death—I was so told at the time—the last item in this account is Oct. 30, 1838—that was supposed to be after Mr. Constable's death in 1840.
MR. CHAMBERS. Q. At the second interview, had this deed of the 14th of Dec. 1841, been prepared? A. No—at the first interview Miss Constable expressed a wish to convey the surplus of this property to Mr. Blackwell alone, and it stood over for me to see Miss Blackwell, which I did—they were both interested under the will of Mr. Stanley, as executors and devisees—I saw Miss Blackwell in the mean time; and, at the second interview, reference was made to her being a party to the deed—I have got the memorandum of what took place at the second interview, with Miss Constable and the prisoner—we further discussed the matter, and I took instructions to prepare the deed—according to Mr. Kemp's, my conveyancer's opinion, in the best way—allusion was made to the sum which ought to have been inserted in the deed in blank, because the interests of some of the parties whose names were in the deed in blank, without sums, had to be attended to in the new deed—1109 l. was the sum mentioned to be inserted in the deed as the sum due to Stanley's executors—( referring to the draft)—that was to be taken as the sum due to his executors, and was to include the sum which ought to have been in the blank deed.
COURT. Q. How could that possibly be, when Stanley did not die till six months after Mr. Constable? A. As I was instructed, there was a sum due to Stanley—I was instructed that there was a debt, part paid in Mr. Constable's life-time, to him, and part to Miss Constable, after his death—it was only part of the sum that ought to have been in the blank deed.
MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Then, having received these instructions with regard to the preparation of this deed, did Miss Constable and the prisoner attend at the time it was executed? A. They did—that was not on the third occasion—I saw them once before—I saw them on the 13th the third time—the first interview was on the 10th of Dec., the second on the 11th, the third on the 13th, and on the 14th I saw them again—on the 13th I explained again to them the nature of the intended deed.
COURT. Q. What are the provisions of the deed? Do they follow the blank deed, and only alter as to the sums? A. It purports to be a conveyance by Miss Constable to the prisoner and Miss Black well, in consideration of 4000 l. and 8 s., composed of a mortgage debt of 2350 l., 1109 l. 7 s., the debt due to Stanley's executors, 161 l. 1 s., the amount paid to Sutcliffe and Birch, and 380 l. for the amount of our debt and interest, altogether making 4000 l. and 8 s.—there is no provision of any annuity or any surplus for her—she expressed her readiness to give the prisoner up any surplus there was, and there is a covenant by the prisoner and his sister to indemnify her for mortgage debts, taking them to themselves—there is mention of the estate in Wales—it is a very small estate indeed—Miss Constable and the prisoner both said it was a very small cottage—there is also some property in Huntingdon that is not valuable, worth 300 l. or 400 l., I believe, not more.
MR. CHAMBERS. Q. She attended, on the fourth occasion, for the purpose of executing this deed? A. Yes, and I believe she then executed it, though I am not the attesting witness.
COURT. Q. Before she executed the deed, did you explain it to her? A. I explained it to her fully—I did not read it in full, but I explained it to her, and she understood it—the consideration was supposed to be exactly 4000 l. of this deed; and when I came to add up these executed deeds, it came to more; therefore it became necessary to have the stamp altered, and she went into the calculation with me—I checked it with her, and the prisoner—I made her quite understand, that the prisoner was not to be her trustee—that she was to have no expectation—that she was to discharge her father's supposed debts and have nothing—she never gave me to understand that there were any debts of her father's beyond this mortgage debt, and those debts mentioned here—all the property was to go in liquidation of those debts—she was to be free of the debts.
Q. And also free of any inheritance,—to be abroad in the world without a shilling? A. Yes; but she always stated the property to me, and the prisoner too, as worth very little more than the mortgage debt—I did not take any pains to sift into the reality of any debt to the Stanleys—I did not know whether he was a man capable of lending 1 s.—I heard nothing of him—I have got a minute the first time I saw Miss Constable and the prisoner, that he (Stanley) was a friend of Mr. Constable, and acted for him—I only knew what they told me, that Mr. Stanley was a friend of Mr. Constable, and that he was an elderly gentleman—I understood that—I knew nothing except what Miss Constable and the prisoner told me.
Q. Did you not think, for the protection of a lady, ignorant of law affairs, that it was your duty to satisfy yourself that this consideration existed? A. I did, in the fullest way possible—I explained every thing to her, and I think Miss Constable knew very well what she was about, from the four or five interviews I had with her, and if she misled me I cannot help it—I acted for her afterwards in several things.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Am I to understand that you knew nothing of the circumstances of Stanley? A. Not in that extended sense—I heard of Stanley when I first came to Ely-place, and I had seen him up to 1836 and 1837, but since then I had lost sight of him altogether—I never was acquainted with him—I have seen him at Ely-place—I believe my predecessors acted for the prisoner's father and brother, in a heavy Chancery suit, and during the progress of those matters, Stanley came to the office on the part of the prisoner's father, who was a client of the house—I never in my life called at Stanley's lodging, that I recollect—I never heard of such a name as Mrs. Jenkinson, or of such a place as Swinton-street,
Gray's-inn-road—I never was there that I recollect—I never called there on Stanley, that I recollect—I will swear I never did—I never recollect calling on Stanley in my life—I never called and paid him 24 s. or 36 s. for him, nor ever paid his rent for him—I never called on Stanley, or paid him anything—I never knew Stanley—I never paid 24 s. or any other sum for his rent, at any house in Swinton-street, Gray's-inn-road—you take me so by surprise—these are questions which I had no idea of ever being asked, because I never called on Stanley, or knew anything at all of his fate—when I saw him last at Ely-place in 1836 or 1837, I did not know his circumstances, but I should say he was not a rich man—I did not know that he was in destitution and poverty—I did not expect from his appearance that he was rich—I can form an opinion of any body as to their apparent wealth and station in life—I am quite prepared to swear that I have never been to any lodging of Stanley's, or to any house where he lodged—I am quite at a loss to recollect what you can allude to—I think you must be under some mistake—I am quite prepared to swear it, and I do swear it to the best of my recollection—I never knew where Stanley was lodging, to my recollection, because I never heard of him except in some way connected with the Blackwells, the prisoner's father and brother—Stanley never came to my office in Ely-place, and sold me cigars or tea—I have never seen him there with cigars and tea to my recollection—he may have had some parcels with him, but I do not know that he was there with tea—if he came, he came on the business of the prisoner's father and brother, connected with the Chancery suits—I have looked to see whether I have any memorandum of his coming to our office, and that enables me to answer you the more clearly as to the years 1836 and 1837—I saw him in 1836 I think, or 1837, about some dispute he had with a Mr. Parker, and I learnt from him then that he was in the tea trade—he did not on that occasion produce cigars for sale—he never did such a thing, nor tea either—he told me himself, in Ely-place, that he was in the tea trade; he did not tell me where he was carrying it on, and I did not ask him—it was not my business—I never knew that he was in the habit of hawking tea about the streets—I never heard of it—the prisoner introduced Miss Constable to me.
Q. Did you know that she was coming? A. I am not sure whether the prisoner did not say he would take the case to her, and show her the opinion—I did not know for certain that she was coming—there was no appointment that I can recollect—I think when I gave him the opinion, it was to show to her—I do not know that he told me he would bring her to me—I will not swear he did not—it is very probable that he would bring her—I expected that he would—I have said as much, but there was no appointment made—I have been in partnership since 1834 or 1835—I never had such a deed as this put into my hands before—there were other deeds with it, and I never saw such a complicated title before; and so thought my senior partner, Mr. Hale, with whom I consulted—it was not left to myself alone.
Q. Have the kindness to tell me whether, when you saw that deed in blank as you found it, you entertained no suspicion of it? A. I thought it a curious deed, but not further than that—it is an extraordinary state for a deed to be in—I do not know that I entertained any suspicion of it, beyond what I have stated—I will swear I did not beyond what I have stated—from the appearance, I necessarily looked on it as an imperfect document, and a very curious document altogether—I did not look on it as a suspicious document, not with a view of supposing that, for instance, it had been forged or obtained improperly, or any thing of that sort.
Q. You thought that an attorney in his senses, would allow any man in his senses to execute a deed charging his estate with several debts, without setting
out what they were? A. We got the deed from Messrs. Sutcliffe and Birch, and we gave a certain credit to it from obtaining it from them—I do not know whether I should have suspected it, if it had not been obtained from them—it would depend upon how it came to us.
Q. Supposing it had been brought by the prisoner, the attesting witness? A. I do not know what I should have done in that case—it would depend on all the circumstances of the case, because affidavits had been made about these deeds by Mr. Birch as alluded to in his bill of costs—I took a retainer from Miss Constable—I did not require her to antedate that retainer.
Q. On your solemn oath did you not send her a retainer, and require her to antedate it, or antedate it yourself? A. Not that 1 recollect—I do not recollect doing it—I have no recollection of requiring her to antedate a retainer for three or four months—to the best of my recollection I did not—I sent it by the post, if I recollect right—I will swear I did not require her to antedate it—( looking at the retainer)—this is dated, Dec. 20th—I will swear that that was not signed in March, 1842—this was sent by the post at the time of the date—here is the envelope, and here is my memorandum at the back, of the same date—I will swear that this was not signed by Miss Constable in March—I will swear it came to me at this time, and that I had it in my possession ever since Dec. the 20th I think—I do not know that I sent it to her—I believe it to be written by Miss Constable—it came by post—I will swear that—I believe this envelope belongs to this letter—I will swear it—to the best of my recollection Miss Constable took a form of a retainer, and she said she would write it, and she sent it by the post—she took the form from me—I think she was going out of the country, or going away—it was necessary to prove her father's will, and I suggested she should leave an authority for us to act.
Q. Why could she not write it at your office? A. I think it was her own request to do it—I swear from recollection that that was the transaction—I could refer to bills of course, but this is a retainer that came by post at this time upon which we acted, and proved the will—I did not say she copied the retainer—I gave her a form which she took away—it was her wish to do it—I do not know why.
Q. Pray do you know Miss Phoebe Blackwell? A. I think I had never seen her to the best of my recollection, before about the autumn when we were acting for her and her brother as Stanley's executors—I never represented her to Miss Constable to be lame, and living in Derbyshire—I will swear that most positively, nor any thing to that effect—I never described her to Miss Constable in my life, because Miss Constable was there with her sometimes, very often at Ely-place—I will swear that on my introducing the deed to be signed by Miss Constable, she did not say, "Mr. Boys, stop here, what has Miss Black well's name to do with me? I do not know her"—as I stated before, Miss Constable in the first instance wished to convey the surplus of this property to the prisoner alone—she wished not to have the property conveyed to Miss Black well—she at first objected to Miss Black well's name being in the deed—I did not tell her that she was so mixed up with Stanley that it could not be prepared in, any other way—I told her that she was mixed up in this way, that they were joint executor and executrix under the will—that they were jointly interested under the will, and that it would be improper to make a conveyance to the prisoner alone—the instructions to counsel to prepare the draft goes on that principle, and I always kept her interest, and the prisoner's in view, for that very reason.
Q. Did you not think it a very unprofessional act to take upon yourself to act for a grantee and a grantor, the grantor being a woman unprotected?
A. Not if she perfectly understood what she was about, and if I had the assistance of counsel also—counsel prepared the draft for all parties—I had my instructions—I took on myself to act for all parties with Miss Constable's sanction—I do not consider that an unprofessional act, with the assistance that I had.
Q. How would the assistance of counsel which had for its object the preparation of that deed, interfere with the question, whether you should act for a party giving, and a party taking? A. Because counsel were instructed to prepare a deed for all parties, which is very frequently the case in the profession.
Q. Did you ever say to Miss Constable, "You appear to be a stranger, have you no friend but this Blackwell; had you not better consult somebody on your own behalf?" A. If I did not not say those very words, I did to the effect—I can go no further than my memorandum and my recollection—I asked her if she wished us to act for her, and she said, "Yes"—I am not sure whether I asked her if she wished anybody else to act for her—I cannot say—we did act for her afterwards—I made myself a trustee in the deed—I did not receive any money on its execution—I obtained money on it afterwards, the costs of it—that was not 150 l.—I think the costs relating to the deed itself were about 60 l. or 80 l.—I forget the amount—there were some other costs—that money was paid on the completion of the security, from the prisoner to Newhall—I do not know who paid it—my clerk settled it—what hand actually paid the money, I do not know—the agreement was a security from the prisoner and his sister to Newhall, and on our handing over the deeds, we were paid our bill of costs.
COURT. Q. By whose direction did you hand over the deeds to Newhall? A. As the solicitors of the prisoner and his sister—I have no written directions—it was a general direction when we were instructed to settle the agreement—there was an agreement to deposit, to which Mr. and Miss Blackwell were parties, and Newhall—I have not got it—I placed this deed in Newhall's hands when the agreement to deposit was executed.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. As between Blackwell and Newhall, you were not concerned for both those, I suppose? A. No, we were concerned for Blackwell and his sister—the agreement was the authority for handing over this deed to Newhall.
Q. On your oath, had you not verbal instructions from the prisoner to hand over the deeds to Newhall, and among them this deed in blank? A. Not from the prisoner alone, but from the prisoner and his sister, on the terms mentioned in that memorandum, which are very particular as to Miss Blackwell, because her interest is different, but still kept in view—Mr. Newhall's solicitor prepared an agreement to deposit, as an equitable security on this property—we were instructed by the prisoner and his sister to deliver over the deeds to Newhall with the agreement, and not without it—we were not justified or authorised in parting with these deeds except with the agreement, when the matter was closed—we were then justified in parting with them, by our instructions as solicitors to the prisoner and his sister—we had directions not to dispose of the deeds without the agreement—we were directed to deposit the agreement and deeds also.
Q. Look at that recital of the deed in blank, and you will see that it there states the deed was executed on the 30th of Dec., although it is not dated, it says "whereas, by an indenture of release bearing no date, but executed on or about the 30th of Dec.," by what instructions did you insert that recital? A. In consequence of a statement in the case—I see it is stated in the case that it was executed on the 31st of Dec.—then I have got a note, "We have, since
this case was drawn, been informed that the deed was executed on the morning of the 30th"—I do not recollect who told me so—I might have got this information from Mr. Hale, or the prisoner might have told me—this note is my own handwriting—I think I made it the day when the prisoner brought back this case, and when he and Miss Constable were together—I cannot say which of the two gave me the information that induced me to make the note—it was when they were present together, to the best of my recollection.
COURT. Q. Do you mean that you suppose it, or that you really have a recollection that the prisoner and Miss Constable were together when you made that memorandum? A. I do—I can throw back my mind to the fact, from the circumstance of the case being brought, and our going through the case.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. When do you say you made that note? A. I think on the 11th—the first day that the prisoner and Miss Constable were with me—that was after the case had come back from counsel—it is very frequently the custom to make notes when we find the fact is not correctly stated—I positively swear it was not there before the case was laid before counsel—I made it because it was an altered state of facts, because it was so very near the time of Mr. Constable's death—that was the subject talked of when Miss Constable was with me for the first time.
COURT. Q. What was talked of? A. The deed having been executed so shortly before Mr. Constable's death.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Will you swear you were not told to make that alteration by the prisoner? A. No, I will not swear it, because they were both together—I cannot carry my recollection so nice as that—I recollect that it was spoken of as a deed, executed very shortly before Mr. Constable's death.
COURT. Q. In what way was that noticed; as a strange thing, out of the ordinary course, or what? A. Only in ascertaining the date, there is no date to the deed, and I had to describe the deed in some way or other in the draft, and it was the only way of arriving at it—I only took it from what I was told by the parties—the date of this new deed is the 14th of Dec., 1841—this retainer was given a week after, as she was going away.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. When was the probate taken out? A. Not for some time; I forget when—there was a sum of money at Somerset House, which she obtained under that probate, and which we got for her—that was not contained in the deed—the will was not proved for some time, because there were proceedings going on in the Court of Chancery, and she did not want to be made a party to them—she came back after some time, saying she wanted this money from Somerset House; the probate was then taken out; we received the money, and paid it to her—about April she came again with a Mr. Petch, and she complained against the Blackwells, and talked about proceeding against them—she asked me to take proceedings against them, not at first, but afterwards; I refused; I told her we were not concerned for either party in dispute; I told her it was a civil remedy—I might have told her that her remedy was an action for trover —she complained that they had taken away all her goods and furniture—I think I did not hear that this was a forged deed until about that time, about the summer, when Mr. Petch and other parties came with her to our office—I never learnt it from my partner, Mr. Hale.
Q. Did you not learn from Mr. Hale that Dr. Lever had been with him, called his attention to it, and warned him against it? A. No, not that it was forged; I have some recollection of something being said about the deed being executed very shortly before Mr. Constable's death, that something
was not right about it; but as to hearing about its being forged I did not hear that till quite lately—I did not hear it till this new deed was executed.
COURT. Q. Surely you did, for you have just said that you made a memorandum on the draft, because you had been informed that it was on the morning of the 30th it was executed, the day before Mr. Constable died? A. Yes; but they are asking me whether I heard it was a forgery before then—Mr. Hale had not said anything to me about its being irregular—he had not said anything about it before the new deed was executed.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. And you had no notice, through Mr. Hale, or anybody else, that the deed was a forgery? be cautious what you swear. A. No, not that I recollect, not till about the time I told you; I do not recollect that it came to my knowledge before the confirmation deed was executed.
COURT. Q. This is a very important fact; nobody could mistake whether when they were about to get a woman to execute a fresh deed, they had notice that the former was a forged one? A. No, I had not at the time this was drawn.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do you mean to pledge your oath to that? A. Yes, positively—I did not know by information from Mr. Hale that there was anything wrong about the deed till after the deed of confirmation was executed—I did not know at the time I took the conveyance to the prisoner and his sister, that, with the exception of this property, they were paupers; I did not inquire—I did not know how they got their living—they were not able to pay me, except through Newhall, who advanced the money for the purpose—I do not think we had required to be paid for what we had done before that time; I will not swear we had not, without referring to my memorandum—I did not at that time know that they were in such very needy circumstances; I knew the prisoner himself was in difficulties—I knew that he had been obliged to quit the house at Camberwell, that there were proceedings at law against him, and that there were overdue and unpaid bills against him; I knew that, at the time of the execution of the deed, but previously I had known nothing whatever.
Q. Did you, under these circumstances, knowing him to be in difficulties and necessities, and this woman to be unprotected, and acting for both, think that he was a fit man to have so important a trust as that reposed in him? A. If their statements were right, and their figures, she was giving him very little; there was very little surplus made out.
COURT. Q. Why was she to give him anything? A. There was the mortgage; in fact, the property was worth very little; in fact, she knew so much about it that she wished to get rid of it; there was the possibility of her being made a party to a Chancery suit; there was a mortgage of 3,000 l. odd, and a legal debt of 300 l. odd, on this very property, and a Chancery suit going on at the time, and Mr. Kirk had been endeavouring to find her out to cite her, and make her a party to the suit, which she was desirous of avoiding; therefore we considered that the prisoner was taking but very little under this deed.
MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Did you go through this case with Miss Constable and the prisoner? A. I did; I read it through, and on the information I received as to the morning of the 30th, I wrote this on the margin—Miss Constable did not deny the accuracy of any of the statements in this case.
COURT. Q. Are you acquainted with the handwriting of Mrs. Blackwell?
A. No; I do not think I have ever seen her write, or the prisoner—this case, and the opinion, were read over to Miss Constable.
---- MARTIN. I am the attesting witness to this deed; I saw Miss Constable execute it.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Were you in the room all the time, or merely called in to witness it? A. Merely called in; I know nothing of what passed.
COURT to MR. BOYS. Was this deed with the blanks in it, with the signatures and the attestation, ever drawn to Miss Constable's attention? A.
