I am not aware that it was—there were two other deeds in her favour, executed, I think, in Feb.—her attention was called generally to deeds—I never called her attention to the fact, that as this was a deed executed by her father on the day before, or the very day of his death, whether she recollected the transaction, or who were present—I never anticipated any inquiry of this kind—I cannot say exactly which told me, but I collected from them that it was the 30th—in the case of a will I should ask whether it was executed on the very day of the man's death—the only question about this deed was the date of it—it was in blank—it had no date; and of course that was the subject of conversation, and the effect of that deed with the two others—I do not recollect that I asked whether the prisoner had ever seen either of the subscribing parties at the time—before this second deed was executed I had reason to believe that the prisoner had seen Miss Constable and her father before the beginning of the year 1841—it did not occur to me that I was making at deed in favour of the prisoner, who was the attesting witness of the other deed, in favour of Stanley, because he was interested under Stanley's will.
( The Deed of Confirmation was here read. It recited the whole of the former deed, with the addition of the blanks being filled up, it stated that there (was a debt of 1109 l. 7 s . due to Stanley from Mr. Constable; and finally conveyed the property mentioned in the first deed to the prisoner and Phoebe Blackwell, for the purpose of meeting the demands upon the estate, including the above sum of 1109 l. 7 s.)
The account referred to, was as follows:
"Mount Pleasant House, Lewisham.—Richard Constable, Esq., Dr. to Joseph Stanley.—1838, Feb. 1st, paid for carpets, as per annexed, 30 l.; June 2nd, paid acceptance for furniture, 25 l.; June 7th, do. Mr. Buffin, 20 l.; July 17th, Stoves at per bill, 30 l.; Radburn, for painting house, 115 l.; Collett, 40 l.; do. balance of bill, 6 l. 15 s. 9 d.; Hind's bill of costs, 22 l. 19 s.; Aug. 16th, Smith's bill for bell-hanging, &c., 44 l.; Paid on account of furniture bought at a sale at Shooter's Hill, 7 l. 12 s. 3 d.; Sept 20th, cow and calf, 18 l. 5 s. do. do. 19 l.; red cow, 13 l. 5 s. cash 20 l.; to do 30 l.; Sept. 23rd, expenses to Wales, on account of Richard Constable's affairs, 50 l.; Sept. 23rd, paid acceptance on account of Foakes, 31 l. 7 s. 6 d.; Oct. 19th, do. Mr. Gibbons, carpenter, balance of account, 20 l.; do. Mr. Gray, for plumbing, 7 l. 16 s. 3 d.; 31st, sow and ten pigs, 8 l.; gilding, 6 l. 6 s. Dec. 26th, cash advanced to Richard Constable, 400 l.; labourer's wages from June 26th to Dec. 26th, at 15 s. per week, 18 l.; do. Gardener for the period as above, 15 s.,—18 l.; for haymaking during the years 1838, 1839, and 1840, 10 l. per year, 30 l.; Mr. Clark, for churning utensils, 10 l. 10 s.; Dec. 27th, paid Messrs. Sutcliffe and Birch on Constable's account, 53 l. 16 s. 3 d.; 1838, Oct. 13th, paid Messrs. Bowman and May, 5 l. 7 s.; to Robert Vice, for hire of gig, 1 l. 16 s.; to Mr. Rose for measuring garden, &c., 1 l. 1 s.; paid taxes 5 l. 10., Total 1109 l. 7 s."
"I have carefully compared the foregoing account with the entries made in the cash-book of my late father, Mr. Richard Constable, and find them to correspond therewith; and I therefore admit this account to be correct, and
that there is justly due from the estate of my late father to the estate of the late Mr. Joseph Stanley the above sum of 1109 l. 7 s.; signed 'Elizabeth Hollis Constable, Friday, Dec. 3rd, 184 l.; Witness, Thomas Newhall, 13, Wood-street, London.' "
COURT to MISS CONSTABLE. Q. Take that paper into your hand, and carefully read the memorandum at the bottom, above the signature? A. I read it before—the signature is decidedly not my handwriting—if any one has sworn that he saw me write it at Camberwell, he has sworn falsely—the words beginning" I have carefully compared," are not my handwriting, not any part of it—I never saw the paper before this evening.
Q. Look at the very beginning, and explain what those items mean? A. (Reading)—"Mount Pleasant House, Lewisharn.—Richard Constable, Dr. to Joseph Stanley. 1838, Feb. 1st, paid for carpets, as per annexed, 30 l."—I suppose that item was obtained from Stanley's memory, or perhaps a bill delivered to him—I should think it was taken from an account that Stanley kept—he and I bought the carpets together—we went together to choose them, and he had 30 l. given to him to pay for them, by my father—there was a trifling balance, which he never accounted for—the next item is, "Paid acceptance, 25 l., for furniture"—that was paid by a bill, and I have the acceptance, but not with me—it was to a man named Rushton, in the Kent-road, accepted by my father—"Mr. Buffin, 20 l., stoves as per bill 30 l."—they did not cost anything like 30 l.—he had the money to pay for them.
Q. Had he any funds of his own, by which he could have paid for these several things? A. I have ascertained since, that he had not 1 s.—he had nothing—"Radburn, for painting house, 115 l."—I paid that man myself in the house, and Stanley was not present—he brought the gentleman down, and gave him a promissory note for the amount, and I paid him in his presence—I do not know the gentleman's name—he lived somewhere at Rotherhithe, and this gentleman was his landlord—"Collet, 40 l."—he was Mr. Abraham Constable's coachman, and received it from my father—"Hind's bill of costs, 22 l. 19 s."—Mr. Hind was the coroner of Sittingbourne, and these were the costs accruing from Mr. C. Pratt signing the deed of reversion, in conjunction with the Thompsons—my father paid that—I was not present—he supplied Stanley with the money to go—he could not have gone without it—"Smith's bill, for bell-hanging, 44 l."—that was paid by an acceptance of Mr. Davies, the wax-chandler, in St. Martin's-lane—"Paid an account of furniture bought at Shooter's-hill, 7 l. 12 s. 3 d."—Mr. Langdon bought the furniture, I believe, with a larger sum than this—we bought a cow and calf, but the sum waa 9 l.—he has doubled it—" Ditto, dtto, 19 l."—it was rather an aged cow, and we had it for 9 l.—'"A red cow, 13 l. 5 s."—I think we gave about 10 l. or 11 l. for it, but Mr. Fluker, I believe, has the items—"By cash, 20 l., by ditto, 30 l.," but then it was cash given to Mr. Stanley not as given my father—"Expenses to Wales, on account of Richard Constable's affairs, 0.5 l."—I have the numbers of all the notes—he received a sum of 300 l., and I gave it him myself—"Sept. 23, paid an acceptance, on account of Foakes, 31 l. 7 s. 6 d."—that was an acceptance that I went and paid Foakes myself, at the Greyhound, at Croydon, on Saturday, the day he always calls to collect his rents—he is the land-steward of Captain Cater, of Tulse-hill—"Mr. Gibbons, carpenter, balance of account, 20 l."—that I paid Gibbons, and I have, no doubt, his voucher for it—"To Mr. Gray, plumbing, 7 l. 16 s. 3 d."—it was something more than 7 l., but not "7 l. 16 s. 3 d."—"For a sow and ten pigs, 8 l."—I paid it myself—Stanley was not on the premises at the time, and the gilding was the gilding
for some pictures, which I paid to a man at Kennington, named Boswell, and took his receipt for it—"Cash advanced to Richard Constable, 400 l."—he did not want any such cash—he had enough of his own, and Stanley was not in a condition to lend it him, if he had wanted it—"Labourer's wages, from June 26th, at 15 s. per week, 18 l."—that was paid by Mr. Freeman, of Coleman-street—it was deducted from the rent, therefore my father could not owe it him; and the gardener likewise; Mr. Freeman told me himself that he had paid the gardener, and deducted it from the rent that was due to us, consequently it was not paid by Stanley—"for hay-making, during the years 1838, 1839, and 1840, 30 l."—he sold a stack of hay that belonged to as for 57 l., for which he never accounted—he sold the grass, on the land for fourteen guineas, and supposing that the hay-stack was where I left it, I gave him 12 l. to pay it last season, therefore he had no claim to make for hay-making—"Mr. Clark, for churning utensils, ten guineas"—that was a man in Fleet-street—I paid him, and have oil receipt for it—he did not know Stanley, and I believe never saw him in his life—"Paid Sutoliffe and Birch, on Constable's account, 53 l. 16 s."—he borrowed 50 l. of Mr. Birch, and I think Mr. Birch said he returned it to him—there is not one farthing that is owing to Stanley—I do not know in whose handwriting this account is—no part of this paper was ever read out to me at the prisoner's house at Camber-well—I never saw it till I came into Court to-day.
Q. Mr. Boys has said that your attention was called to the amount due, namely, 1,109 l. therein stated? A. I beg Mr. Boys's pardon—I never had my attention called to any such thing—this case was never shown or read to me by Mr. Boys—I never had any case shown to me there—he never called my attention to his having taken counsel's opinion respecting the new deed—I dare say I should not have understood it if he had.
Q. Did the prisoner ever state in your presence and that of Mr. Boys, that the deed which is now called forged, was executed on the 30th of Dec.? A. I believe I can truly swear, that I never heard the prisoner mention my father's name in any way in his life—I never gave Mr. Boys to understand that the first deed was executed on the 30th of Dec.—I could not have told him so, nor should I have suffered such a statement to have passed unnoticed.
Q. This confirmation deed which you executed, conveys to the executor and executrix of Stanley, all the property, to pay the supposed creditors of your father, and states that his estate is indebted 1109 l. odd to Stanley's executors; was that explained to you by Mr. Boys? A. No—I had no explanation of the kind—I never entered into it—it was never suggested to me, or ever suspected—it was given to the prisoner under a very different impression—I agreed to make it over to him under a very different impression.
Q. What was the expectation held out to you to make over the property to him? A. That he would give me a deed of annuity of 30 l. a year, that he would clear the estate of the incumbrances bearing on it of 2,300 l., I think, and Mr. Parker's bill of costs.
Q. How came you not, when executing the deed, to say, "I see nothing about this 30 l. a year that I am to have?" A. I did not read tie deed myself, nor did I hear it read, except a small part, which took a very short time, and on hearing Miss Black well's name I objected to it—I omitted to state when I was in the witness-box before, that on my calling at the prisoner's, at the time Mr. Newhall was there, the prisoner took an opportunity of telling me that Mr. Newhall was a man of 700 l. a year, and was the gentleman who was to advance the money from the City, to clear the incumbrances, and he was rather doubtful of my communicating with Mr. Newhall—Mr. Newhall left the house with me, and the prisoner requested that if he inquired where I
lived, to tell him that I lived at Brompton, but he did not make such an inquiry—while I was there Mrs. Black well just put her head into the room, and called her husband into the passage, and I heard her request him not to allow Mr. Newhall to make out the sums to be entered in the deed.
Q. Then he was preparing some deed? A. He took a little side of a note or something of that kind from his pocket, and a pencil, and the prisoner and he had a conversation as to the amount that ought to be entered in the deed to make it valid—this account was not produced to me—it was a small piece of paper much less than this—this was not produced to me as the result of any memorandum made—Newhall was taking some step to suggest a sum to be put in the deed.
Q. What did you understand, any sum at all was to be put in the deed for, unless your father's estate was indebted to Stanley? A. Because I understood that the deed of reversion, that had been prepared for the Thompson family by Mr. Birch was not available, because it showed no money for consideration, and Mr. Newhall said that that was an evil that must be guarded against in preparing the one that was to be given to the prisoner—I was told it was necessary to introduce a consideration—I did not understand that the introduction of that consideration pledged me to pay a debt—no debt was explained to me, or such a thing suggested—I thought it was a merely nominal thing, that it was for the sake of validity, and I was the more, confirmed in that by asking a friend, what would be the fate of a deed that showed no money consideration, and he said it would be valueless—I was not aware that the sum to be inserted in the deed was to go into the pocket of Stanley's executors—I heard no more about it, and I did not know it was inserted, or that any thing more was said on the subject—the estate was in a most ruinous condition, in consequence of the prisoner not having earned ont the views he proposed, and no money I am sure had been raised on it, except by selling hay and grass, and what would raise a little ready money, or perhaps the furniture—his proposal was that it should not be sold—I did not understand the operation of the deed which I was called upon to execute—I did not know that I was making an absolute conveyance of all my property to him.
Q. Then why were you to have 30 l. a year by way of annuity, if it was of no benefit to him? A. It was then at his own option to do as he pleased—he could sell it if he pleased, but he did not bold out that view to me—he said he had the means of turning it into the same comfortable residence it had been, for himself—he was to have it if he had given me the 30 l. a year, and fulfilled his other engagements—I did not dispute that at all—if he had paid me my 30 l. a year and discharged the estate from its incumbrances, I meant him to have the property, and I did not consider he would be very greatly advantaged—he has never paid 1 s. of any thing—he has suffered every thing to go to ruin and destruction—I imagined that the debt of 1109 l. was only a consideration to give validity to the deed—I did not understand that it meant to appropriate that as a supposed debt to Stanley—I never had that pointed out to me.
GUILTY of Uttering. Aged 25.— Judgment Respited.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18430130-815]
815. DANIEL HOPKINS was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of Jan., 1 mare, price 3 l., the property of Thomas John Bolton.
JOHN SEABROOK . I am in the service of Thomas John Bolton, a contractor for post riding-horses, in Brunswick-street, Stamford-street. The prisoner has been a post riding-boy—on Tuesday, the 21st of Jan., the witness Gibbs brought the prisoner to me at the Post-office, with a mare
—I knew it to be Mr. Bolton's mare—Gibbs said, in the prisoner's presence, that he had brought it to him, and sold it him for a sovereign, and asked me if the prisoner was authorized to sell it—I said, certainly not, he must take him over the water to master's premises—he said a boy named George Morgan stole it, and said if he would sell it he would give him 1 s.—I gave him in charge.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Are you the only person belonging to the establishment here to-day? A. Yes—the horse was not missed at all—it was valued at 3 l.
WILLIAM GIBBS . I am a general dealer. On the 31st of Jan., the prisoner was coming down Cow-cross, on the mare, which was very lame indeed—I asked him whether it was for sale—he said, "Yes"—I said, "Who does it belong to?"—he said, "My master"—he told me who his master was—I asked him where his master lived—he said, up by Sadler's Wells—he was a green-grocer—I asked him the price—he said be wanted 30 s.—I said, "Is that the lowest?"—he said, "No, master says I may take 1 l. "—I said, "I will go along with you, and see whether it is all correct"—I gave him the 1 l., and went with him towards Sadler's Wells, to see if his master lived there—before we got there, he ran away from me—I ran, caught him, and asked what made him run away—he said he was going to leave bis master, and did not wish to give him the money—I asked him for the money back—he gave it to me, and told me he had stolen it, and who he brought it from—I took him to the Post-office—the mare has been left in Sharp's-alley, in care of my wife.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did you mean to swear he told you he had stolen it? A. He said him and another had stolen it from Mr. Bolton's place—that was not in Seabrook's presence—it was before he came up—he said no further about his master, but that he was going to leave him that night—that he should stick his wages to the money—he told the Magistrate he had stolen it—that was taken down in writing, as far as I know—I heard my deposition read over to me—I am no scholar, but this is my mark—I heard him say to Seabrook that another boy stole it, and he sold it for him, but that is not what he told me—the stables the horse is supposed to come from are over the water.
( The witness's depositions being read, omitted to state that the prisoner said he had stolen the mare.)
WILLIAM GIBBS . I said so at the office—what I said there was nothing but the truth.
HANNAH WHITE . I am the wife of William White, foreman at Mr. Bolton's premises, at Lambeth. I have seen the prisoner—I know a boy named Morgan—I saw him come into the yard, put the halter on the horse, and lead it out.
Cross-examined. Q. Was he alone when he did that? A. Yes, quite—I saw him take it out of the yard—I did not see him take it any distance—there was nobody in the yard but me saw him come in, and put the halter on the mare—I did not see the prisoner or anybody else there.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430130-816]
816. WILLIAM CARPENTER was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
ANN WILLIAMS . I lodge in Mr. Judd's house, in Blackfriars-road. I purchased a washhand-stand of the prisoner, at Mr. Judd's shop, on Saturday, the 19th of Nov.—I paid 4 s. for it.
Cross-examined by MR. HEATON. Q. How long have you known the prisoner? A. Since the 3rd of Sept.—I have paid my rent twice to him—I did not see Mr. Judd daily—sometimes he was not there for two or three days together—sometimes not from one week to another—he has another shop at some distance—I have lived in the house with my husband since the 6th of May—we were on friendly terms with the prisoner—I did not ask him to get me this stand at the cost price—my son-in-law sent me to buy a double washhand-stand—I do not know that the prisoner ordered it of the maker for me, nor that 4 s. is the maker's price for it, and the ordinary charge 6 s.—the prisoner did not say he had got this from one of the persons who made for them, on purpose for me—when I asked him he said he had not got one at that price in the house—he did not say he would get one—it might be a week or a fortnight before I got it—I did not tell him that 4 s. was as much as I could afford—I do not recollect that he said he would get me one—it was not for myself, and of course I took no more trouble about it—I did not ask him to get one made for me—he never said he would get the person who made for Mr. Judd to make one for me, or anything of the kind—there was no other person present when I paid for it—I did not see the stand in the shop, nor see it brought up to my room—it was painted, and looked quite new and clean—I do not know whether it looked as if it had come from the maker—I know that a stand would acquire a darker hue by remaining in the shop—it did not look as if it had been in a broker's shop.
SOPHIA MARIA UNDERWOOD . I live in Fore-street, Cripplegate. On the 5th of Dec. I bought at Mr. Judd's shop, twelve yards of carpeting at 2 s., 2 d. a yard, and a hearth-rug for 12 s. 6 d.—my daughter was with me—to the best of my belief the prisoner is the man I paid—he gave me this card ( producing it) as a bill—the things came to 1 l. 18 s. 6 d.
Cross-examined. Q. Was not Mr. Judd present? A. The prisoner showed us a hearth-rug, which was 12 s. 6 d.—we said we would rather have a darker, and he brought us one which he said was 14 s. 6 d.—I said I would give him 12 s. 6 d. for that—Mr. Judd came into the shop, and the prisoner showed him the mark on the back, and he then left the shop—I said, "If they won't take 12 s. 6 d. for it, I won't have it," and the prisoner rolled it up, and we had it—after the 14 s. 6 d. rug had been shown to us, the prisoner and Mr. Judd spoke together—we had agreed for the carpet before—I did not hear the prisoner ask Mr. Judd whether he would take 12 s. 6 d., nor tell Mr. Judd the quantity of carpet we had bought—it was then rolled up—it was a Kidderminster carpet—I did not hear the prisoner say there were twelve yards of it at 2 s. 2 d. a yard, or ask whether he might let the rug go at 12 s. 6 d.—he rolled it up, and then Mr. Judd left the shop.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Had you been long in the shop? A. No, I was only there about a quarter of an hour—Mr. Judd came in at a side door.
ELIZA ANN UNDERWOOD . I went with my mother to Mr. Judd's shop, on the 5th of Dec.—I saw her pay for the carpet and rug—I think the prisoner is the man she paid 1 l. 18 s. 6 d. to—Mr. Judd came in while the bargain was going on—he staid a very short time—he left us together.
Cross-examined. Q. You got the rug for 2 s. less than he asked for it?
A. Yes—when Mr. Judd came in, we were asking the prisoner, "Have you any rugs?"—I do not think Mr. Judd was in the shop that minute, but he came in directly—the prisoner took out three rugs—we looked at them, and thought the first one at 12 s. 6 d. was too light—then for the darker one he wanted 14 s. 6 d.—the prosecutor came into the shop, and the prisoner said, "The lady is no bad judge"—I do not think he could hear us bid 12 s. 6 d. instead of 14 s. 6 d.—he passed quickly by—the prisoner described the size of
the rug to him, but not the price—I think it wat about eleven feet—I do not think he said, "They want it for 12 s. 6 d."—I saw him speaking to Mr. Judd, but I cannot say whether it was before or after we offered the 12 s. 6 d.—the carpet was cut off, and was lying there at the time—the prisoner might have said to Mr. Judd, "The ladies have bought twelve yards of carpet," but I do not remember it—all the transactions took place openly—we were in the open shop—the road is a great thoroughfare—I think it was about four in the afternoon, which is a very busy time—the prosecutor passed quickly by, he did not stay in the shop, he was busy with a cart, with some things at the door, to the best of my remembrance—I saw my mother put her hand into her pocket, and hand something to the prisoner—I do not think the prosecutor was by at that time—after we agreed to have the rug, the money was paid—I do not think anything more was said about the price after the prisoner spoke to the prosecutor—my mother had offered 12 s. 6 d.—it was wrapped up with the carpet immediately, and the money paid—I think the prosecutor was not in the shop at that time—I only saw him once.
RACHEL MORGAN . I am the wife of Lewis Morgan; we keep a dairy. On the 3rd of Dec. I went to Mr. Judd's shop, and bought a looking-glass for 2 l. 15 s. of the prisoner—I paid 2 l. at the shop, and when the prisoner brought the glass home I paid him the 15 s.—this is the receipt—( producing it.)
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know Mr. Judd? A. I know him now—I did not see him at that time—I bought the glass on a Saturday evening, between seven and eight o'clock, which was a busy part of the evening—I believe there were two persons in the shop—two men, or a man and a boy—I am not sure—whoever was in the shop could see me pay the 2 l.—it was paid openly on a table in the shop—I paid the 15 s. at my boose in St. John-street, Smith field, as I agreed with him at the shop—I was very few minutes bargaining with him.
JOHN JUDD . I deal in furniture, and live in Blackfriars-road. The prisoner had been there about four months to manage the shop as a salesman—he had 18 s. a week, and 6 d. in the pound commission on all he sold—it was his duty to enter every article in a book, the price, and the address of the person, and to account to me for the money whenever I asked him for it—he has never accounted to me for 4 s. for a washhand-stand, sold to Mrs. Williams—I have searched the book—it is not down—I have since been to see—it—it was a part of my stock—he had not accounted for 1 l. 18 s. 6 d. for the carpet and rug, nor for the 15 s. for the glass sold to Mrs. Morgan—I challenged him with that, as he said he had entered all up—I said I recollected the glass being sold—he said he had forgotten it, begged my pardon, and said he had spent the money—I have seen him write, and I know this card and receipt to be his writing.
Cross-examined. Q. He said he had spent the money? A. Either spent it or lost it—this conversation was on the 7th of Dec., when I gave him in charge—he told me he had spent some of the money, and lost the remainder—I used to go into the shop very frequently, but I considered the prisoner a trust-worthy person, and left it to him—I should say I was in the shop when the glass was sold, if not I was when it went away—I did not know the price he sold it for—this was on Saturday night—I had the conversation with him on the Wednesday following—I took the book on Monday and asked for a settlement, but we did not settle, as I was called out—we did not usually settle on Mondays—sometimes we did it twice a week—I do not recollect that we ever exceeded a week till the last time, and we should not have omitted it then, only I was called out—I was on friendly terms with the
prisoner—I considered him a fair salesman—he sold the things to Mr. Capron—I do not know that he made rather too good a bargain by that—he was not to have half the profit—I did not agree that he was to have 4 l. 10 s. for that bargain—I did not get 9 l.—I should say I got about 4 l. 10 s. out of 15 l.—his commission would have been about 4 l. 10 s. if all the goods had been sold—there would have been about 90 l. worth—he was entitled to 6 d. in the pound, and no more—I was not in partnership with him, and he never was treated by me as a partner or a master, but as a salesman—I never represented him to be my brother or a master—I believe he has represented himself to be such in two or three instances—I never suffered him to do so in my presence—there was not an altercation between us as to whether he was not entitled to 4 l. 10 s. on the transaction with Mr. Capron—he did not represent that it was such a bargain, having got 9 l., that he ought to have 4 l. 10 s.
Q. Was there not a question about his mother paying the amount of the sums you charge him with; and did he not refuse because he was to have 4 l. 10 s.?— A. No—he proposed before the Magistrate to pay, but I refused it—his mother came to me to make proposals, but I said it must take its course—this book which is here is the prisoner's hand-writing—the books in which the entries were made were not kept at my other shop—they used to come to me every night, and go back in the morning—there were not the same books for both shops—I do not know that the prisoner had a private memorandum-book—I believe the policeman took a small pocket-book from him, but I never knew he had one till it was taken from him—the books were always fetched to the shop where the prisoner was, or I took them myself—they might have been away for half a day, and then he used to make his entries on a small slip of paper, and copy them in the book—my order-book and cash-book were not left at my other shop on the 5th of Dec.—I took them on that Monday morning, about half-past nine or ten o'clock to have a settlement with him, but I was called out—my other shop is in the London-road—I do not know that there has been any talk about an action being brought against me for giving the prisoner into custody—I was never threatened with an action, and I did not prefer this charge in consequence of any threat.
Q. Are these entries in this part of this book in the prisoner's writing?
A. Yes, they are something which he has been pencilling which are nothing regarding the trade—Mr. Capron's name is not here—I cannot tell what this entry of 4 l. 10 s. is—it is not in the regular way of the book—I never saw this entry before—I have had possession of this book from the time I made the charge—the prisoner has not had an opportunity of making an entry since—this is not the entry of his commission—there is his commission-paper just dropped out of the book—( pointing to it)—I have not torn any leaf out of this book—I had the prisoner before the Magistrate on the Thursday evening—I did not then state that I did not wish to prosecute him—I said I wished to be lenient with him—there was no arrangement between us as to how much he owed me—there was on a former transaction long before this, when he was deficient 2 l. 10 s., but I forgave him that—he was to pay me 10 s. a week—the Magistrate did not say he could not see any case against the prisoner—he began to take depositions against him—the Court was not sitting here at that time—I cannot say how soon it sat afterwards—the Magistrate said, we must take our witnesses before the Grand Jury—he did not say he thought there was no case against him—he said he thought the case was very black against him—we could not come to this Court the first time, because the prisoner was remanded—he was remanded a second time,
and then we preferred the bill before the first Grand Jury that sat—the Magistrate did not tell us to go the first time—he did on the second—he did not complain of our delay—neither the 4 s., nor the 1 l. 18 s. 6 d. are entered in this book, nor was the glass entered, till I challenged him with it—he took the book and entered it—I did not say anything to him about the 1 l. 18 s. 6 d.—I was in the shop at the time he sold the carpet and rug, as I generally was—I saw there were customers in the shop—he did make a statement to me that they wanted the rug for 2 s. less—I cannot exactly say that he told me that they had bought a carpet, as an inducement to take off the 2 l.—I do not know that there was 2 s. taken off—to the best of my recollection, I believe he did tell me that the price of the rug was 14 s. 6 d., and they wanted it for 12 s. 6 d.—there was some cut off the carpet—I was not present when the things went away—I did not see the money paid—Mrs. Underwood was not a customer—I never saw her before—I did not expect he was giving her credit, but it is as often the case that the goods are paid for at home, as in the shop—I knew two or three days afterwards that the carpet, and rug were sent home on the Monday on which they were purchased—it was on the Wednesday night that I asked the prisoner to account—I did not ask him to account for the hearth-rug and carpet—it did not strike me about them till afterwards—I had not then looked through the book—I said to him on the Thursday morning, "You have not entered the carpet and rug"—he then begged my pardon, and said he had spent the money—he had had from Monday until Thursday to have entered it—he had been away during that time—he went on Monday night, and ought to have been at the shop on Tuesday morning—I was not aware he was not there till about eleven o'clock that morning, when I went and opened the shop myself—he came in about an hour after, drunk, in a cab —I asked the cab -man where he brought him from, and he said from a gambling-house—I know Mr. Harding, of Cripplegate—I did not send him amongst his men, to get a list of prices, but on the Monday night, about half-past seven, we had some talk together, and he said he could get his list of prices—I said I should be glad if he could—I did not tell him to get it at any price—he did not say he could get it by giving the men drink—he came back with a list of prices the same evening, and then went off—his wife came to me next morning to know where he was, as he had not been home all night—Wednesday night was the first time I spoke to him about the glass; he begged my pardon, said he had spent part of the money and lost the remainder—on Thursday he said the same with regard to the carpet—he paid me a cheque of 6 l. which he received for a debt he collected for me—he told me on the Monday, that he had not got it, but on Tuesday he gave it me, and 4 l. in gold—I did not speak to him about the washhand-stand—I did not know it myself till since his examination—he did not say it was a private job of his own—the regular price for them is 4 s. or 4 s. 6 d.
MR. BODKIN. Q. What was the 10 s. a week that you were to receive from him? A. He was deficient 50 s. one week, and he was to pay it at 10 s. a week, but he only paid one 10 s., and I forgave him that—the first item of 4 s. for the washhand-stand was on the 19th of Nov., and there is no entry of that—there are entries of other sales subsequent to that in this book, in his own writing—I did not know of that stand being sold till after the first examination—this book, in which it was his duty to enter every sale—on Saturday, the 3rd of Dec., I was there all that day—I took it away myself late that night, and brought it again myself from my other shop in the London-road early on the Monday morning—it remained there all that day, and Tuesday, but on Tuesday he was not there till late—on Monday, I sent him to Mr. Harding's about some prices—he came back with them very late, close upon twelve
o'clock, and said he had been drinking with Mr. Harding's men—I saw no more of him that night, and the following morning I found my shop closed at eleven o'clock; when he came that day, he got out of the cab two or three yards below the shop, just round the corner, he then remained at the shop half an hour—I told him to go home, and go to bed, as he was not fit for business—he came on Wednesday, remained all day, and in consequence of something I discovered, I spoke to him that evening—I first asked him about the glass—I looked in the book, and found there was no entry of it—I then spoke to him about it—he begged my pardon, and said he had spent some money, and lost the rest—he did not say anything about the carpet and rug, and it did not strike me at that time—I then looked in the book, and found they were not entered, and then on Thursday I spoke to him about them—he gave me the same answer, and he was 2 l. 15 s. in money deficient that he had not accounted for—he was given into custody on the Thursday, and taken before the Magistrate the same day, and remanded for close upon a fortnight—the Magistrate ordered him to be detained in Horsemonger-lane—I do not recollect that on two occasions the Magistrate said the Grand Jury were sitting—my attorney attended the examination.
MATTHEW GODBARD ( police-constable M 167.) I took the prisoner on the 8th of Dec.—Mr. Judd told him what it was for—he said he was very orry, and wished Mr. Judd to look over it, and come to some sort of terms—he said he hoped I would persuade Mr. Judd to be merciful to him.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you take a book from him? A. Yes—he said he was very sorry he had spent Mr. Judd's money in a drunken frolic—he did not say how much it was—he had this book in his hand—he and Mr. Judd were together some time, and had some conversation about accounts—they were talking about some former balances, and the prisoner said, "I thought you would have taken this as you have the other"—Mr. Judd said he would do no such thing—they went to their books, and were some few minutes going through them—I did not see that Mr. Judd had his book in his hand—he had some papers.
CHARLES WRIGHT . I am attorney for this prosecution. I attended at the prisoner's first examination—he was remanded from Thursday till the following Saturday week to give me time to inquire—he attended then, and was remanded till the 7th of Jan. to give me further time—before the 7th of Jan. a Session was holden here—I came and preferred this bill by desire of Mr. Cottingham—I applied that the depositions should be taken, and the Magistrate said he saw no difficulty in the matter.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you tell him for what you wanted time? A. I supposed there were further cases—I do not think I had one of the witnesses there at first—I did not enter into the several charges—I did not know of the washhand-stand then—it was on the Thursday evening—I rather think the looking-glass was the only charge that was then made—I think the others came out afterwards—I went again on the Saturday week following, and then the Magistrate did not take any depositions—the prisoner complained that I had not gone before the Grand Jury, and the Magistrate said, "I gave time to find cases"—the prisoner said the Magistrate had remanded him to give me an opportunity to go before the Grand Jury—on that occasion no depositions were taken—the Magistrate did not tell me that the Grand Jury would sit on the 5th of Jan.—he said he would remand the prisoner till the 7th of Jan. to give me an opportunity to go before the Grand Jury.
COURT. Q. When the prisoner complained that he had been remanded for you to make application to the Grand Jury, what did the Magistrate say? A. He said, "Certainly not"—he gave me time to gain evidence.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430130-817]
817. WILLIAM CARPENTER was again indicted for embezzlement.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
RACHAEL MORGAN . On the 3rd of Dec. I purchased a chimney-glass at the prosecutor's shop, from half-past seven to half-past eight o'clock in the evening—I did not know whether it was master or servant I bought it of—I paid 2 l. as a deposit on the glass—I did not wish to pay more till I saw it brought home.
Cross-examined by MR. HEATON. Q. You have told us there were two persons present? A. Yes, either two men or a man and a boy—the only one that was talking to me about the glass was the prisoner, and I gave him the money.
JOHN JUDD . I carry on business in Blackfriars-road. The prisoner was in my service as a seller, and was authorised to receive money on my account—it was his duty when so receiving money to account to me by entering it in a book directly the articles were sold—he never accounted to me by entering it in the book, or otherwise, for 2 l. paid by Mrs. Morgan on the 3rd of Dec.
Cross-examined. Q. I suppose all you said in the last case was true? A. I should say it was—I do not know that I asserted any thing untrue.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

819. DENNIS DOWNING was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of Jan., 1 purse, value 3 d.; 2 sovereigns, 2 half-severeigns, 1 half-crown, 36 shillings, and 10 sixpences; the property of Harriet Pryer; to which he pleaded

[trialAccount t18430130-820]
820. RICHARD GROVES LEVERS was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of Jan., 6 pence, the monies of George Richard Gainsford and another, his masters.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE RICHARD GAINSFORD . I am in partnership with Mr. Good, as linen-drapers, in High-street, Borough. The prisoner was our shopman for nearly two years—in consequence of some circumstances I directed Johnson George, on the 17th of Jan., to mark some copper money—on the 18th a policeman was sent for, and the prisoner's box was searched in my presence, a quantity of copper was found there, and some was marked.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Have you no more than one partner? A. No—the prisoner had 40 l. and 45 l. a year—the marked money was found in his box in his bed-room—one or two more young men slept in that room—the box was locked—he unlocked it himself.
JOHNSON GEORGE . I am shopman to Messrs. Gainsford and Good. On the 17th of Jan., by direction of Mr. Gainsford, I marked six penny-pieces, and about two o'clock placed them on a recess behind where the prisoner was in the habit of serving—about an hour afterwards I missed five of them, and the other one after five o'clock—I saw the copper that was found in the prisoner's box—the policeman brought it to me from the station—I examined it—among it I found the sixpence which I had previously marked and laid in
the recess—I put a cross on the edge of each penny-piece—these are the six I marked.
Cross-examined. Q. The recess is very handy to the prisoner, as he was doing business in the shop? A. Yes—if a person wanted change suddenly he should not give it out of his pocket, and then take it from where it might be—it is not the custom for our young men to have coppers in their pocket—they should be put into the till.
MR. PAYNE. Q. The recess was not the usual place to put coppers in? A. No—it was put there for the purpose of trying him—if a person wants change they go to the desk for it—there is a person at the desk for that purpose.
JOHN COOK ( police-sergeant M 8.) On the 18th of Jan., about half-past two o'clock, I was called to the prosecutor's—I went up stairs, and searched a box, after I had searched the prisoner—he opened it—I found in it 18 l. 13 s. in gold and silver, and 1 l. 11 s. 1 1/2 d. in loose coppers—I took charge of the copper, and produced them to Johnson George—he picked out these six penny-pieces.
Cross-examined. Q. You went to search for some copper? A. We went to see if we could find any thing of Mr. Gainsford's—we took all the money we found in the box, which I produce here.
GUILTY. Aged 25.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18430130-821]
821. JOHN NORMAN was indicted for embezzlement.
MARY JACKSON . I am the wife of John Jackson. The prisoner was in our service—on the 24th of Dec. I sent him with half a hundredweight of coals to Mrs. Hands—he was to bring back 9 d. for them—on the 31st I sent him with some coals to Mrs. Fenton—he was to bring back 1 s. 6 d.—he did not bring me either sum—he said they would pay next time—he did not pay 1 s. 6 d. from Gay.
JOHN JACKSON . The prisoner never paid me either of these sums.
MARTHA HANDS . I received some coals on the 24th, and sent 9 d. back by the prisoner.
ELIZA FENTON . I received some coals on the 31st of Dec.—I sent 1 s. 6 d. back by the prisoner.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18430130-822]
822. JAMES SMITH and THOMAS POWERS were indicted for stealing, on the 12st of Jan., 30lbs. weight of rope, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of John Churchin, in a vessel on the navigable river Thames.
ROBERT BARKER . I am mate of the Richmond Packet, lying in the Thames—John Churchin is the master. This rope belongs to that packet.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Who had the care of this vessel? A. The captain, Mr. Churchin—I was absent from the ship about an hour.
JOSHUA JUDGE . I am an inspector of the Thames Police. On Saturday night, the 21st of Jan., I saw the prisoners in a boat—I saw Smith get on to the Richmond, and soon after jump back—I boarded their boat, and found this rope in it—I said, "Where did you get this?"—Smith said a lighterman had sent them for it—I said, "Do you know his name?"—he said, "No"—I said I should take them to his vessel—Smith said, "It is no use to go, it did not come from there."
Cross-examined. Q. Smith seemed the active person? A. Yes—Powers took no part in the conversation—I cannot tell whether Powers was employed to row Smith.
Smith's Defence. I was sent after it by a lighterman, who said he would pay us for it—I did not go on board the vessel.
SMITH *— GUILTY . Aged 19.— Transported for Ten Years.
POWERS — NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430130-823]
823. MATILDA GARDNER was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of Jan., 1 sovereign, 1 half-sovereign, 1 half-crown, and 3 shillings, the monies of Edward Ockenden, from the person of Rebecca Hester Ockenden.
EDWARD OCKENDEN . I am a labourer, and live in Ely-place, South Lambeth. On the 7th of Jan. I went with my grandchild to Oxford-market where I ought to receive my pension—on my way, near the Horse Guards, I met the prisoner—she followed us up to Oxford-market—I received a sovereign, a half-sovereign, a half-crown, and three shillings—I gave that to my grand-daughter, for her grandmother—I then went to a public-house—the prisoner was with us, and I told her I had given my grand-daughter the money—we came out, and came on to Regent-street—I asked my little girl if she had got the money safe—she said, "Yes"—I said, "Let me look"—she took it out—it was in a piece of newspaper—I said, "Put it into your pocket, and take it home to your grandmother"—in coming along, I slipped down—I went into the Jolly Gardeners, and got the dirt off my coat—the prisoner was by me—when I was going home, in a cab, and told the child to pay, she said the prisoner had taken the money out of her pocket.
REBECCA HESTER OCKENDEN . I shall be eleven years old next July. recollect having a sovereign, a half-sovereign, and some silver—I put it into my pocket, and when we got to the Jolly Gardeners my grandfather fell down—when we went into the house the prisoner sat beside me—I felt her putting her hand into my pocket, and when I felt afterwards the money was gone—no one but her could have taken it—I am sure she had it.
EMMA COSTER . I know the prisoner—she went away from me that morning, and had no money at all—she came home in the evening, and she then had a half-sovereign and some silver.
Prisoner's Defence. I met the prosecutor; he asked me to go with him; when we got to the Jolly Gardeners I stopped some time, and then left him.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18430130-824]
824. JOHN SHEPHERD was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of Jan., 21lbs. weight of palm oil, value 6 s. 7 3/4 d., the goods of John Carvallo de Medeiros.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLES DAVID STEWART . I was in the employ of John Knill and Co., who live in Frogmore-lane, Wandsworth—we had a tub of palm oil on the premises—it might have been there two or three months previous to the 9th of Jan.—there was 1 cwt. of the oil—it was the property of John Carvallo de Medeiros—he is a partner in the firm, but this was his private property—on the 9th of Jan. I searched in the warehouse for it—I found the tub, but a quantity of oil, about 21lbs. missing—the prisoner was occasionally employed by me, but he was not allowed access to the warehouse—some palm oil was produced to me in a pail—the oil is here in a cloth—I believe this to be part of that oil that was in the tub—it resembles it in quality, smell, and other particulars—I can positively identify it as having undergone a partial chemical process—I have compared it with the oil left in the tub, and I believe it to be some of the oil that was missing from it—it was worth about 35 l. a ton.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. When did it undergo this process? A. I do not know—I assisted in the operation—that was the only tub that was subjected to that process—it was the first time we had ever tried it in that way—I examined the cask on the day we were at the police-station—I think it was the 6th of Jan.—I can ascertain this oil had undergone the process by the peculiarity of its appearance—it is now different from other palm oil, by its having an acid smell—I believe that is only produced by the chemical process—I never met with it in any that had not undergone the process—it is a bleaching process—I believe it is commonly applied to palm oil—I do not know that it is common to palm oil to have this acid smell—the prisoner has been a frequent purchaser of staves of tallow-tubs at our warehouse—I suppose he has bought 5 l. or 6 l. worth of them—they are 10 s. a hundred—I do not think that one of them had had palm oil in it—a considerable quantity of grease adheres to the tubs, but the staves are scraped before they are sold—there could not be much grease scraped off after they are sold—I never knew of its being done—I will swear that this grease, which is here produced, is palm oil—I have been in the business two years and a half—soap-makers have more to do with the business than I have.
THOMAS ATKINS . I am pot-boy at the Grapes public-house, at Wandsworth. On the 2nd of January a little before nine o'clock, I saw the prisoner coming from Mr. Knill's premises with a little boy—the prisoner had a pail on his shoulder, and his arm through the handle—it appeared to me as if it was very heavy, he stooped his back—he was going towards his own house.
Cross-examined. Q. What is your notion of a great weight, a hundred pounds? A. It might be a hundred pounds—he was doing this quite openly.
HENRY BAILEY . I am servant to Mr. Durant, a tallow-chandler, at Putney. On the 4th of Jan. the prisoner came to his shop with the grease which has been produced and represented as palm oil, in a pail—there was twenty-one pounds of it—I paid him 2 d. a pound for it—it came to 3 s. 6 d.—it was given up to the officer—we had bought some of him previously.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you give him what you considered a fair value? A. I did not know the value—I took it to the foreman, who did know—we were not exactly aware what it was—we put it with our kitchen-stuff.
WILLIAM READ ( police-sergeant V 18.) I went and got this oil from Bailey—I did not find any at the prisoner's house.
GUILTY . Aged 47.— Confined Six Months.

827. SELINA WEEDON was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of Dec., at Lambeth, 6 spoons, value 2 l.; 1 ring, 2 l. 10 s.; one pair of sugar-tongs, 17 s.; 1 brooch, 5 s.; 5 handkerchiefs, 1 l. 10 s.; 2 pencil-cases, 3 s.; 1 toothpick, 2 s. 6 d.; 2 sovereigns, and 3 sixpences; the property of John James Berry, in his dwelling-house.
JOHN JAMES BERRY . I am a brushmaker, living in Walnut-tree-walk, Lambeth; it is my dwelling-house. The prisoner was lodging at my house on the 21st of Dec., and on the 1st of Jan. I missed the articles stated in the indictment, to the value of 10 l. from my front-room up stairs, in a teacaddy—I asked the prisoner if she had been up in my front-room, and taken any thing away—she said no she had not—I got an officer, and searched her apartments, but could find nothing—this is my ring—it is the only article that is found—I am sure it is mine—all the other things were in the place where this was, except the handkerchiefs, which were in a drawer very near to it—the prisoner lived with a man there.
Cross-examined by MR. WILD. Q. Is this a lodging-house? A. No—very few persons come to see me, I am very much out.
JOHN POOLE . I am a pawnbroker, in Great Queen-street, Lincoln's Inn-fields—I took this ring in of the prisoner, on the 21st of Dec., in the name of Ann Brooks.
Cross-examined. Q. You carry on a good business? A. Yes, a number of persons come to my shop.
GUILTY of stealing the ring. Aged 24.— Confined Two Months.

828. CHARLES PETERS was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of January, 56lbs. weight of coals, value 6 d., the goods of Robert Gammon, in a vessel on the navigable river Thames.
GEORGE WALKER JUDGE . I am a Thames-police inspector. About eight o'clock in the morning of the 21st of Jan., I was crossing the water from Prince's-stairs, and saw the prisoner in the after-room of a barge called the Bill, belonging to Mr. Gammon—I saw him bring a tub of large coals, and put into his boat—I shot astern of the barge, and got into the boat—I told him to row me across the river—I showed these coals to Mr. Gammon, and he recognized them.
ROBERT GAMMON . I am owner of this barge called the Bill, and the coals in it—I have examined, and compared these, and believe them to be mine.
Prisoner's Defence. I was working up the river, and asked a lighterman if I might have these coals in the barge; he said, "Stop a minute, and lend me a hand to pull this barge in, then you may have them." I had other coals in the boat.
GEORGE WALKER JUDGE . He did not say a word of this till he got halfway across the water—he had 50lbs. of other coals in the boat, but these were in the tub.
GUILTY. Aged 28.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Six Months.

829. HENRY DRAPER was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of Jan., 2 pairs of boots, value 1 l. 13 s.; and 2 pairs of shoes, value 10 s.; the goods of Thomas Bingley, his master.
THOMAS BINGLEY . I am a boot and shoemaker, and live in Pleasant-row, Old Kent-road. I have a shop in the Park-road—the prisoner was my apprentice—I left my shop about four o'clock, on the 23rd of Jan., and returned about ten—I then found the prisoner in custody—these are my shoes and boots—the prisoner had no business with them.
JOHN SYMES . I am a relative of the prisoner—he came to me about twenty minutes to eight o'clock, on the 23rd of Jan.—I was in bed—he asked me to come out with him—I asked where he was going—he said, "I have got some boots here to pawn"—I asked him where he got them from—he said, "I will tell you when you come out"—I put on my boots, and went out—I asked him again—he said he had taken them from his master,
and wanted me to pawn them for him—I said I would have nothing to do with them, and told him to take them back—he said no, he would throw them away, if I did not like to pawn them—I said I would take him to his master's—he said, "No"—I then said I would take them from him, and have the money for him on Sunday—I sent word to his mother to come home, and we went to his master's, but could make no one hear, and came away—next morning I was coming down with them again, and the policeman took me and the prisoner.
GUILTY . Aged 14.— Confined Three Months.

830. DANIEL LEONARD and DANIEL TRACEY were indicted for stealing, on the 7th of Jan., 37 yards of printed cotton, value 21 s.; the goods of William Barnett.
HARRIETT BARNETT . I am the wife of William Barnett, a linen-draper, in Cherry-garden-street, Bermondsey. About seven o'clock on Saturday night, the 7th of January, I saw Tracey take a piece of print from the door, inside the shop—Leonard was with him—I ran out, but could not catch him—I heard Tracey say, " Jack, there is a nice piece"—I am sure they are the two boys.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Where were you when you heard Tracey say this? A. Close by the print in the shop—I know this print by the pattern—there is no mark on it.
MARY ANN WHITE . I am the wife of David White, a lighterman. About half-past seven o'clock that night, two boys arrived at my house—it was dark—I do not know either of them—this is my mark to this deposition, which I made before the Magistrate.
Q. Did you tell the Magistrate, "The prisoner and two other lads came to my house on Saturday night?" A. There were only two in company—I do not know which of the prisoners came, I think it was one of them—I think Tracey was one—I had not known him a long time—I had not had many things before from them—they had never been there before—I will not swear they had not—I think Tracey was one that came that night, and I think they brought this piece of cotton—I have heard of persons being indicted for receiving stolen goods.
GEORGE KEMP ( police-constable M 205.) I went to White's house, and found the cotton—I apprehended Leonard, I saw him coming out of her house, between six and seven o'clock the same night.
WILLIAM TERRY . I saw Mrs. Barnett following the two prisoners—they had a bundle, and were running from towards her house to Mrs. White's, round the back way of the houses.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. A labourer—I work for Mr. Denny, a corn-lighterman—I have never been in any trouble myself—I ran down the City-gardens to see which way they went, and heard Tracey say to Leonard, "Take it down to Mrs. Hurley's, as plain as I could understand, but Mrs. White's they must have said.
Tracey's Defence. I know nothing about it.
LEONARD — GUILTY .
TRACEY — GUILTY .
Confined Three Months, and Whipped.
[trialAccount t18430130-831]
831. WILLIAM GODDARD was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of Jan., 1 bushel of oats, value 3 s.; the goods of Thomas Reeve Denny, in a barge on the navigable River Thames.
WILLIAM GILLS I am a lighterman to Mr. Thomas Reeve Denny, a corn merchant, of Hope wharf, Upper Ground-street, Blackfriars. On Saturday, the 14th of Jan., I brought his barge to Middle-tier, Horsleydown, with
150 quarters of black oats in it—we got in sixty-five quarters—it began to rain—I covered them over with a tarpaulin, and left 129 sacks full—the next morning I went down, and missed one sack—I have seen these oats produced by the officer—( examining them)—there is a difference between the two—there is a similar hull on each of them—they are similar oats.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You thought they were not the same at first? A. One parcel is brighter than the other—two samples may have the same hull in them, and yet be different—I judge by other qualities—I have some doubt about these being the same—I have been acquainted with these sort of things ten years—I remember exactly how many of these oats I had loaded-I had left 129—I counted them before I left—I did not count how many were in the barge when I got back, but I saw the place of the sack, there might have still been 129.
GEORGE WALKER JUDGE . I am a Thames police inspector. About half-past six, on Saturday evening, the 14th of Jan., I was proceeding down the river; I saw a skiff between the two lower tiers, Horsleydown, with two men in it—as she passed close to me I saw a bag similar to this one under the back thwart—I said to my men, "Shove astern directly "—the two men in the boat began to pull across—I jumped out of the boat into the water, ran after them up the stairs, and lost them—I came back, and found no bag, but a few black oats in the boat, and directly after one of my men handed me a bag of oats—I went to the tier, and found a barge of oats, and one bag there had had about a bushel taken out of it—I have compared those oats with those in this bag, and have no doubt of their being the same—on Sunday morning I went to the prisoner's house—he was not at home—I left word that I had picked up his boat, and he must come to the Thames-police for it—when he came I asked where he was the night before—he said he left the boat at five o'clock, and went home to tea, and he was at home at six.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you in a position to see the persons in the boat better than any one else? A. No—it was the prisoner's master's boat.
HAMMOND JOHN GRIFFIN ( Thames police-constable, No. 33.) I saw the skiff—the prisoner was rowing it—I am sure he was one—when Judge jumped into the water the prisoner ran away—Hall found the oats.
Cross-examined. Q. How long had you known the prisoner? A. Between two and three years—I only saw him this evening as he passed me—I was rowing—he was about a foot from me—I have no doubt of his being the man—I mentioned him to Judge.
JOSEPH HALL ( Thames police-constable. No. 56.) I saw the skiff come from between the tiers—I did not notice the men—I saw the oats floating down the water.
WILLIAM PIKE . I am a waterman. I saw the prisoner rowing down inside the tier at a quarter past six o'clock, and about ten minutes after I saw him row back again—I saw the bag overboard—I did not see any one chuck it overboard.
Cross-examined. Q. You knew the boat? A. Yes—I knew the men who were rowing it.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430130-832]
832. WILLIAM FRANCIS STEEL was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of July, I watch, value 1 l. 15 s.; the goods of Joseph Knowles, his master.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN KNOWLES . I am a silversmith and watchmaker, and live in Bolingbroke-row,
Camberwell-road. The prisoner had been in my service for about thirteen months—this is my watch—it was in my possession a week or a fortnight previous to the 28th of July—about two months prior to the prisoner's being taken, which was on the 30th of Dec., I missed ten watches, and that was one of them—I mentioned this one, and described it to him—he said they were lent—he did not say to whom—he handed me three duplicates—he said one was of the gold watch which the lady had inquired after, and the others of two watches belonging to me—I took one of the duplicates to Mr. Bradbury's, saw this watch there, and obtained it from him.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You are not a watch-maker? A. No—the prisoner was employed to repair watches for me, and he made one—I had not then known of his having pledged my watches—I have since ascertained that he had pledged watches from time to time—I do not know any instance of his pledging a watch, and taking it out—I remember a spring dial at a beer shop—I do not remember that being pledged, but I remember its being taken out—soon after the prisoner was in my employ I bought the duplicate of a spring dial of a person, and the prisoner said, "I have one, if you will redeem it," but I did not know it bad been pledged by him—I never knew he had pledged any thing—he did not take work home with my consent or knowledge—I remember a 3 l. lever watch—he did not take that home with my knowledge to put a new escapement—I employed him to make a new watch for Mr. Kay—I did not know that he took that home—I do not know how much he expended on that—I sold it for seven guineas—I did not pay him for it—the prisoner did not take home a watch of mine—I know there was a watch with eight holes in the shop for some time.
Q. And then as you did not pay the prisoner he took it? A. No, he showed me a watch, and said he should put my name on it, but I never saw it after—I do not know that when he was apprehended he was finishing a clock with a double dial at home; nor that I owe him any thing but for that little watch—I think he said it was 3 l. 15 s.—" Mr. Kay" is the prisoner's writing on the back of the duplicate he gave me—this is the watch.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Had this watch required any repairs? A. I hung it in a case to be repaired at the prisoner's leisure—I gave him no directions whatever to take it out of my house—he had no right to take it out—I had not paid what I owed him, because he set fire to my premises carelessly, and the damage done was 6 l. or 7 l.—he said he would pay me—he had not demanded the money for this watch—I asked him several times what it was—his tools are with me now—he may have them if he pays what is due—there is 2 l. 10 s. remaining due on a gold watch which he took away.
HENRY WILLIAM HALL . I am assistant to Mr. Bradbury, a pawnbroker in the London-road—this watch was pledged in the name of John Brown, on the 28th of July—Mr. Knowles took it out—this is the duplicate of it.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know who pledged it? A. No, I did not take it in.
JOSEPH KNOWLES re-examined. This is the duplicate the prisoner gave me.
SAMUEL WRIGHT ( police-constable P 172.) I took the prisoner on the 30th of Dec. at the prosecutor's shop—I said it was for taking some watches from his master—he said he had done so, and he could not deny it—I then went to his own house—I saw a female there—I told her to turn out her pockets—the prisoner was present, and told her to give the duplicates up—I got several duplicates there, some from his wife, and some from his son, whom he sent up stairs.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you present when he gave up the duplicate
which relates to this watch? A. I was not—it was given up before I took him, I believe—I am sure the prisoner said he had taken the watches, and could not deny it.
COURT to MR. KNOWLES. Q. Had you made any charge against him before he gave you up these duplicates. A. I questioned him about a watch that a lady had called for, and then he gave me up the three tickets.
WILLIAM BEAL , a haberdasher in Bolinbrook-row; and George Wilkins, a carpenter at Brixton, gave the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY . Aged 40.— Transported for Seven Years.
(There were two other indictments against the prisoner; and the prosecutor stated he had paid 50 l. to redeem watches.)
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18430130-833]
833. RICHARD RAVENSCROFT was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
HANNALL CLARK . I live in Surrey-street, Strand. I had, on the 9th of Jan., purchased goods at Mr. Harvey's, in the Westminster-road, to the amount of 3 l. 18 s. 6 1/2 d.—on the 10th of Jan. they were brought home to me by the porter, but the prisoner is the person who saw the carpet measured, and I paid him 3 l. 18 s. 6 d. for the articles which I had purchased—I took a receipt from him for the amount—I told him I was going to the shop to purchase some drapery, and there were some little things which I had purchased at the shop, which I would pay for in a few days.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. What day was this? A. On Tuesday, the 10th of Jan.—there was some sheeting and other things left, which he did not know the cost of—I paid 8 s. 1 d. for them on Monday the 16th—I paid the 3 l. 18 s. 6 d. at five o'clock in the afternoon.
JAMES NICOL . I am clerk to Mr. Joseph Harvey. The prisoner was in his service on the 10th of Jan. as a salesman—he had been there from two to three months—Mrs. Clark was a customer of ours—it was the prisoner's duty to receive sums of money, and to pay them directly to the cashier, and then to tell me of it, that it might be placed paid in the book—he did not tell me, on the 10th of Jan., that he had received 3 l. 18 s. 6 d. from Mrs. Clark—on the 16th he came to me, and said, "I have made a mistake, I wish you would put it to rights for me; I received the money from the customer in Surrey-street, Strand; and though I ought to have made it paid in the book, I omitted to do so; I wish you would do it for me now"—I said, as I had no doubt he had paid the money to the cashier, I would do so, and asked the amount—he said 3 l. 18 s. 10 d., but he had only received 3 l. 18 s. 6 d.; that he had received it on the 11th, and paid it to the cashier on the 12th—he did not say which cashier—I then made inquiries of the cashier—the prisoner keeps a book in which he writes the various amounts he receives during the day—I referred to it on the morning of the 16th, after he had spoken to me, and found that no such sum had been entered—in the afternoon of the same day I found it had been entered—it had been interlined betwixt the two days.
Cross-examined. Q. Who is it that settles the accounts of the cashiers? A. They account, of course, to their employers—the money is paid by the young men to the cashiers—the 8 s. 1 d. that Mrs. Clark paid on the 16th was received by the prisoner—he came and told me it was received—he paid it in the regular way—I do not know whether it was late on the evening on the 10th that he went to Mrs. Clark—we shut up at eight o'clock, sometimes a little later—I do not remember his coming back on the night of the 10th—from the entries in my book it appears he did not account to me for any sum—it is my business to make things paid in the book, when I am satisfied that
the money is paid—there was nothing I made paid in the books on the 10th from the prisoner.
ELIZA WILLIAMS . I am a cashier, employed by Mr. Harvey. I was acting as cashier on the 10th, 11th, and 12th—in my absence for meals persons act for me—I did not on either of those days receive from the prisoner 3 l. 18 s. 6 d.—I was there from five o'clock till eight on the evening of the 10th—I can say positively that 3 l. 18 s. 6 d. was not paid to me by the prisoner on any one of these days—it is my duty to see the cheque-book kept by the prisoner—the men call over their books at night, the money is counted by the cashier, who takes it to the bank in the morning—the prisoner's book is compared with the sheet of the day, and if he had paid this sum in that day, the money in the sheet would not have balanced, which it did correctly—it would have been 3 l. 18 s. 6 d. more.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you got your book here? A. Yes, and the sheet—my accounts are quite correct between the 10th and 16th—they never failed to balance on any occasion within a shilling or two—I keep on my paper an account of what each person pays on a small cheque—the prisoner paid me various sums between the 10th and 16th—the sheets will show it—this sheet is for the 12th—on that day I received various sums, amounting to 17 l. 2 s. 6 d., as the sheet states—on the 11th I received 35 l. 1 s. 5 d., and one cheque was 30 l.—on the 13th I received 11 l. 8 s. 5 d.—on each of these days I received some money from him, varying in amount, but not that sum—I swear he has never paid me more by mistake than he should—I think on one occasion I paid him more than I ought to have done, and he returned it
COURT. Q. Supposing him to receive several sums, are they separately entered, or only the total sum? A. They are separately entered—if he received 2 l. 10 s. and 1 l. 10 s. they would be entered as two separate items.
JULIANA BURTON . I am occasionally employed to receive cash during the absence of Williams—I did not receive 3 l. 18 s. 6 d. on the 10th, 11th, or 12th of Jan. from the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you got your sheets? A. There are no sheets but these, and these would contain the amount of what he paid me as well as Williams—they are made up at the end of the day—these papers are in Williams's handwriting.
ELIZA WILLIAMS re-examined. The separate sums are put into his book, then they are added together, and the total amount is put on the sheet.
COURT. Q. Is there a book in which the prisoner's separate payments would appear, and to whom he paid them; would it appear how much he paid to each person? A. No, it would be proved by the sheet that the prisoner paid in the money—the book would represent the various sums he paid in, and this paper or sheet is the cash account of the day, under the names of all the persons paying in money—the total that each person paid—the fractional parts appear in the prisoner's book.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Look at this book kept by the prisoner; look on the 11th of Jan., and look at the sheet made up on the 11th, what is the sum of money apparently received from the prisoner according to the sheet? A. 35 l. 1 s. 5 d., and the total in the book is 35 l. 1 s. 5 d.—it appears that 3 l. 18 s. 6 d. has been interlined after the 35 l. 1 s. 5 d.—if this 3 l. 18 s. 6 s. had been in the book when it was brought to me on the 11th, it would not have appeared right—I should not have allowed the book to have been made up with an error of 3 l. 18 s. 6 d.—it is not added with either of the days—I did not on the 16th, or any day before the 16th, receive this sum.
COURT. Q. Do the book and sheets correspond on every day except the
days on which it is alleged he received 1 l. 13 s., and 3 l. 18 s. 6 d.? A. They do within a few shillings.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Is it a part of your duty to go through the book of each of the shopmen, for the purpose of seeing whether the tickets you have corresponded with the books? A. Either I or Mr. Hoskings do—it it more generally my duty to do it—I have since gone through this book from the 10th to the 16th—they correspond in all particulars, except these two items of 1 l. 13 s., and 3 l. 18 s. 6 d.
ARTHUR WOOLF HOSKINGS . I have compared the books and the sheets—I count the money myself—3 l. 18 s. 6 d. has not been received from the 10th to the 16th—I have counted up the books, and find the 3 l. 18 s. 6 d. has not been paid—the sum of 1 l. 13 s. has not been entered on the 9th of Jan.—if that sum had been paid in the cash would not balance, and I find my cash right on all occasions, with the exception of 4 s., 5 s., or 6 s.—we cannot balance to a 1 d.—it is the duty of the young men to compare their books with the sheet every evening, but if a young man is out we have it done for him.
COURT. Q. When the prisoner pays in a sum, has he the means by his own book and the sheet, of ascertaining whether they have given him credit for it? A. No—he would give a bill at the cashier's desk—these bills are given to the customer—we see by the bill that the amount paid agrees with the sum paid up at the desk—each one has a cheque, puts the number of his book on it, enters that amount into the book, and puts the book back into its place—whatever he receives he must enter in his own book—that is a book that he had access to—in the evening the young man who is called comes and adds his book up to see what has been taken in the course of the day—the cashier says, "How much do you make your book?"—he says, "So much"—if it corresponds with the cheques it is passed-if not, the cheques are all called over one by one—the prisoner has to call it himself, to see where the error may be.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. If he received money and it has escaped him of putting it in his book, what means have the owners of knowing he received it? A. Because he makes a cheque of it—if he does not make a cheque, it would of course appear in the book—we never allow any one to go away without making a cheque—if a man takes goods home he does not take the book with him.
Q. If he took goods to show, and they were bought, suppose he received the money and forgot it, what then? A. These goods are all entered before they go out, and the length of the goods are put down in the entry-book, and when he comes back they are compared to see that they are all right—the party who keeps that book does not let it go out—I do not know whether there is an entry in the approbation-book, of the goods sent to Mrs. Clark—whether it has been paid would not appear in that book, but he would have his memory refreshed by the circumstance of the approbation-book.
JAMES NICOL re-examined. Q. Does it appear by the entry-book that he took out goods to the amount of 3 l. 18 s. 6 d., and that they had been sold? A. These had been sold previously.
Cross-examined. Q. Whose entry is this? A. It is entered by one of the junior hands, and posted by me into the ledger—the junior would get it from the communication of the prisoner—the reason this payment of the 10th of Jan. is run through is, the entry is copied into a smaller book, and then the pen is drawn through them—I said I would make this paid, but I did not till I had inquired—this 1 l. 13 s. was on the 6th of Jan.—I believe not by the prisoner, but by Goodman.
RICHARD GOODMAN . I am a salesman, in the employ of Mr. Harvey. On the 5th of Jan., the prisoner showed me a pattern of satinette, and gave me instructions to send thirteen yards to Mrs. Richards, of Greenwich, at 2 s. 6 d. a yard—I cut it off, and gave it to the prisoner—it came to 1 l. 13 s. 0 1/2 d.
JOHN HOUGHTON . I am in the upholstery department at Mr. Harvey's. On the 7th of Jan., I had some work to do at Greenwich—the prisoner gave me a brown paper parcel, and told me to deliver it to Mr. Richards, of Greenwich—I gave it to my man—he brought me back 1 l. 13 s., which I paid to the prisoner, on Monday the 9th, between nine and ten o'clock in the morning—I said I had got some money for him—he said, "What is it for?" I said, "For the parcel you gave me to take to Greenwich"—on the following day, Tuesday, he asked me if I recollected what money I paid him the day before, as he had put it into his pocket with his own money, and did not recollect it—I told him a sovereign, a half-sovereign, a half-crows, and a sixpence.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you receive money? A. Occasionally—I sometimes pay it to the young men that serve, and sometimes to the cashier—I never was directed to pay the cashier—we consider it right either way.
SAMUEL BARNARD . I was directed to deliver the brown paper parcel at Greenwich—I delivered it to Mr. Richards, and paid the money to Houghton.
ELIZA WILLIAMS re-examined. This sum has not been accounted for either on the 9th or 10th—it is not in the prisoner's book on either of those days.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined One Year.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18430130-834]
834. THOMAS BROOKER was indicted for unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin, knowing the same to be counterfeit.
MESSRS. CHAMBERS and CHARNOCK conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZAWILLIAMS. I am the wife of Samuel Williams, a cow-keeper, in the Old Kent-road. On the 13th of Jan. the prisoner came for a penny-worth of milk—I served him a halfpenny-worth by mistake—he gave me a shilling—I told him it was bad—he said he did not know it, he would go and get me 1 d. for the milk, if I would take the mug of milk and give him the shilling—I said I would not return the shilling, but detain him—I sent for an officer—the prisoner walked out of the yard till he got round the corner, then he ran—he left the shilling in my hand—I called a policeman, gave him the shilling, and he gave it me back again—I put it on a shelf, and the next morning I marked it and gave it to the officer.
ANNE MARIA BROWN . I live with a cow-keeper, in Great George-street, Bermondsey. About nine o'clock in the evening of the 13th of Jan. the prisoner came for a halfpenny-worth of milk—I served him—he gave me a shilling—I took it to my master—I am sure I gave him the same shilling.
MARK DUCKETT . I am Brown's master—she gave me a shilling on the 13th of Jan.—I marked it and gave it to the policeman—I came out and saw the prisoner in my shop, and said, "We have been taking some bad money; I am doubtful whether you might not have been passing some here"—he said he had not—I sent my man fora policeman, and gave him in charge—I went
into a shop in the neighbourhood to weigh the shilling—it proved a bad one—the prisoner said he lived in a street just by the lamp-post.
THOMAS RAWLEY ( police-constable M 269.) I took the prisoner on the 13th—I produce the shilling given me by Duckett.
MR. JOHN FIELD . These are both counterfeit, and both cast in one mould—this last one is very easily detected.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18430130-835]
835. GEORGE FOULCHER was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of Jan., 1 pair of boots, value 15 s., the goods of Edward Neale.
WILLIAM DUNFORD . On the 14th of Jan. the prisoner put up at my master's stable—I missed a pair of boots, an hour after he was gone, belonging to Edward Neale, my master—these are them—there was no one in the stable but the prisoner and me.
WILLIAM SMITH . The prisoner came and asked me to buy the ticket of these boots—I said I had no money, but he put the ticket into my waistcoat-pocket—three days after that I heard of the boots being stolen—this is the ticket.
JAMES CLIPSON . The prisoner lodged with me. On the 15th of Jan. he said he had an excellent pair of boots to sell—he brought them from the boot of his coach—these are them—I did not buy them.
ROBERT ADAMS ( police-constable E 109.) I took the prisoner, got the ticket from Smith, and these boots from the pawnbroker.
Prisoner's Defence. I brought a man from Norwood, and put him down at the corner of Westminster-bridge; I bought the boots of him for 4 s.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined Four Months.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, FEBRUARY 27 TH, 1843.
